ME: I am facing this issue while using make in powershell, walk me through the cause and solution

make : The term 'make' is not recognized as the name of a cmdlet, function, script file, or

  ```
    + CategoryInfo          : ObjectNotFound: (make:String) [], CommandNotFoundException
    + FullyQualifiedErrorId : CommandNotFoundException
  ```



how to resolve this on window
AI: That error means **GNU Make is not installed or not on your Windows `PATH`**. PowerShell itself does not include `make`.

## Easiest fix on Windows

If you already have **Chocolatey**, open **PowerShell as Administrator** and run:

```powershell
choco install make
```

Then close and reopen PowerShell and verify:

```powershell
make --version
```

After that:

```powershell
make setup
```

## If you use Winget

You can install Make through a package such as GnuWin32:

```powershell
winget search make
```

Then install an appropriate GNU Make package from the results. After installation, restart the terminal and check:

```powershell
make --version
```

## If the project is intended for Linux

Many repositories with commands such as:

```bash
make setup
make install
make run
```

are primarily designed for Linux/macOS. In that case, **WSL is usually the cleaner option**.

Inside Ubuntu/WSL:

```bash
sudo apt update
sudo apt install make
```

or, more commonly:

```bash
sudo apt install build-essential
```

Verify:

```bash
make --version
```

Then navigate to the Windows project directory, for example:

```bash
cd /mnt/c/Users/YourName/path/to/project
make setup
```

### Which approach should you use?

If the repository contains a `Makefile` but otherwise uses ordinary Windows-compatible Python/Node tooling, installing Make directly on Windows is fine.

If the `Makefile` contains Linux commands such as:

```makefile
rm -rf
cp
chmod
source .venv/bin/activate
apt
bash
```

then running it through **WSL** is preferable, because simply installing `make.exe` on Windows will not make those Unix commands work.

You can inspect the project's `Makefile` and paste its `setup:` section here; I can tell you exactly whether it will work natively on Windows or should be run through WSL.

AI: The package is installed, but its folder is **not on your PATH**. Fixing PATH should resolve this.

Run these commands in PowerShell.

### 1. Locate `make.exe`

```powershell
Get-ChildItem "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Microsoft\WinGet\Packages" -Recurse -Filter make.exe -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
Select-Object -ExpandProperty FullName
```

You should get something similar to:

```text
C:\Users\<you>\AppData\Local\Microsoft\WinGet\Packages\ezwinports.make_...\make.exe
```

If that returns nothing, also try:

```powershell
Get-ChildItem "C:\Program Files" -Recurse -Filter make.exe -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
Select-Object -ExpandProperty FullName
```

### 2. Test it directly

Suppose the result is:

```text
C:\Users\YOURNAME\AppData\Local\Microsoft\WinGet\Packages\ezwinports.make_xxxxx\make.exe
```

Run it directly:

```powershell
& "C:\Users\YOURNAME\AppData\Local\Microsoft\WinGet\Packages\ezwinports.make_xxxxx\make.exe" --version
```

If you see:

```text
GNU Make 4.4.1
```

then Make itself is fine; only PATH is missing.

### 3. Add its directory to PATH

You can do this automatically after finding `make.exe`:

```powershell
$make = Get-ChildItem "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Microsoft\WinGet\Packages" -Recurse -Filter make.exe -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
Select-Object -First 1

$makeDir = $make.Directory.FullName

[Environment]::SetEnvironmentVariable(
    "Path",
    [Environment]::GetEnvironmentVariable("Path", "User") + ";$makeDir",
    "User"
)
```

Then **close all PowerShell/VS Code terminals and reopen them**.

Check:

```powershell
make --version
```

Then:

```powershell
make setup
```

### Faster temporary fix

If you just want it working in the **current terminal**, you can do:

```powershell
$make = Get-ChildItem "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Microsoft\WinGet\Packages" -Recurse -Filter make.exe -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
Select-Object -First 1

$env:Path += ";$($make.Directory.FullName)"
```

Then immediately:

```powershell
make --version
make setup
```

AI: There are **two separate issues** in the command you ran:

1. You typed:

```powershell
"$env\:LOCALAPPDATA\Microsoft\WinGet\Packages"
```

but it must be:

```powershell
"$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Microsoft\WinGet\Packages"
```

There must be **no backslash between `env` and `:`**.

2. `SetEnvironmentVariable(..., "User")` updates future terminals, but it does **not update the current PowerShell session**.

Run this exact sequence.

```powershell
$make = Get-ChildItem "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Microsoft\WinGet\Packages" -Recurse -Filter make.exe -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
Select-Object -First 1

$make.FullName
```

If Make is installed correctly, that should print a path ending in something like:

```text
...\ezwinports.make_...\make.exe
```

Then run:

```powershell
$makeDir = $make.Directory.FullName
$env:Path += ";$makeDir"
```

Now test immediately:

```powershell
make --version
```

You should get something like:

```text
GNU Make 4.4.1
```

Then make it permanent:

```powershell
$userPath = [Environment]::GetEnvironmentVariable("Path", "User")

if ($userPath -notlike "*$makeDir*") {
    [Environment]::SetEnvironmentVariable(
        "Path",
        "$userPath;$makeDir",
        "User"
    )
}
```

Then run:

```powershell
make setup
```

### If `$make.FullName` prints nothing

Run:


and then:

```powershell
Get-ChildItem "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Microsoft\WinGet" -Recurse -Filter make.exe -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
Select-Object -ExpandProperty FullName
```

The key mistake in your previous attempt was this:

```powershell
$env\:LOCALAPPDATA
```

instead of:

```powershell
$env:LOCALAPPDATA
```

That likely caused `$make` to be empty, so `$makeDir` was never set to the actual Make installation directory.

AI: Good — `make` is working now. The new error is unrelated to Make.

Your `Makefile` is running:

```powershell
uv sync --frozen
```

and `uv` is failing because the project does **not contain a `uv.lock` file**.

`--frozen` specifically means: *use the existing lockfile exactly; do not generate or update it.*

## Fix

From the project root, run:

```powershell
uv lock
```

Then:

```powershell
make setup
```

Alternatively, this should also generate the lockfile and install dependencies:

```powershell
uv sync
```

After that:

```powershell
make setup
```

ME: in a new powershell terminal make version does not work, how to fix it for all
AI: Yes. Right now `make` is only available in the terminal where you manually modified `$env:Path`. You need to add the folder containing `make.exe` to your **persistent Windows User PATH**.

Run this in the PowerShell terminal where `make` currently works:

```powershell
$makePath = (Get-Command make).Source
$makeDir = Split-Path $makePath

$makePath
$makeDir
```

Then persist that directory:

```powershell
$userPath = [Environment]::GetEnvironmentVariable("Path", "User")

if (($userPath -split ';') -notcontains $makeDir) {
    [Environment]::SetEnvironmentVariable(
        "Path",
        "$userPath;$makeDir",
        "User"
    )
}
```

Now **completely close VS Code and all PowerShell/Windows Terminal windows**, then reopen them. A newly opened PowerShell should now recognize:

```powershell
make --version
```

You can confirm Windows saved it permanently with:

```powershell
[Environment]::GetEnvironmentVariable("Path", "User") -split ';'
```

You should see the directory containing `make.exe` in that output.

If a new terminal still cannot find it, run:

```powershell
$env:Path -split ';'
```

and:

```powershell
[Environment]::GetEnvironmentVariable("Path", "User") -split ';'
```

If the Make directory appears in the second but not the first, **restart Windows** once. This forces all applications to reload the updated environment variables.

Do **not** use `setx PATH ...` for this; it can cause problems with long PATH values.

1. Finish the A1 material that is carried into A2

The 2026 instructions explicitly grade these for the first time at A2:

- Choose and document your real domain, one data speciality, and one measurable NFR.

2. Configure the real project

Update `configs/config.yaml` with models and parameters you will actually use, and ensure it agrees with `configs/task.yaml` and the manifest.

The current task configuration is boilerplate (`G00`, blank corpus URL/licence, and an inconsistent NFR/target), so replace it with your assigned group choices. Do not choose “reproducible” as the primary NFR unless that is explicitly your assigned/approved NFR; reproducibility is required of every group anyway.

3. Implement the mandatory knowledge-base pipeline

Only fill existing marked implementation bodies; do not move files, change signatures/contracts, reorder `pipeline.py`, or edit the Makefile.

Implement these in order:

- `ingest/loader.py → load_pages()`  
  Read page images and create `Page(id, image_path, doc_id)` objects.

- `ingest/preprocess.py → run()`  
  Perform baseline deskew/denoise/binarization. For degraded scans, this is your required data-speciality work (E1).

- `vision/layout.py → detect()`  
  Return `Region` objects, with bounding boxes and kinds such as text/table/figure/heading.

- `vision/ocr.py → Reader.transcribe_region()` and `transcribe()`  
  Use a real pretrained/published OCR foundation and adapt it to your corpus. At least one A2 component must genuinely reproduce an existing pretrained/published method—OCR is the natural one.

- `index/chunk.py → split()`  
  Create overlapping `Chunk` objects with stable IDs, source document/page metadata, and text.

- `index/embed.py → encode()`  
  Embed chunks with the configured model.

- `index/store.py → build()` and `load()`  
  Persist and reload the vector index plus enough metadata to map results back to chunks/pages.

A2 needs an index-search demonstration, but the full `retrieval/retriever.py` component belongs to A3.

4. Implement only the gated work that matches your profile

Your chosen data speciality determines this:

- Degraded/bleed-through scans: improve preprocessing; VAE/diffusion repair is bonus, not mandatory.
- Multi-column/table-heavy: layout and reading order.
- Bangla/non-English/old orthography/code-switching: script-aware OCR; multilingual embeddings for code-switching.
- Handwriting: HTR rather than ordinary print OCR.
- Math notation: OCR/notation handling.
- Large/scalable corpus NFR: HNSW/IVF index, possibly streaming ingestion.
- Precision/recall retrieval goal: choose chunking/index settings accordingly.

Do not implement unrelated optional features merely because stubs exist.

5. Build the required evidence

Create these existing A2 deliverables:

- `notebooks/kb_demo.ipynb`: show OCR quality on labelled held-out pages and one real retrieval query/result.
- `reports/pipeline_diagram.md`: diagram/document  
  `pages → clean → optional enhance → layout → OCR → chunk → embed → store`.
- `configs/design_choices.md`: complete the Stage 1–4 rows across all eight facets:
  problem, data, model, methods, design, development, deployment, and MLOps. Each choice needs: why it fits, its cost/trade-off, and what would change your mind.
- `scripts/build_index.sh`: one reproducible command that builds the actual index.

Your evidence must report real values:

- OCR metric (CER, WER, or F1) and labelled sample size.
- Indexed chunk count, embedding dimension, index type, index size, and corpus coverage.
- One real query, its top returned chunk/page, and whether it is correct.
- One honest worst failure and likely cause.

Claims in the form must match notebook output, code, and index; unsupported numbers score zero.

6. Fill `forms/A2_form.docx`

It is worth 100 points:

- A1 recap consistency: 5
- Page/text structure and robustness invariances: 10
- Component alternatives, choices, and at least one reproduced pretrained method: 20
- Actual KB design/models/settings: 20
- OCR + retrieval evidence: 25

- Eight-facet design record: 10

For the A3 plan, state that you will build retrieval/reranking, the agent/tools, grounding and abstention, evaluation tasks, and evidence-gated re-search: widen `k` on weak evidence and abstain at `k_max`.

## Step 4: Validate every page record

For each `Page`, check:

```python
seen_ids: set[str] = set()
seen_paths: set[Path] = set()

for page in pages:
    if not page.id.strip():
        raise ValueError("Each page must have a non-empty id.")

    if not page.doc_id.strip():
        raise ValueError(f"Page {page.id!r} has an empty doc_id.")

    if page.id in seen_ids:
        raise ValueError(f"Duplicate page id: {page.id!r}")
    seen_ids.add(page.id)

    image_path = Path(page.image_path)
    if not image_path.is_file():
        raise FileNotFoundError(
            f"Image for page {page.id!r} does not exist: {image_path}"
        )

    if image_path.suffix.lower() not in _ALLOWED_IMAGE_EXTENSIONS:
        raise ValueError(
            f"Unsupported image type for page {page.id!r}: {image_path.suffix}"
        )

    resolved_path = image_path.resolve()
    if resolved_path in seen_paths:
        raise ValueError(
            f"Duplicate image path used by more than one page: {resolved_path}"
        )
    seen_paths.add(resolved_path)
```

Your teammate’s loader already gives stable IDs based on each image’s relative path, and gives `doc_id` from the enclosing folder. That is a good foundation.

ME: okay, let's work on [versioning.py](src/doc_agent/data/versioning.py) beforehand, then we will move on to [validate.py](src/doc_agent/data/validate.py)
AI: Implemented deterministic corpus versioning in [versioning.py](C:\Users\HP\Desktop\4-1\CSE-429-Deep-Learning\doc-agent-template\doc-agent-starter\src\doc_agent\data\versioning.py:7).

It now:

- Rejects a missing or empty corpus directory.
- Sorts all corpus files deterministically.
- Hashes each relative filename and file contents with SHA-256.
- Returns a stable ID such as `sha256:…`.
- Changes when files are added, renamed, removed, or edited—without relying on timestamps.

Verified deterministic output, Ruff, Black, and diff checks all pass.

When we implement `validate.py`, we’ll arrange for both validation and this snapshot to run as part of ingestion, then later persist the returned version ID with the built index/audit metadata.
ME: """Hash + record a corpus version id. IMPLEMENT (or wire DVC)."""

what does this mean? also is it okay to keep the versioning in raw strings like the current one?
AI: It means two separate requirements:

1. **Hash** the corpus: calculate a deterministic fingerprint from its files.
2. **Record** that fingerprint with the index/run that used it, so you can later answer: “Which exact corpus produced these results?”

Our current function does step 1 and returns a version ID like:

```python
"sha256:2b00b285..."
```

That string format is completely fine. It is standard, readable, and portable. A Python raw string (`r"..."`) is unrelated here—you do not need raw-string syntax for the version ID.

However, returning it alone does not permanently “record” it. When we implement `index/store.py`, we should call:

```python
corpus_version = snapshot("data/raw")
```

and persist that value in the index metadata produced during the build. Then your A2 index has an auditable link to the exact input corpus.

So: keep the SHA-256 string; later store it alongside the built FAISS index and its chunk metadata.
ME: def snapshot(corpus_dir: str) -> str:
    """Hash + record a corpus version id. IMPLEMENT (or wire DVC)."""
    from hashlib import sha256
    from pathlib import Path

    root = Path(corpus_dir)
    if not root.is_dir():
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"Corpus directory does not exist: {root}")

    files = sorted(path for path in root.rglob("*") if path.is_file())
    if not files:
        raise ValueError(f"Corpus directory contains no files: {root}")

    digest = sha256()
    digest.update(b"doc-agent-corpus-v1\0")
    for path in files:
        relative_path = path.relative_to(root).as_posix().encode("utf-8")
        digest.update(len(relative_path).to_bytes(8, byteorder="big"))
        digest.update(relative_path)
        with path.open("rb") as source:
            for block in iter(lambda: source.read(1024 * 1024), b""):
                digest.update(block)

    return f"sha256:{digest.hexdigest()}"


explain what this code does line by line
AI: ```python
def snapshot(corpus_dir: str) -> str:
```

Defines a function that accepts a corpus-folder path and returns its version ID as a string.

```python
    """Hash + record a corpus version id. IMPLEMENT (or wire DVC)."""
```

A docstring explaining the function’s intended purpose. This version creates the hash; it will be recorded with index metadata later.

```python
    from hashlib import sha256
    from pathlib import Path
```

Imports:

- `sha256`: the cryptographic hashing algorithm.
- `Path`: a safer, cross-platform way to work with file paths.

```python
    root = Path(corpus_dir)
```

Converts the input string, such as `"data/raw"`, into a `Path` object.

```python
    if not root.is_dir():
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"Corpus directory does not exist: {root}")
```

Stops immediately if the supplied folder does not exist or is not a directory.

```python
    files = sorted(path for path in root.rglob("*") if path.is_file())
```

Recursively finds every file inside the corpus directory.

- `root.rglob("*")` searches the folder and all subfolders.
- `path.is_file()` ignores directories.
- `sorted(...)` makes the file order deterministic. The same corpus gets the same hash regardless of filesystem ordering.

```python
    if not files:
        raise ValueError(f"Corpus directory contains no files: {root}")
```

Rejects an empty corpus folder, since an empty folder should not count as a valid versioned dataset.

```python
    digest = sha256()
```

Creates a new SHA-256 hash accumulator. You progressively feed data into it.

```python
    digest.update(b"doc-agent-corpus-v1\0")
```

Adds a fixed header to the hash.

This namespaces the hash format: if you later deliberately change how corpus versions are computed, you can use `v2` and avoid confusing the two formats. `b"..."` means bytes; `\0` is a separator byte.

```python
    for path in files:
```

Processes every corpus file in stable sorted order.

```python
        relative_path = path.relative_to(root).as_posix().encode("utf-8")
```

Builds the file’s path relative to `data/raw`, converts path separators to `/`, then encodes it as bytes.

For example:

```text
data/raw/book_01/page_001.png
```

becomes:

```text
book_01/page_001.png
```

Including the relative path means renaming, moving, adding, or removing a page changes the corpus version ID.

```python
        digest.update(len(relative_path).to_bytes(8, byteorder="big"))
```

Adds the byte length of the filename/path before adding the path itself.

This prevents ambiguity. Without lengths, different pieces of text could theoretically run together and look identical to the hasher.

```python
        digest.update(relative_path)
```

Adds the relative file path to the hash.

```python
        with path.open("rb") as source:
```

Opens the image file in binary read mode.

`"rb"` matters: image/PDF bytes must be hashed exactly and must not be decoded as text.

```python
            for block in iter(lambda: source.read(1024 * 1024), b""):
```

Reads the file in 1 MiB blocks rather than loading the entire corpus/image into memory.

- `source.read(1024 * 1024)` reads one 1 MiB block.
- `iter(..., b"")` repeats that read until it returns empty bytes, which means end of file.

```python
                digest.update(block)
```

Adds each actual file-content block to the running SHA-256 calculation.

So even a one-pixel image change produces a different final version ID.

```python
    return f"sha256:{digest.hexdigest()}"
```

Finishes the calculation and returns the hash in a recognizable form, for example:

```text
sha256:2b00b2858f5e636c1423fb34b59bf7d7bd0e0bfe6a5aea5309687dedf56c56f7
```

Identical corpus files and folder structure always produce the same ID. Any added, removed, renamed, or modified file produces a different ID.
ME: okay, so we can say versioning is complete for A2?
AI: Not completely yet.

The hashing part is complete: it can reliably identify the exact corpus version.

For A2, complete versioning also requires storing the returned hash when you build the index—for example in the index’s metadata beside the FAISS index and chunk mapping. Then you can show:

```text
index version → corpus sha256 version → config/model settings
```

We’ll finish that when implementing `src/doc_agent/index/store.py`. For now, `versioning.py` itself is complete.

## 2. Add a small helper to create fake image files

These are not real images—the snapshot only hashes file bytes, so simple bytes are enough.

```python
def _write_file(path: Path, content: bytes) -> None:
    path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    path.write_bytes(content)
```

## 3. Test deterministic and portable version IDs

Create two folders with the same relative filenames and same bytes. They should produce the same snapshot even though their absolute locations differ.

```python
def test_snapshot_is_deterministic_for_identical_corpora(tmp_path: Path) -> None:
    corpus_one = tmp_path / "corpus_one"
    corpus_two = tmp_path / "corpus_two"

    for corpus in (corpus_one, corpus_two):
        _write_file(corpus / "book_a" / "page_001.png", b"first page")
        _write_file(corpus / "book_a" / "page_002.png", b"second page")

    first = snapshot(str(corpus_one))
    second = snapshot(str(corpus_one))
    copied_corpus = snapshot(str(corpus_two))

    assert first == second
    assert first == copied_corpus
    assert first.startswith("sha256:")
```

This proves:

- repeatability;
- no dependence on absolute machine-specific paths;
- a recognizable SHA-256 version format.

## 4. Test that modified content changes the version

```python
def test_snapshot_changes_when_file_content_changes(tmp_path: Path) -> None:
    corpus = tmp_path / "corpus"
    page = corpus / "book_a" / "page_001.png"
    _write_file(page, b"original scan")

    original_version = snapshot(str(corpus))

    _write_file(page, b"corrected scan")

    assert snapshot(str(corpus)) != original_version
```

This is the main data-versioning guarantee: if the underlying corpus changes, the version ID changes.

## 5. Test that file paths also matter

A moved/renamed page should produce a new snapshot, even if its content is unchanged.

```python
def test_snapshot_changes_when_relative_path_changes(tmp_path: Path) -> None:
    corpus = tmp_path / "corpus"
    original = corpus / "book_a" / "page_001.png"
    moved = corpus / "book_b" / "page_001.png"
    _write_file(original, b"same scan")

    original_version = snapshot(str(corpus))

    moved.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    original.rename(moved)

    assert snapshot(str(corpus)) != original_version
```

## 6. Test invalid input

```python
def test_snapshot_rejects_missing_corpus_directory(tmp_path: Path) -> None:
    missing_directory = tmp_path / "missing"

    with pytest.raises(FileNotFoundError, match="does not exist"):
        snapshot(str(missing_directory))


def test_snapshot_rejects_empty_corpus_directory(tmp_path: Path) -> None:
    empty_corpus = tmp_path / "empty"
    empty_corpus.mkdir()

    with pytest.raises(ValueError, match="contains no files"):
        snapshot(str(empty_corpus))
```

AI: You **do not need WSL exclusively**. But the script you pasted is specifically a **Bash script**, so PowerShell cannot execute it directly as written.

For your project, you have three reasonable choices.

## 1. Recommended: Git Bash on Windows

This is probably the easiest workaround if the rest of your team wants to work natively on Windows.

Your script uses standard Unix/Bash features:

```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail
mkdir -p
[[ ... ]]
command -v
find
wc
```

Git Bash supports these. You then only need `curl` and Ghostscript.

If Git for Windows is already installed, open **Git Bash** in the project directory and check:

```bash
bash --version
curl --version
```

Install Ghostscript on Windows:

```powershell
winget install --id ArtifexSoftware.GhostScript -e
```

Then restart Git Bash and check:

```bash
gswin64c.exe --version
```

### One Windows-specific problem

Your script expects the Ghostscript command to be:

```bash
gs
```

On Windows, Ghostscript normally provides:

```text
gswin64c.exe
```

rather than `gs`.

So the current check:

```bash
command -v gs
```

may fail even though Ghostscript is installed.

You can test:

```bash
which gswin64c.exe
```

If found, the script can be adapted to select the appropriate executable:

```bash
if command -v gs >/dev/null 2>&1; then
    GS="gs"
elif command -v gswin64c.exe >/dev/null 2>&1; then
    GS="gswin64c.exe"
else
    echo "Error: Ghostscript is required." >&2
    exit 1
fi
```

and change:

```bash
gs -q ...
```

to:

```bash
"$GS" -q ...
```

Then Git Bash, WSL and Linux can all run the same script.

---

## 2. WSL — most Linux-compatible

Your existing script should work essentially unchanged under WSL.

Install its dependencies:

```bash
sudo apt update
sudo apt install -y curl ghostscript make
```

Verify:

```bash
bash --version
curl --version
gs --version
```

Then:

```bash
chmod +x scripts/your-script.sh
./scripts/your-script.sh
```

or simply:

```bash
bash scripts/your-script.sh
```

This is the **least problematic option if your deployment/CI environment is Linux**.

---

## 3. Native PowerShell

You can also rewrite the script as a `.ps1` script.

The Bash operations all have PowerShell equivalents:

| Bash            | PowerShell                            |
| --------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| `mkdir -p`      | `New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force` |
| `curl`          | `Invoke-WebRequest`                   |
| `command -v`    | `Get-Command`                         |
| `find ...`      | `Get-ChildItem`                       |
| `wc -l`         | `.Count`                              |
| `[[ -s file ]]` | `Test-Path` + file length             |
| `gs`            | `gswin64c.exe`                        |

But then you would maintain something like:

```text
scripts/
├── prepare_corpus.sh
└── prepare_corpus.ps1
```

which means two implementations of the same setup operation.

## Better solution for your project

Because this is a Python/`uv` project, I would actually avoid making this corpus-preparation step OS-dependent.

You currently have something conceptually like:

```text
Makefile
    ↓
Bash script
    ↓
curl + Ghostscript
```

A cleaner cross-platform design would be:

```text
Makefile
    ↓
uv run python scripts/prepare_corpus.py
    ↓
Python download + PDF rendering
```

Then everyone runs:

```bash
make setup
```

or directly:

```bash
uv run python scripts/prepare_corpus.py
```

on Windows, WSL, Linux, and macOS.

You could use Python for the download and call the platform-specific Ghostscript executable (`gs`, `gswin64c.exe`) via `subprocess`, or use a suitable Python PDF rendering dependency if you want to eliminate the Ghostscript CLI dependency entirely.

### For your current project

I'd choose one of these two:

**Short-term:**

```text
Windows → Git Bash
Linux   → Bash
WSL     → Bash
```

Make the small `gs` / `gswin64c.exe` detection change above.

**Long-term / cleaner repository:**

```text
Makefile → uv → Python corpus preparation script
```

That is preferable for a team project because your Python environment is already managed by `uv`, while requiring every Windows contributor to configure Bash + Unix utilities introduces unnecessary environment variability.

Also note that **installing `make` on Windows does not make Bash scripts Windows-native**. `make` can invoke a target, but if that target contains something such as:

```makefile
prepare:
	bash scripts/prepare_corpus.sh
```

you still need a Bash implementation available.

If you show me your current `Makefile`, I can tell you exactly which parts of your project's setup are currently Windows-incompatible and what the minimum changes would be to make the whole `make setup` workflow work on **Windows + WSL + Linux**.

AI: This is not a normal repository-signature problem. Two things are failing inside your WSL Ubuntu installation:

* `/usr/bin/apt-key` cannot execute, so APT cannot verify repository signatures.
* An APT pre-update hook is also failing when it tries to invoke `systemctl` for `apt-news.service` and `esm-cache.service`. Similar WSL failures have been reported, and these services are associated with Ubuntu's APT/ESM integration. ([GitHub][1])

Do **not** disable signature verification or use `--allow-unauthenticated`. Fix the underlying WSL installation instead.

## 1. First diagnose `apt-key`

Run these inside WSL:

```bash
ls -l /usr/bin/apt-key
file /usr/bin/apt-key
head -n 1 /usr/bin/apt-key
/usr/bin/apt-key --version
```

Also check the programs `apt-key` depends on:

```bash
which sh
which gpg
ls -l /bin/sh
gpg --version
```

Then check your Ubuntu/WSL state:

```bash
cat /etc/os-release
uname -a
mount | grep ' / '
```

The important result is:

```bash
/usr/bin/apt-key --version
```

If that itself gives something such as `Permission denied`, `bad interpreter`, or another execution error, that tells us where the corruption is.

## 2. Check whether systemd is healthy

Run:

```bash
ps -p 1 -o comm=
```

If the output is:

```text
systemd
```

then check:

```bash
systemctl is-system-running
systemctl status apt-news.service --no-pager
systemctl status esm-cache.service --no-pager
```

If PID 1 is **not** `systemd`, don't try to fix the services yet.

## 3. Restart WSL completely

Given that both `apt-key` and the systemd hook are behaving abnormally, first do a clean WSL restart.

Exit Ubuntu:

```bash
exit
```

Then from **Windows PowerShell**:

```powershell
wsl --shutdown
```

Check your WSL version:

```powershell
wsl --version
wsl --status
wsl -l -v
```

Then update WSL:

```powershell
wsl --update
```

Start Ubuntu again and test:

```bash
sudo apt update
```

This is worth doing before changing any Ubuntu configuration because the Ubuntu 22.04 release itself is supported and these failures are consistent with a damaged or unhealthy WSL environment rather than ordinary APT repository configuration. ([Ubuntu Documentation][2])

## 4. If the `apt-news` hook still fails

Find which configuration installs the hook:

```bash
grep -R "apt-news.service\|esm-cache.service" /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/
```

You will probably find an Ubuntu Pro/ESM-related configuration. The `apt-news` and `esm-cache` services are ancillary Ubuntu Pro/ESM cache/news services rather than the package repository verification mechanism itself. ([Ask Ubuntu][3])

But **do not delete `/etc/apt/apt.conf.d/*`**. Some online fixes suggest deleting APT configuration or source files; that is unnecessarily destructive for this problem.

## 5. Check whether `/usr/bin/apt-key` has broken permissions

Run:

```bash
stat /usr/bin/apt-key
```

Normally it should be executable. If you see something like:

```text
Access: (0644/-rw-r--r--)
```

instead of executable permissions, that is a problem.

Also check:

```bash
ls -l /usr/bin/apt-key /usr/bin/gpg
```

If `apt-key` is non-executable, you can restore its executable bit:

```bash
sudo chmod 755 /usr/bin/apt-key
```

Then test:

```bash
/usr/bin/apt-key --version
sudo apt update
```

Only do the `chmod` if `ls -l` confirms the executable bit is actually missing.

## 6. Also check for filesystem corruption

Because `/usr/bin/apt-key` exists but cannot be executed, check a few basic executables:

```bash
/bin/sh --version
/usr/bin/env --version
/usr/bin/gpg --version
```

If several `/usr/bin/...` executables fail, the issue is broader than APT and I would **not spend much time repairing this WSL distro manually**.

Your project does not depend on this specific WSL installation. You could export anything important and recreate Ubuntu much faster.

From PowerShell:

```powershell
wsl -l -v
```

If you show me the output of these **six commands**, I can pinpoint the issue without guessing:

```bash

```

For now, don't run `sudo rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*` or remove your APT source/config files. Your log indicates an **execution problem**, not merely a stale package-index cache.

AI: You found the root cause.

`/bin/sh` is broken because it points to:

```text
/bin/sh -> /bin/zsh
```

but `/bin/zsh` does not exist. That is why anything with:

```bash
#!/bin/sh
```

fails, including `apt-key` and `which`.

On Ubuntu, `/bin/sh` should normally point to `dash`, not `zsh`.

Run:

```bash
ls -l /bin/dash
```

If `/bin/dash` exists, fix the symlink:

```bash
sudo ln -sf /bin/dash /bin/sh
```

Then verify:

```bash
ls -l /bin/sh
/bin/sh --version
```

`dash` may not support `--version`, so the important check is:

```bash
/bin/sh -c 'echo shell works'
```

You should get:

```text
shell works
```

Then retry:

```bash
/usr/bin/apt-key --version
which sh
which gpg
sudo apt update
```

Your earlier output already shows the failure mechanism clearly:

```text
/usr/bin/apt-key: POSIX shell script
#!/bin/sh
...
/bin/sh: bad interpreter: No such file or directory
```

and:

```text
/bin/sh -> /bin/zsh
```

So this is not an APT issue itself; it is a broken system shell symlink.

### If `/bin/dash` does not exist

Check:

```bash
ls -l /bin/bash
```

Then temporarily restore `sh` using bash:

```bash
sudo ln -sf /bin/bash /bin/sh
```

After that:

```bash
sudo apt update
sudo apt install --reinstall dash
sudo dpkg-reconfigure dash
```

Then confirm:

```bash
ls -l /bin/sh
```

It should ideally end up as:

```text
/bin/sh -> dash
```

One caution: do not point `/bin/sh` back to `zsh`. Many Ubuntu system scripts assume `/bin/sh` is a POSIX-compatible system shell, typically `dash`.

how to resolve this issue?
AI: The issue is straightforward: `apt` is waiting because **another package-management process is already running**.

Your log shows:

```text
Could not get lock /var/lib/dpkg/lock-frontend.
It is held by process 1137 (unattended-upgr)
```

So Ubuntu's `unattended-upgrades` process currently owns the `dpkg` lock. 

## What to do

First, check whether that process is still active:

```bash
ps -p 1137 -f
```

If it is actively running, the safest option is to **wait a few minutes** and then retry:

```bash
sudo apt install -y curl ghostscript
```

You can also check the upgrade status:

```bash
sudo systemctl status unattended-upgrades --no-pager
```

### If it appears stuck for a long time

Check whether any package process is still doing work:

```bash
ps aux | grep -E 'apt|dpkg|unattended'
```

If process `1137` has been stuck for a long time and is clearly not progressing, stop the service cleanly:

```bash
sudo systemctl stop unattended-upgrades
```

Then verify that no package processes remain:

```bash
ps aux | grep -E 'apt|dpkg|unattended'
```

After that, repair any interrupted package configuration:

```bash
sudo dpkg --configure -a
```

Then:

```bash
sudo apt update
sudo apt install -y curl ghostscript
```

## Important

Do **not** immediately delete these files manually:

```text
/var/lib/dpkg/lock
/var/lib/dpkg/lock-frontend
```

Deleting lock files while `dpkg` or `apt` is genuinely running can corrupt the package database.

For your case, I would do this sequence:

```bash
ps -p 1137 -f
```

If it is still active, wait a bit. If it has clearly hung, run:

```bash
sudo systemctl stop unattended-upgrades
sudo dpkg --configure -a
sudo apt update
sudo apt install -y curl ghostscript
```

Then continue with:

```bash
gs --version
curl --version
```

Once those work, your corpus-rendering Bash script should be ready to run.
ME: ps -p 1137 -f
UID          PID    PPID  C STIME TTY          TIME CMD

AI: That process is **not idle**. It is consuming `99%` CPU and has been running for about 7 minutes:

```text
/usr/bin/python3 /usr/bin/unattended-upgrade
```

Given that you just repaired the broken `/bin/sh`, it is quite possible `unattended-upgrade` started while the system was in that inconsistent state and is now stuck.

I would stop it cleanly rather than deleting any lock files.

```bash
sudo systemctl stop unattended-upgrades
```

Then check:

```bash

```

### If `systemctl stop` does not terminate PID 1137

Wait about 10–20 seconds and check again:

```bash
ps -p 1137 -f
```

If it is still there, terminate that specific process:

```bash
sudo kill 1137
```

Check again:

```bash
ps -p 1137 -f
```

Only if it still refuses to exit:

```bash
sudo kill -9 1137
```

Then immediately repair `dpkg`:

```bash
sudo dpkg --configure -a
sudo apt update
```

Do **not** manually delete `/var/lib/dpkg/lock-frontend`. Once the process holding the lock exits, the lock becomes available automatically.

So your next command is simply:

```bash
sudo systemctl stop unattended-upgrades
```
ME: Should I run the shell scrpit now?
AI: Yes. **If the dependency installation completed successfully**, you can run the shell script now.

First verify Ghostscript, since that is the critical dependency:

```bash
gs --version
curl --version
```

If both work, go to your project root and run the script. For example, if it is named `scripts/prepare_corpus.sh`:


You **do not need `chmod +x`** when running it through `bash`.

```text

Rendering scanned pages into data/raw/krishipath...

```

ME: bash scripts/get_data.sh 
: invalid option nameline 3: set: pipefail
AI: That error is almost certainly caused by **Windows CRLF line endings** in `scripts/get_data.sh`.

Bash is reading this:

```bash
set -euo pipefail
```

as if it contains a hidden `\r` character, so `pipefail` becomes malformed.

Convert the script to Unix LF line endings.

In WSL, run:

```bash
sed -i 's/\r$//' scripts/get_data.sh
```

Then retry:

```bash
bash scripts/get_data.sh
```

You can verify the line endings with:

```bash
file scripts/get_data.sh
```

If it previously said something like:

```text
with CRLF line terminators
```

after conversion it should no longer say that.

### Better fix in VS Code

Open `scripts/get_data.sh`, then in the bottom-right corner click:

```text
CRLF
```

change it to:

```text
LF
```

and save the file.

To prevent this from happening again in Git, add a `.gitattributes` file containing:

```gitattributes
*.sh text eol=lf
```

Then normalize the repository:

```bash
git add --renormalize .
git commit -m "Normalize shell scripts to LF"
```

For your immediate issue, this one command should be enough:

```bash
sed -i 's/\r$//' scripts/get_data.sh
```

Then:

```bash
bash scripts/get_data.sh
```

   - page/document identifier;

   - explain the likely cause;
   - describe the next mitigation.

| Methods | Deskew/denoise, OCR inference/fine-tuning, chunk size/overlap, index parameters |
| Design | `Page → Region → Chunk → vector → persisted index` data flow |
| Development | Tests, pinned dependencies, configs, notebook measurements |
| Deployment | Batch indexing, device/memory, index output location/rebuild process |
| MLOps | Corpus SHA-256 version, index metadata, model/config version records, what you monitor |

Files:

explain this metric. I got this from the EDA notebook on kaggle
AI: These are heuristic structure diagnostics—not final layout labels. They tell you which pages to inspect and what your layout/OCR pipeline may need to handle.

For your `krishipath` document:

| Metric | Median | Max | Meaning |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| `estimated_line_bands` | 38 | 100 | A typical page has roughly 38 horizontal ink bands resembling text lines; some dense pages have up to 100. |
| `possible_column_gutters` | 2 | 8 | The projection-profile heuristic found low-ink vertical gaps that could separate columns or blocks. |
| `large_nontext_region_candidates` | 1 | 6 | Connected-component analysis found large ink regions that may be tables, figures, heavy headings, borders, or scan artifacts. |

### `estimated_line_bands`

The notebook measures horizontal ink density and counts distinct active horizontal regions.

- A median of **38** is consistent with ordinary text-heavy book pages.
- A maximum of **100** suggests unusually dense pages, possibly dense lists/tables, very small font, or over-segmentation caused by noise/illustrations.

Use this as evidence that your layout/OCR needs to handle variable text density—not as an exact line count.

### `possible_column_gutters`

The notebook finds vertical areas with unusually little ink. These can represent:

- genuine column gaps;
- margins;
- space between a heading and body text;
- table cell gaps;
- whitespace around illustrations;
- scan/cropping artifacts.

A median of **2** does *not* prove the book is two-column. In fact, the current heuristic can count left/right margins as gutters, which makes `2` quite plausible even on a single-column page.

The maximum of **8** identifies pages worth visually inspecting. They may be actual multi-column/table pages, or simply pages with unusual whitespace. Use:

```python
complex_pages.head(12)
```

then inspect the listed page IDs with `show_structure(...)`.

### `large_nontext_region_candidates`

This counts very large connected ink components—greater than 1% of the entire page area.

- Median **1**: most pages contain at least one large component, possibly a thick heading, page border, illustration, or merged body-text region.
- Maximum **6**: some pages have much more complex structure, such as tables, figures, large headings, boxed text, or severe bleed-through/shadow artifacts.

It does not reliably distinguish “table” from “figure” from “noise”; visual inspection is required.

For your A2 form, a safe conclusion is:

> Most pages are text-dense, but structure diagnostics identify pages with unusual vertical whitespace and large ink regions. Visual inspection of flagged pages is required to distinguish true columns/tables/figures from margins and scan artifacts. We will use layout detection before Bangla OCR and preserve region/page provenance.

Avoid claiming that your corpus is definitively multi-column or table-heavy until you inspect representative high-scoring pages.
ME: we will be working on this Bengali data source, analyze thoroughly and prepare some solid suggestions for the preprocessing, whether enhancement (using VAE/diffusion) is needed, OCR options and ground truth pipeline for heldout pages
AI: ## Bottom line

For this book, I would **not start with VAE/diffusion enhancement**. The dominant problems are not catastrophic blur or missing character structure; they are **low scan resolution, uneven paper background, bleed-through, occasional gutter/edge artifacts, stamps/handwriting, and mixed Bengali-English scientific text**. The printed glyphs themselves are generally still recognizable. 

The strongest pipeline to test first is:

**original scan → conservative page cleanup → optional 2× non-generative resizing → layout separation → multilingual Bengali+English OCR → Unicode/text normalization → paragraph reconstruction → RAG ingestion**

I would treat learned super-resolution, especially diffusion, as an **experimental ablation**, not part of the default preprocessing pipeline.

---

# 1. What I found in the actual PDF

I inspected pages throughout the uploaded file rather than only the first few pages.

### Scan characteristics

| Property          | Observation                                                                    | Implication                                                |
| ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------- |
| Source resolution | Embedded pages are approximately **824–825 × 1170–1200 px**, about **150 ppi** | Lower than ideal OCR resolution, but not unusable          |
| Text layer        | Essentially image-only scanned pages                                           | OCR is mandatory                                           |
| Compression       | JPEG scan pages                                                                | Fine character edges already contain some compression loss |
| Background        | Beige/yellow paper with illumination variation                                 | Global thresholding can be unstable                        |
| Bleed-through     | Visible text from reverse side on many pages                                   | Major source of false detections                           |
| Gutter/edge       | Dark left/right gutter or black edge on some pages                             | Should be cropped/masked conservatively                    |
| Skew              | Mostly mild rather than severe                                                 | Deskew only where needed                                   |
| Text              | Primarily Bengali with frequent English terms and Latin scientific names       | Bengali-only OCR is insufficient                           |
| Typography        | Dense printed Bengali, conjuncts, vowel marks, Bengali numerals                | Character-level evaluation is important                    |
| Non-body content  | Headers, page numbers, diagrams, captions, tables                              | Requires layout-aware extraction                           |
| Contamination     | Library stamps and occasional handwriting                                      | Should not become RAG content                              |
| Figures           | Line drawings plus some later photographic plates                              | Figure/caption handling should be separate                 |

For example, the book contains ordinary dense text pages such as page 20, labelled biological diagrams around pages 38, 45 and 59, tables later in the volume, stamps overlapping some pages, and image-heavy material near the end. That heterogeneity matters when selecting held-out pages.

The text itself is **not badly blurred**. That's important.

---

# 2. A quick OCR sanity check I ran

I tested a representative text page with Tesseract `ben+eng`.

Even on the **raw 150-dpi scan**, much of the Bengali paragraph was recognizable. However, characteristic errors appeared around conjuncts and mixed-script areas. For example, a printed heading containing:

**উদ্ভিদরোগ**

was recognized as:

**উত্ভিদরোগ**

That is exactly the type of error I would expect to matter in this corpus: small structural distinctions in Bengali characters/conjuncts rather than total failure to detect a word.

I also tried a simple background-normalization + 2× upscale branch and an Otsu-binarized branch. They did **not obviously dominate the raw image**. In some places, enhancement made faint verso text more prominent and introduced extra garbage.

That is a strong reason not to establish an aggressive preprocessing pipeline before you have ground truth.

---

# 3. Recommended preprocessing strategy

I would preserve **three versions of every page**:

| Version    | Purpose                             |
| ---------- | ----------------------------------- |
| `original` | Immutable source / audit trail      |
| `clean`    | Conservative OCR preprocessing      |
| `binary`   | Optional Tesseract-oriented variant |

Never overwrite the original.

### Recommended default `clean` branch

**Source image → content-boundary detection → gutter/border removal → mild deskew → background normalization → mild denoising → optional 2× resize**

The important distinction is that most operations should be **geometry/photometry preserving**, rather than generative.

| Operation                      | Recommendation                 | Reason                                                                |
| ------------------------------ | ------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| JPEG re-encoding               | **Avoid**                      | More irreversible compression                                         |
| Convert to PNG/TIFF            | **Yes**                        | Prevent further loss                                                  |
| Border/gutter crop             | **Yes**                        | Removes strong false foreground                                       |
| Deskew                         | **Conditional**                | Don't resample pages already straight                                 |
| Dewarp                         | Probably unnecessary initially | Pages are fairly flat                                                 |
| Grayscale                      | Test                           | Often useful for Tesseract                                            |
| Background normalization       | **Yes, mild**                  | Addresses aged paper/illumination                                     |
| CLAHE                          | Test cautiously                | Can exaggerate bleed-through/noise                                    |
| Median/bilateral denoise       | Very mild                      | Bengali marks are thin                                                |
| Sharpening                     | Very mild or none              | Oversharpening can create false strokes                               |
| Adaptive threshold/Sauvola     | Experimental branch            | May help Tesseract but harm other models                              |
| Otsu threshold                 | Experimental branch            | Background is not spatially uniform                                   |
| 2× Lanczos/bicubic resize      | **Worth testing**              | Gives OCR approximately 300-dpi-sized input without inventing content |
| Morphological erosion/dilation | Generally avoid                | Dangerous for matras and conjuncts                                    |

A useful point here: **upscaling from 150 to 300 dpi does not recreate lost information**. It merely presents the existing information at a scale that some OCR recognizers handle better.

---

# 4. Bleed-through needs special attention

I think this is more important than super-resolution for this source.

The reverse-page text appears as weak, low-contrast structure behind the primary text. A naïve CLAHE or sharpening operation can therefore make the interference **stronger**.

So I would test two background approaches:

**A. Illumination/background division**

Estimate a slowly varying background using a large Gaussian/morphological kernel and divide/subtract it from the grayscale image.

**B. Local thresholding**

Use Sauvola/adaptive thresholding with a window substantially larger than a Bengali character.

Do **not** decide between them visually. Decide based on held-out CER.

---

# 5. Do we need VAE or diffusion enhancement?

## My recommendation: **No, not initially**

There are three reasons.

### 5.1 There is still considerable real character information

At 150 dpi the characters are small, but the strokes are mostly visibly present. You are dealing more with:

**noise + contrast + bleed-through + JPEG degradation**

than with:

**unrecoverable, extremely low-resolution glyphs**.

That is a much better setting for conservative preprocessing.

### 5.2 Generative restoration is unusually risky for OCR

For ordinary photographic SR, inventing plausible texture is often acceptable. For text, inventing or deleting a single stroke can change the character.

Recent text-super-resolution research explicitly notes this problem. TextSR reports that generic diffusion SR can produce inconsistent/hallucinated text details and therefore introduces OCR/text priors specifically to preserve multilingual character structure. ([arXiv][1])

So something that looks **sharper to a human** can actually produce **higher CER**.

### 5.3 A normal VAE is even less compelling here

A VAE trained for reconstruction tends to optimize a compressed latent representation. Fine textual structures are exactly the sort of high-frequency information that can be smoothed.

A VAE could make sense as part of a **specifically trained document restoration system**, but I would not add one simply because the images are old.

---

# 6. When I *would* test learned enhancement

I would only introduce it if your locked evaluation shows something approximately like:

> Best conservative preprocessing + best OCR still has unacceptable CER, with a substantial fraction of errors caused specifically by degraded strokes rather than OCR language modelling/layout.

Then test it as an ablation:

| Experiment | Input                         |
| ---------- | ----------------------------- |
| E0         | Raw scan                      |
| E1         | Classical preprocessing       |
| E2         | Classical + 2× interpolation  |
| E3         | Non-generative/task-driven SR |
| E4         | Text-aware diffusion SR       |

The selection metric must be **OCR accuracy**, not PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS or "looks clearer".

Task-driven document SR research similarly evaluates super-resolution according to its usefulness to OCR rather than only visual fidelity. ([arXiv][2])

For this project, I would put **E4 last**.

---

# 7. OCR systems I would actually benchmark

My current candidate set would be:

| Priority     | OCR                                       | Role                                          | Assessment for this book   |
| ------------ | ----------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------- | -------------------------- |
| **1**        | **Surya OCR 2**                           | Main modern open model                        | Very promising             |
| **2**        | **Tesseract 5 `tessdata_best` `ben+eng`** | Reproducible baseline                         | Essential                  |
| **3**        | **Google Document AI / Vision OCR**       | Cloud upper-bound comparator                  | Strong benchmark candidate |
| **4**        | **EasyOCR `bn+en`**                       | Alternative open baseline                     | Useful comparator          |
| Experimental | Custom Tesseract/recognizer fine-tuning   | Domain adaptation                             | Only after error analysis  |
| Low priority | PaddleOCR recognition                     | Not suitable out-of-box for Bengali currently | Don't use as primary       |

## Surya

This is the one I would test seriously now.

Its current implementation combines OCR with **layout analysis, reading order and table handling**, which is particularly relevant because your book contains headings, figures, captions, tables and regular body text. Its published repository reports Bengali performance separately on its multilingual benchmark (`bn`: 82.7% pass rate), though that is an internal benchmark and absolutely should **not** be treated as the expected accuracy on this book. ([GitHub][3])

Surya's own documentation also specifically suggests increasing resolution and trying binarization/deskewing for older or blurry scans. ([GitHub][3])

The integrated layout capability is arguably as valuable here as the recognizer.

---

## Tesseract

Tesseract should remain your **canonical naïve/open baseline**.

Run at least:

```text
ben
ben+eng
```

and use **`tessdata_best`**, not only whichever distro model happens to be installed.

Tesseract's official documentation describes `tessdata_best` as its more accurate, slower model set; importantly, those floating-point models can also serve as the basis for later LSTM fine-tuning. ([Tesseract OCR][4])

For this corpus, mixed-language recognition matters because passages frequently contain constructs such as Bengali prose followed by:

```text
Plant Pathology
Phytopathology
chlorophyll
Phytophthora infestans
Bacillus ...
```

A Bengali-only recognizer will corrupt useful scientific terminology.

---

## EasyOCR

EasyOCR officially added Bengali support and also supports custom recognition training. ([GitHub][5])

I would benchmark it, but I would place it behind Surya and Tesseract because the document is predominantly a **structured scanned book**, rather than arbitrary scene text.

---

## PaddleOCR

This is one case where I would **not automatically recommend a popular OCR model**.

PP-OCRv5 advertises 106 languages, but the current official supported-language table lists Devanagari languages, Tamil, Telugu, Arabic, etc., and **does not list Bengali** among its recognition models. ([PaddlePaddle][6])

So I would not use PP-OCRv5 recognition as a principal Bengali baseline unless you train/adapt a recognizer.

Some of Paddle's **document preprocessing/layout components** can still be useful independently.

---

## Cloud comparator

Google Cloud's current OCR documentation explicitly lists Bengali support, and Document AI OCR supports more than 200 languages. ([Google Cloud Documentation][7])

Even if you do not deploy a cloud system, processing your held-out pages once can be useful as an **external reference point**.

---

# 8. The held-out ground-truth set is the most important thing to build first

I would build this **before optimizing preprocessing**.

Otherwise you will end up choosing filters because the pages look nicer rather than because recognition improves.

### Proposed split

For the first reliable benchmark:

| Dataset             |               Pages | Purpose                              |
| ------------------- | ------------------: | ------------------------------------ |
| Development GT      |        **10 pages** | Tune preprocessing/PSM/OCR settings  |
| Locked test GT      |        **30 pages** | Final model/preprocessing comparison |
| Training/adaptation | Separate pages only | Used later if fine-tuning            |

Thirty test pages are enough to reveal systematic errors while keeping manual transcription manageable.

Do **not** choose 30 consecutive pages. Stratify them.

For the 30-page test set I would approximately target:

| Page category                            | Pages |
| ---------------------------------------- | ----: |
| Normal body text                         |    12 |
| Heavy bleed-through / low contrast       |     6 |
| Dense Bengali + English/scientific names |     4 |
| Figure/diagram + labels/captions         |     4 |
| Tables/list-like structures              |     2 |
| Stamps/handwriting/heavy contamination   |     2 |

Keep most pages representative of actual content ingestion rather than front matter.

---

# 9. Ground-truth annotation pipeline

I recommend the following procedure:

1. **Freeze the original page image.** Extract it once, store as lossless PNG/TIFF, and record PDF page number, image dimensions and SHA-256 hash.

2. **Assign page regions before transcription.** Use at least `BODY`, `HEADING`, `CAPTION`, `FIGURE_TEXT`, `TABLE`, `HEADER`, `FOOTER`, `PAGE_NUMBER`, and `NON_CONTENT`. Library stamps and handwriting should generally be `NON_CONTENT`, because you do not want them in the crop-disease knowledge base.

3. **Generate line polygons automatically**, for example from Surya/Tesseract text detection. These are only annotation proposals; the annotator must correct them.

4. **Manually transcribe each printed line against the original image.** Do not use an enhanced page as the authoritative image.

5. **Preserve the book exactly.** Do not modernize Bengali spelling, silently correct the author's spelling, translate terminology, change Bengali numerals to Arabic numerals, or replace Latin scientific names.

6. **Use Unicode NFC normalization.** Beyond Unicode canonical normalization and consistent whitespace policy, avoid linguistic normalization in OCR ground truth.

7. **Have a second person verify every final test page.** Ideally use independent transcription followed by adjudication. If annotation budget is limited, use first-pass transcription plus blind character-by-character verification.

8. **Lock the 30-page test set.** After looking at test CER, do not modify preprocessing or fine-tune on those pages. All iteration happens on the 10-page development set.

9. **Evaluate every OCR pipeline against exactly the same GT.** Preserve model outputs before any LLM correction.

10. **Bootstrap confidence intervals by page**, rather than treating every character as an independent sample. This avoids unrealistically narrow confidence intervals because errors on the same scanned page are correlated.

---

# 10. What exactly should count as ground truth?

This is particularly important for this book.

Suppose a page contains:

```text
শস্যের রোগ          <- running header
২৮                  <- page number

[main paragraph]

Phytophthora infestans

[figure]
[figure labels]
চিত্র ৪: ...

[BANSDOC Library stamp]
```

I would maintain separate targets.

### `body_gt`

Includes:

**heading + paragraph + scientific names + useful captions**

This is what matters most for RAG.

### `all_printed_gt`

Includes all legitimate printed text, including running headers and page numbers.

Useful for evaluating pure OCR.

### `figure_gt`

Captions and textual diagram labels.

Useful for multimodal/figure extraction.

### `non_content`

Stamps, handwritten catalogue notes, accidental marks.

Do not put these into the OCR reference string for the body-text metric.

This prevents a ridiculous situation where a better OCR system gets penalized because it correctly ignored a purple library stamp.

---

# 11. Transcription policy for Bengali deserves care

For example, distinguish:

```text
দ / ত
ব / ভ
ি / ী
ে / ৈ
ো /ৌ
ণ / ন
ড় / ড
ঢ় / ঢ
```

and particularly conjuncts such as:

```text
দ্ভ
ত্ত
ক্ত
ক্ষ
জ্ঞ
ন্ত্র
ষ্ট
দ্ধ
```

These are precisely the kinds of structures that become fragile at 150 dpi.

Also keep Bengali danda:

```text
।
```

distinct from:

```text
.
|
```

because OCR systems commonly confuse them.

For scientific names, preserve:

```text
Phytophthora infestans
```

rather than accepting something superficially similar such as:

```text
Phytophthora infestens
```

Those errors are disproportionately harmful for retrieval.

---

# 12. Metrics I recommend

Use **CER as your primary OCR metric**.

[
CER = \frac{S+D+I}{N}
]

where (S) = substitutions, (D) = deletions, (I) = insertions, and (N) = characters in the reference.

Then report:

| Metric                        | Why                                   |
| ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| **Overall CER**               | Primary OCR quality                   |
| Bengali CER                   | Core corpus accuracy                  |
| Latin/scientific-name CER     | Critical for disease/pathogen names   |
| Digit CER                     | Chapter/page/numeric information      |
| WER                           | Human-interpretable secondary measure |
| Line deletion rate            | Detect missing text                   |
| False-positive character rate | Detect bleed-through/stamp OCR        |
| Reading-order errors          | Important around figures/tables       |

For RAG, I would eventually add a second-level experiment:

**OCR pipeline → chunking → retrieval → answer evaluation**

because a reduction from, say, 6% CER to 5% CER is useful only if it improves retrieval or answer grounding.

---

# 13. Build an OCR error taxonomy

Don't only record a CER number.

For each dev-page error, classify it into something like:

| Error                          | Example cause              |
| ------------------------------ | -------------------------- |
| Bengali consonant substitution | Low character resolution   |
| Conjunct error                 | Joined/blurred strokes     |
| Vowel-mark deletion            | Tiny stroke lost           |
| Danda/punctuation error        | Low-resolution punctuation |
| Word-space insertion/deletion  | Dense printing             |
| Bengali/Latin confusion        | Mixed scripts              |
| Scientific-name corruption     | English model weakness     |
| False text                     | Bleed-through              |
| False text                     | Stamp                      |
| Missing line                   | Layout/detection failure   |
| Wrong order                    | Figure/table interference  |
| Figure-label omission          | Layout segmentation        |

After 10 dev pages, this table will tell us whether we actually need:

**better preprocessing, better recognition, better layout analysis, or fine-tuning.**

Without that analysis, it is very easy to solve the wrong problem.

---

# 14. Experimental matrix I would run

A manageable first experiment is:

| ID | Preprocessing                        | OCR                               |
| -- | ------------------------------------ | --------------------------------- |
| B0 | Raw scan                             | Tesseract `ben+eng`               |
| B1 | Raw scan                             | Tesseract `tessdata_best ben+eng` |
| P1 | Crop + deskew + background normalize | Tesseract best                    |
| P2 | P1 + 2× interpolation                | Tesseract best                    |
| P3 | P1 + local binarization              | Tesseract best                    |
| S0 | Raw / minimal                        | Surya                             |
| S1 | Crop + mild cleanup                  | Surya                             |
| E0 | Raw                                  | EasyOCR                           |
| C0 | Raw/minimal                          | Cloud OCR comparator              |

Only after these:

| ID  | Enhancement                    | OCR      |
| --- | ------------------------------ | -------- |
| SR1 | Non-generative SR              | best OCR |
| SR2 | Text-aware learned restoration | best OCR |
| SR3 | Text-aware diffusion           | best OCR |

That design will give you a defensible answer to the enhancement question rather than an intuition.

---

# 15. My expected winning configuration

Based on the pages I inspected, my starting hypothesis would be:

> **Conservative border/gutter cleanup + mild illumination normalization + optional 2× conventional upscaling + Surya or Tesseract-best bilingual OCR.**

I would **not binarize globally by default**.

For Surya specifically, I would probably give it relatively natural-looking RGB/grayscale pages and let the model handle text detection/layout. Surya's documentation itself notes that preprocessing such as binarization and deskewing can help difficult old scans, but that should be experimentally selected. ([GitHub][3])

For Tesseract, I would investigate the binarized branch more aggressively.

---

# 16. Fine-tuning should come after this benchmark

If the error analysis shows repeated book-specific errors—for example the same Bengali conjuncts are repeatedly confused because of this font/scanning style—**Tesseract fine-tuning becomes quite reasonable**. Tesseract's documentation specifically notes that fine-tuning from an existing language can work well when the new data mainly differs through font/domain characteristics and may require relatively modest additional data. ([Tesseract OCR][8])

At that stage, don't sacrifice the 30 held-out pages.

Create separate line crops from **other pages**, preferably thousands of lines using:

```text
line_image.png
line_image.gt.txt
```

and fine-tune `ben`/a bilingual recognition setup from `tessdata_best`.

If a transformer recognizer such as TrOCR is considered later, the same line-level GT architecture is reusable; TrOCR's design explicitly supports pretraining followed by fine-tuning on human-labelled OCR data. ([arXiv][9])

---

## Recommended project decision

I would formally define the pipeline now as:

**Stage 0 — Baseline**

`raw 150-dpi page → Tesseract ben+eng`

This remains your intentionally naïve OCR baseline.

**Stage 1 — Document preprocessing**

`lossless extraction → border/gutter removal → deskew if necessary → background normalization`

**Stage 2 — Recognition benchmark**

`Tesseract-best vs Surya vs EasyOCR vs optional cloud OCR`

**Stage 3 — Selective preprocessing**

Choose `raw / normalized / normalized+2× / binarized` solely using the **10-page development GT**.

**Stage 4 — Locked evaluation**

Report CER/WER and error breakdown on the untouched **30-page held-out test set**.

**Stage 5 — Only if necessary**

Fine-tune OCR or investigate task-driven/text-aware SR.

**I would leave VAE/diffusion out of the main pipeline until Stage 5.** For this particular source, I expect good OCR/model selection and bleed-through-aware preprocessing to give substantially more value per unit effort than generative enhancement.

For the downstream crop-disease RAG, I would also keep both `raw_ocr_text` and `normalized_text` with page/region provenance so that every retrieved passage remains auditable back to the original scan.

AI: Yes—**a chapter-level split is substantially more defensible than a random page-level 70/15/15 split for this book**, but I would make two adjustments to how you describe and construct it.

The scanned source is highly homogeneous in page style, typography, paper degradation, and layout, while chapters contain locally repeated terminology and structures. Randomly assigning neighboring pages to train and test would therefore create considerable leakage: the model could see visually and linguistically near-identical material during training. The uploaded scan illustrates this repeated textbook format and within-chapter continuity. 

## 1. Should "held-out" mean test only?

Terminologically:

* **Training set** — used to fit model parameters.
* **Validation set** — excluded from training, but used repeatedly for model selection, preprocessing choices, hyperparameters, early stopping, OCR thresholding, etc.
* **Test set** — completely untouched until the final evaluation.

So technically, **both validation and test are held out from training**. But when a paper says **"held-out test pages"**, that normally means the **test set specifically**.

For your workflow I would use:

> **Train chapters:** model fitting / preprocessing development
> **Validation chapters:** tuning and model/preprocessing selection
> **Held-out test chapters:** final evaluation only

The test set should essentially be treated as sealed.

---

## 2. Chapter-level splitting is the right idea

Your proposal is:

| Split      |     Chapters | # chapters | Pages | % chapters | % of 378 content pages |
| ---------- | -----------: | ---------: | ----: | ---------: | ---------------------: |
| Train      | Remaining 23 |         23 |   272 |      69.7% |              **72.0%** |
| Validation |    Ch. 18–22 |          5 |    68 |      15.2% |              **18.0%** |
| Test       |    Ch. 23–27 |          5 |    38 |      15.2% |              **10.1%** |
| **Total**  |              |         33 |   378 |       100% |                   100% |

So there are actually two ways of describing your split:

**By chapters:** approximately **70/15/15**.

**By pages:** approximately **72/18/10**.

That is **not inherently a problem**. You should simply report both rather than saying that you have a 70/15/15 page split.

I would write it conceptually as:

> "We partition the document at the chapter level to prevent page-level leakage. Of the 33 chapters, 23/5/5 chapters are assigned to training, validation, and test sets, respectively, corresponding to approximately 70/15/15% of chapters. Because chapter lengths vary, the resulting page distribution is approximately 72/18/10%."

That is methodologically much cleaner than artificially cutting chapters to obtain exact page percentages.

---

# 3. I would **not** optimize exclusively for chapter count, though

This is the one weakness in the current proposal.

Your validation set has **68 pages**, whereas the test set has only **38 pages**:

[
\frac{68}{378}=18.0%
]

versus

[
\frac{38}{378}=10.1%.
]

The problem isn't that the percentages differ. The problem is that the **final test set is the smaller of the two**.

Usually I would prefer the reverse:

[
N_\text{test} \geq N_\text{val}
]

or at least roughly equal sizes.

Why? Validation is mainly needed for choosing among preprocessing/OCR configurations. The test set determines the precision of your final reported CER/WER and downstream results.

### Therefore I would aim for something closer to

**70% train / 15% validation / 15% test by pages approximately**, *while preserving complete chapters*.

Something like:

* Train: ~265–275 pages
* Validation: ~50–60 pages
* Test: ~50–60 pages

would be somewhat stronger statistically.

But I **would not break a chapter merely to hit 15.0% exactly**.

---

# 4. More important concern: Ch. 18–27 are contiguous

This is potentially more important than the 68-vs-38-page imbalance.

Your current split is:

```text
...
Ch 17
------------------
Ch 18
Ch 19     VALIDATION
Ch 20
Ch 21
Ch 22
------------------
Ch 23
Ch 24     TEST
Ch 25
Ch 26
Ch 27
------------------
Ch 28
Ch 29
...
```

This produces a **temporally/positionally contiguous holdout**.

That is defensible if your intended evaluation question is:

> "Can the model generalize to a completely unseen continuous section of the book?"

But it is less representative if the intended question is:

> "How well will our OCR/preprocessing pipeline work across arbitrary unseen chapters from this book?"

For that second question, I would prefer chapter-level **stratified sampling across the document**, for example selecting test chapters from different portions of the book.

Not random pages—**random or stratified chapters**.

Why?

The book changes considerably in content and page structure. Earlier sections contain general plant-pathology material, microbiology, fungi, diagrams, Latin terminology, etc., while later chapters become crop/disease-specific. The scan also contains pages with dense prose, numbered lists, diagrams and mixed Bangla/English/scientific names. 

A test set containing only neighboring chapters may therefore accidentally measure performance on one particular region of the book.

---

# 5. For OCR specifically, I would stratify by **visual difficulty**, not merely chapter topic

This distinction is important.

For RAG, semantic diversity across chapters matters.

For OCR, what matters much more is:

* clean vs degraded pages;
* bleed-through;
* skew;
* page curvature;
* faded printing;
* stamps/handwriting;
* Bangla-only vs Bangla + English/Latin;
* tables/lists;
* diagrams and captions;
* unusual typography;
* dense vs sparse text.

Your scan contains several of these characteristics—for example diagrams interspersed with normal prose and mixed Bengali/Latin scientific terminology. 

So the ideal test set should contain a reasonable sample of **each visual condition**.

Chapter-level grouping prevents leakage, but visual stratification makes the evaluation representative.

---

# 6. Important distinction for your RAG system

There is one place where "test pages" can become confusing.

### OCR training

If you fine-tune an OCR model:

```text
Train chapters
     ↓
OCR model training

Validation chapters
     ↓
OCR tuning/model selection

Test chapters
     ↓
FINAL OCR CER/WER evaluation
```

The test chapter ground truth should never influence preprocessing decisions or fine-tuning.

### RAG evaluation

For your final KrishiPath RAG system, however, **the test chapters should normally still exist in the searchable corpus**.

Suppose a test question is:

> What are the symptoms of disease X?

and the answer occurs in Chapter 24.

The retriever obviously needs Chapter 24 in its document index.

What must remain held out are the **evaluation questions, reference answers, relevance judgments, and any supervised training examples derived from those chapters**.

So:

```text
                    RAG Corpus
                        │
          ┌─────────────┼──────────────┐
        Train          Val            Test
       chapters      chapters       chapters
          │             │              │
          └──────── all indexed ────────┘


Training QA pairs     ✓
Validation QA pairs   used for tuning
Test QA pairs         NEVER used for tuning
```

If you remove test chapters from the RAG corpus entirely, you are evaluating an **out-of-corpus question answering task**, which is not what your document-reading RAG is supposed to do.

---

# 7. What I recommend for this project

I would use the following protocol.

### OCR/preprocessing experiment

Keep **complete chapters as groups**.

Use approximately:

[
70% / 15% / 15%
]

but treat these as **targets rather than strict requirements**.

Choose chapters so that:

1. train has ~70% of pages;
2. validation has ~15%;
3. test has ~15%;
4. test contains several different page/scan conditions;
5. validation/test chapters do not overlap training;
6. test chapters are frozen before model development.

I would tolerate something like:

[
70/14/16,\quad 71/15/14,\quad 72/14/14
]

without concern.

I would be somewhat less happy with **72/18/10**, specifically because the test set is relatively small.

---

## 8. Your current Ch. 18–22 / Ch. 23–27 split is still defensible

I wouldn't call it incorrect.

I'd rate the choices approximately like this:

| Strategy                                                        | Assessment                       |
| --------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| Random 70/15/15 pages                                           | **Avoid** — leakage              |
| Complete chapters, exact 5/5                                    | **Good**                         |
| Your current 23/5/5 chapter split                               | **Good, but test is page-small** |
| Complete chapters chosen to approximate page balance            | **Better**                       |
| Chapter-grouped + visual/content stratification + ~page balance | **Best**                         |

So I **would not discard your current split solely because it gives 68 validation and 38 test pages**. It is scientifically explainable.

However, since you have not started annotation yet, **this is the moment to improve it**.

My preferred design would be:

> **Keep five validation and five test chapters if possible, but choose which chapters belong to each set based jointly on chapter length, document position/content, and OCR difficulty rather than simply taking Chapters 18–22 and 23–27 consecutively. Aim for roughly 50–60 pages in each validation and test set.**

That gives you the strongest argument in the methodology section: **group-wise, leakage-resistant, approximately balanced, and representative sampling**.

One final point: the **25 non-chapter pages out of the 403 total should not simply be folded into the 70/15/15 calculation**. Front matter, TOC, index, bibliography, etc. have structurally different layouts. I would keep them as a separate auxiliary/document-structure category rather than using them to determine the chapter split.

- Train: about 272 pages → ~67%
- Validation: 68 pages → ~17%
