## Held-out pages

Treat `grading_kit/heldout_pages/` as **test/evaluation-only** pages:

- Never train on them.
- Never tune preprocessing, select OCR models, choose chunking, or tune parameters based on their scores.
- Use their human transcriptions in `labels.jsonl` for final OCR evaluation and later grader checks.

So yes: your held-out pages should come from the **test partition only**, not validation.

Validation pages can have separate labels if you need them for OCR/preprocessing/model selection, but they should remain in the ordinary corpus area or a clearly documented development split—not in `grading_kit/heldout_pages/`.

## Best strategy

Add at least two more independently sourced Bengali crop-disease documents/books if possible, then split by source document:

```text
Document/book A, B, C → train
Document/book D       → validation
Document/book E       → test + held-out evaluation pages
```

This is the only approach that fully satisfies the documented split rule.

Each document should have a distinct `doc_id`, which your loader already derives from the folder under `data/raw/`:

```text
data/raw/
├── crop_disease_book_1/
├── crop_disease_book_2/
├── crop_disease_book_3/
├── crop_disease_book_4/
└── crop_disease_book_5/
```

## If you must use the single 403-page book

Use chapters as an explicitly stated **grouped proxy split**, not as a document-level split. Keep each complete chapter in exactly one partition; never let pages from a chapter appear in multiple splits.

Improve your current method by assigning chapters based on their **page totals**, not their count. Select whole chapters until you get as close as possible to:

```text
Train:      ~282 pages (70%)
Validation: ~60 pages (15%)
Test:       ~60 pages (15%)
```

The chapter title/contents pages can stay with their associated chapter rather than being ignored; assign every page once.

- Choose the test chapters first.
- Copy a representative subset of their page images into `grading_kit/heldout_pages/`.
- Manually transcribe those selected pages in `grading_kit/labels.jsonl`.
- Do not use those labelled held-out pages for any selection or tuning.
- Use validation chapters for OCR-model/preprocessing comparisons.

A safe statement for your report would be:

> Because the current corpus contains one source volume, we use non-overlapping complete chapters as grouped development partitions to reduce leakage. This is weaker than the required source-document split; future corpus expansion will add independently sourced Bengali crop-disease volumes and assign whole volumes to train, validation, and test.

That is honest, but the stronger recommendation remains: expand to multiple independent books before submission.

## Why I prefer this over Ch. 18–22 / 23–27

Your original split was:

* Validation: Ch. 18–22 → **68 pages**
* Test: Ch. 23–27 → **38 pages**

The new split fixes three issues.

### 1. Validation and test are exactly balanced

Both contain:

[
57/378 = 15.08%
]

So you no longer need to justify why validation has nearly twice as many pages as test.

### 2. The held-out chapters are distributed throughout the book

Instead of:

```text
Train ───────── Val Val Val Val Val ─ Test Test Test Test Test ─ Train
```

you get something closer to:

```text
V    T      T     V  T      V          T  V       T  V
│    │      │     │  │      │          │  │       │  │
1    6      8    12 14     17         23 25      30 32
```

This is much better for estimating **generalization across the document**, rather than performance on one continuous section.

### 3. The sets contain heterogeneous material

The proposed test set includes substantially different types of material:

* **Ch. 6 — Bacteria**, pp. 22–42
* **Ch. 8 — Virus**, pp. 110–122
* **Ch. 14 — fungicides and their use**, pp. 147–155
* **Ch. 23**, pp. 305–310
* **Ch. 30 — plant disease epidemiology**, pp. 362–369

Likewise, validation samples early conceptual material, disease-control material, crop-specific material, fruit-disease material, and late-book disease-resistance material. The TOC shows that these chapters occur across very different sections of the book. 

That diversity is particularly useful because your OCR has to cope not only with Bengali prose but also with **English/Latin scientific terminology, enumerations, diagrams, captions, and crop/disease names**.

## One important point

I would **not randomly regenerate this split every experiment**.

Once you choose it:

* `train_chapters = remaining 23`
* `val_chapters = [1, 12, 17, 25, 32]`
* `test_chapters = [6, 8, 14, 23, 30]`

freeze it and record it in your repository/configuration.

The **test chapters should not be inspected for CER/WER-driven preprocessing decisions**. You can create their ground truth, but during development you should only look at validation performance when deciding:

* preprocessing pipeline,
* binarization,
* deskewing,
* enhancement,
* OCR model,
* fine-tuning epochs,
* decoding configuration,
* VAE/diffusion enhancement, etc.

Then evaluate the frozen pipeline once on the 57-page test set.

[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\text{Validation} &= {1,12,17,25,32} && 57\text{ pages}\
\text{Test} &= {6,8,14,23,30} && 57\text{ pages}\
\text{Train} &= \text{remaining 23 chapters} && 264\text{ pages}
\end{aligned}}
]

For this particular single-document dataset, **I would use this instead of the contiguous Ch. 18–22 / Ch. 23–27 scheme**. It gives you a much cleaner methodological justification.

## How to populate `heldout_pages`

Your `heldout_pages` should be a labelled **evaluation subset of the 57-page test split**, not necessarily all 57 pages.

A practical A2 target:

- Select 15–25 pages across the five test chapters.
- Take approximately 3–5 pages per test chapter.
- Include a mix of:
  - normal dense text;
  - low-contrast/faded pages;
  - pages with Bangla conjuncts/matras;
  - pages with mixed Latin scientific terms;
  - lists, tables, headings, or complex layouts;
  - at least one genuinely hard page.

Copy only these selected images to:

```text
grading_kit/heldout_pages/
```

Then manually transcribe each selected page exactly in:

```text
grading_kit/labels.jsonl
```

Example:

```json
{"page_id": "krishipath_ch06_p014", "text": "exact human transcription here"}
```

Use IDs that match the held-out image stem exactly.

Do not use those pages to:

- fine-tune OCR;
- select an OCR model;
- choose preprocessing thresholds;
- choose chunk size;
- report development results.

They are your final A2 OCR evidence pages.

## Should test transcriptions be committed?

Yes. Commit the held-out **test page images** and their exact human transcriptions:

```text

├── heldout_pages/
│   ├── krishipath_ch06_p014.png
│   ├── krishipath_ch08_p007.png
│   └── ...
└── labels.jsonl
```

Do not commit the full raw corpus, but these selected held-out samples and labels must be in the repository because they are your reproducible A2 evaluation evidence.

## Do you need to transcribe validation pages?

Not necessarily all 57.

- If you use a frozen pretrained OCR model with no model/preprocessing selection: validation transcriptions are optional.
- If you compare OCR engines, preprocessing recipes, fine-tune any model, or tune thresholds: you need a separate labelled validation sample.

I recommend manually transcribing a smaller validation subset—about 10–15 pages, spread across all five validation chapters and covering the same difficult conditions. Use it only for development decisions.

Keep it clearly separate from the test set. The cleanest practice is:

```text
Validation labels: private/local development material used for model selection.
Test labels:       committed `grading_kit/labels.jsonl`, used only for final evidence.
```

If you need the validation labels in Git for team reproducibility, first decide a fixed existing location and schema together; do not mix validation records into `grading_kit/labels.jsonl` unless you add a clear split field and ensure your evaluation code cannot accidentally score validation and test together.

# Corpus provenance

* **Source (URL):** [শস্যের রোগ by Hasan Ashrafuzzaman](https://old.bansdoc.gov.bd/iaeabooks/IAEA%20Books/543%20%E0%A6%B6%E0%A6%B8%E0%A7%8D%E0%A6%AF%E0%A7%87%E0%A6%B0%20%E0%A7%8D%E0%A6%B0%E0%A7%8B%E0%A6%97-%E0%A6%B9%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%B8%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%A8%20%E0%A6%86%E0%A6%B6%E0%A6%B0%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%89%E0%A6%9C%E0%A7%8D%E0%A6%9C%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%AE%E0%A6%BE%E0%A6%A8.pdf)

* **Licence / usage rights:** Open access through the Government Digital Library Repository (BANSDOC); redistribution terms will be verified before publishing or redistributing the scans.

* **Pages:** 403
  **Words:** 116,539
  **Size on disk:** 60.13 MB

* **Scan/script difficulty notes:** Dense printed Bangla with conjuncts and vowel marks, mixed with English and Latin scientific terms. The scans contain fading, bleed-through, gutter shadows, uneven contrast, and occasional layout elements such as headings, lists, tables, figures, and captions.

* **Split policy:** The corpus currently consists of a single source volume, so an independent-document train/validation/test split is not possible. We therefore use a fixed, non-overlapping **chapter-grouped split** as a leakage-reduction proxy. Chapters, rather than individual pages, are the grouping unit so that adjacent pages from the same chapter cannot appear across development splits.

  * **Training:** the remaining 23 chapters, comprising 264 of the 378 chapter pages (69.8%).
  * **Validation:** chapters 1, 12, 17, 25, and 32, comprising 57 chapter pages (15.1%).
  * **Test:** chapters 6, 8, 14, 23, and 30, comprising 57 chapter pages (15.1%).
  * The remaining 25 non-chapter pages are treated as **auxiliary corpus pages**, rather than assigning them to the supervised training split. They may be processed and included in the final RAG corpus after the OCR/document-processing pipeline has been frozen.

  **OCR development protocol**

  * OCR fitting, fine-tuning, preprocessing selection, and any OCR-specific hyperparameter selection are performed using the **training chapters only**.
  * If OCR model selection requires a development set, an internal development subset is created from within the training chapters; external validation or test chapters are not used for OCR fitting or preprocessing selection.
  * After the OCR and document-preprocessing pipeline is finalized, it is **frozen** and applied to all 403 pages, including validation chapters, test chapters, and auxiliary non-chapter pages.
  * A predefined, stratified subset of pages from the five test chapters is manually transcribed to create OCR ground truth under `grading_kit/heldout_pages/` with corresponding `grading_kit/labels.jsonl` records. Page selection is fixed independently of model errors and should represent relevant scan/layout conditions.
  * Test-page ground-truth transcriptions are reserved exclusively for final OCR evaluation and must not be used to select OCR models, preprocessing operations, enhancement methods, or hyperparameters.

  **RAG development and evaluation protocol**

  * Once the OCR/document-processing pipeline is frozen, all 403 pages are processed through the same pipeline.
  * The resulting corpus is chunked, embedded, and indexed across the **entire document**, including training, validation, and test chapters. Validation and test chapters are therefore present in the searchable RAG corpus; they are not treated as unseen documents.
  * Any component that changes the stored corpus representation—such as OCR preprocessing, chunking strategy, chunk size/overlap, or embedding model—must be finalized before the final evaluation index is frozen.
  * RAG/agent runtime parameters that do not alter the indexed corpus, such as retrieval thresholds, reranking thresholds, abstention criteria, or agent-routing policies, may be tuned using **validation questions derived from the validation chapters**.
  * After validation-based selection is complete, all pipeline and agent parameters are frozen.
  * Final QA evaluation uses **test questions derived from the five test chapters**. Test questions, reference answers, relevance judgments, and other test annotations must not be used during model development or parameter selection.
  * The same frozen all-page index is used for final evaluation; test performance is measured on previously unseen evaluation questions, not by withholding the corresponding source pages from retrieval.

  **Evaluation**

  * Evaluation is conducted at two levels:

    1. **OCR accuracy:** measured on the manually transcribed held-out subset from the test chapters using metrics such as CER and WER.
    2. **RAG/QA accuracy:** measured using the frozen system and held-out test questions, with retrieval and answer-quality metrics defined separately.

  **Limitation and mitigation:** Because all splits originate from the same physical volume, typography, printing characteristics, scan conditions, vocabulary, and authorial style remain correlated across training, validation, and test chapters. The chapter-grouped split reduces direct page-level and within-chapter leakage but does not measure generalization to an independently scanned document. This limitation will be reported explicitly. If feasible, later evaluation will include one or more independent Bangla crop-disease documents as an external test corpus.

### Why this version is stronger

The methodological boundary now becomes:

[
\text{OCR train}
\rightarrow
\boxed{\text{freeze OCR/preprocessing}}
\rightarrow
\text{OCR all 403 pages}
\rightarrow
\text{build full-corpus index}
\rightarrow
\text{validation QA tuning}
\rightarrow
\boxed{\text{freeze system}}
\rightarrow
\text{test OCR + test QA}
]

The most important distinction is:

**“Held out” does not mean “absent from the RAG corpus.”**

For OCR, the test **labels** are held out. For QA, the test **questions/answers/judgments** are held out. The underlying test-chapter pages can—and for a document-grounded RAG system, should—be present in the frozen searchable index.

I would use this revised formulation over the current one.

There are also two points worth tightening.

### 1. A subset of the test partition is correct for `heldout_pages/`

You **do not need to manually transcribe all 57 test pages**.

The assignment explicitly describes `grading_kit/heldout_pages/` as a small set of page images that are never used for OCR training, with `labels.jsonl` as their ground-truth transcription. The A1 build guide says to “transcribe a few page-images,” and A2 asks for OCR quality on a **labelled sample**, not the entire test partition.  The submission guide reinforces this by saying not to commit the full corpus and to commit only the few held-out sample pages. 

So distinguish these concepts:

| Term                 | Meaning                                                            |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Test partition**   | All 57 pages belonging to the five test chapters                   |
| **OCR held-out set** | A stratified manually transcribed subset of those 57 pages         |
| **QA test set**      | Final unseen questions authored/verified from test-chapter content |

That is a standard and defensible design. I would select the OCR held-out subset **before looking at model errors**, and stratify it across the five test chapters and page conditions: clean/degraded, dense prose, mixed Bangla-English/Latin, headings/lists, figures/captions, etc.

Transcribing all 57 pages would give a stronger OCR estimate, but it is not required and is probably an inefficient use of annotation effort.

### 2. The validation set has a specific role

Based on the clarification you received, **validation chapters should primarily be used for RAG/agent development after the OCR/index pipeline is frozen**.

The clean workflow is:

[
\text{OCR training on Train}
\rightarrow
\text{freeze OCR}
\rightarrow
\text{OCR all 403 pages}
\rightarrow
\text{build full index}
\rightarrow
\text{tune on Validation questions}
\rightarrow
\text{freeze system}
\rightarrow
\text{evaluate on Test}
]

The validation questions can tune things such as `top-k`, `weak_threshold`, `k_step`, `k_max`, reranking settings, evidence/abstention thresholds, confidence thresholds, and prompt/agent-policy choices. This fits the mandatory evidence-gated re-search behaviour specified by the project.  The codebase even explicitly defines calibration as fitting temperature on validation data. 

For **OCR model/preprocessing selection**, if you need validation during fine-tuning, take a small internal development subset from the **23 training chapters**. Do not use the five external validation chapters for OCR selection if you want to follow the instructor's Q&A literally.

This gives you:

```text
TRAIN chapters
├── OCR fitting
└── internal OCR-dev subset if needed

VALIDATION chapters
└── RAG/agent development questions

TEST chapters
├── OCR held-out labelled subset → final OCR metric
└── final QA questions → final RAG/agent metric
```

That is cleaner than using the same validation chapters for everything.

Your chapter split is technically an exception to the generic assignment rule. The walkthrough says the preferred approach is to split by whole books/documents because pages from the same book are correlated.  The project specification repeats that document-level splitting is the preferred leakage-safe unit. 

However, the instructor clarification you received explicitly addresses your **single-document case** and permits blocks/chapters for train/validation/test. Therefore your chapter-grouped split is appropriate as long as you state that it is a **proxy necessitated by having one source volume**, not equivalent to independent-document generalization.

ME: based on all the context, prepare a detailed preprocessing pipeline and OCR stage plan alongside other possible improvements for this use case. @krishipath.pdf and other docs in sources
AI: Below is the pipeline I would use for **KrishiPath**, taking into account the actual scan characteristics of *শস্যের রোগ*, your fixed chapter split, the assignment architecture, and the requirement to fine-tune/evaluate OCR rather than merely call an OCR API.

The central recommendation is:

> **Do not make generative enhancement the default.** Build a conservative classical preprocessing + layout + Bangla-capable OCR pipeline first, quantify its errors, fine-tune the recognizer using training-only labels, and introduce VAE/diffusion enhancement only as a gated branch if it measurably improves OCR on degraded pages.

That is particularly important here because Bengali vowel marks, conjuncts, and the horizontal headline/matra are easy to damage with aggressive denoising, morphology, or generative reconstruction.

---

# 1. What the corpus actually requires

The rendered book pages show several distinct OCR problems. Normal body pages are predominantly single-column printed Bangla, but there is substantial mixed-script content: English terminology such as *Plant Pathology*, historical names, and Latin scientific names occur inside Bangla sentences. There is also visible background show-through/bleed-through, non-uniform illumination, gutter darkening, faded print, page-edge shadows, occasional stamps and handwriting, contents/list pages, and later figures with captions. 

This maps well onto the assignment's designated engineering requirements. The workbook explicitly associates Bangla with conjunct/matra-aware OCR, Bangla-English code-switching with mixed-script reading, bleed-through with suppression, and figure-heavy material with layout detection.  The codebase then places classical preprocessing in `ingest/preprocess.py`, optional generative repair in `ingest/enhance.py`, layout segmentation in `vision/layout.py`, and script-aware recognition in `vision/ocr.py`. 

So I would characterize the actual technical problem as:

[
\boxed{
\text{moderately degraded scan}
+
\text{Bangla script complexity}
+
\text{Bangla/Latin code-switching}
+
\text{occasional non-prose layout}
}
]

The **dominant difficulty is recognition**, not image reconstruction.

---

# 2. Recommended end-to-end OCR pipeline

```text
PDF
 ↓
native page image extraction
 ↓
page quality analysis
 ↓
border/gutter cleanup
 ↓
illumination + contrast normalization
 ↓
light denoising
 ↓
deskew / optional dewarp
 ↓
multiple image representations
 ├── normalized grayscale
 └── adaptive-binarized
 ↓
layout detection
 ↓
text-region / line extraction
 ↓
Bangla + English/Latin OCR
 ↓
Unicode/script normalization
 ↓
raw OCR + normalized OCR retained separately
 ↓
chunking
 ↓
embedding
 ↓
full-document index
```

This follows the assignment's required A2 ordering of ingest → preprocessing → layout → OCR → chunk → embed → index. 

---

# 3. Stage 0 — Preserve the raw source

Do **not** begin by modifying the page images.

For every page, preserve:

| Field               | Purpose                               |
| ------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| `document_id`       | stable source identity                |
| `page_id`           | unique internal page ID               |
| PDF page            | reproducibility                       |
| printed page number | citation mapping                      |
| chapter             | split enforcement                     |
| raw image hash      | detect accidental changes             |
| split               | train / validation / test / auxiliary |
| raw image           | immutable source                      |

Extract the embedded raster at its **native resolution** whenever possible. Rendering a low-resolution raster PDF at an artificially high DPI does not restore missing detail; it mostly resamples existing pixels.

Keep the raw image forever. Every experiment should derive from it.

---

# 4. Stage 1 — Automated page-quality profiling

Before designing preprocessing thresholds, quantify the corpus.

For every page calculate a small set of inexpensive measurements:

| Measurement                          | What it detects         |
| ------------------------------------ | ----------------------- |
| grayscale mean/std                   | brightness and contrast |
| local contrast                       | faded pages             |
| Laplacian variance                   | blur                    |
| estimated skew angle                 | rotation                |
| dark-pixel distribution near borders | gutter/edge shadow      |
| background variance                  | uneven illumination     |
| connected-component density          | unusual layouts         |
| text-area ratio                      | sparse vs dense pages   |
| OCR confidence from baseline         | recognition difficulty  |

Then group pages loosely into:

**normal**, **faded**, **high bleed-through**, **shadow/gutter**, **figure-heavy**, and **non-content/front matter**.

Do this using **training data only when thresholds are tuned**.

This gives you a much stronger A2 analysis than saying simply "the pages are degraded."

---

# 5. Stage 2 — Conservative classical preprocessing

This should be your production default.

## 5.1 Border and gutter handling

The pages have dark vertical gutter regions and occasional adjacent-page material. 

Detect large continuous dark regions near the left/right edges and crop them conservatively.

Do **not** crop based simply on fixed percentages because:

* left/right pages have different gutters;
* page numbers may lie close to edges;
* figure captions can extend further than normal text.

Use content-aware cropping.

---

## 5.2 Grayscale conversion

Maintain:

```text
raw RGB
normalized grayscale
```

You usually do not need RGB for recognition, but retaining it helps later inspection and enhancement experiments.

---

## 5.3 Background/illumination correction

This corpus has yellowed paper and uneven illumination.

Estimate a slowly varying background using either:

* large-kernel Gaussian filtering, or
* morphological opening.

Then normalize foreground relative to the estimated background.

Conceptually:

[
I_{\text{norm}}
===============

\frac{I}{B+\epsilon}
]

where:

* (I) = grayscale page,
* (B) = estimated background illumination,
* (\epsilon) prevents numerical instability.

This is preferable to globally increasing contrast because it handles localized gutter darkness.

---

## 5.4 Light denoising

Start with either:

* small median filtering, or
* mild bilateral filtering.

The critical word is **mild**.

Bangla contains small distinctions that can disappear under aggressive smoothing. In particular, you do not want preprocessing to eliminate small vowel signs or weaken the continuous matra.

---

# 6. Stage 3 — Deskew and dewarping

Estimate skew using text baselines, projection profiles, or Hough-line statistics.

Apply rotation only when the estimated skew is confidently non-zero.

For most pages here, severe geometric distortion does not appear to be the primary issue.  Therefore:

**deskew = normal preprocessing**

but:

**heavy dewarping = conditional**

Do not run expensive page-dewarping models indiscriminately.

---

# 7. Stage 4 — Do not commit to one binarization

I would preserve at least two OCR candidates:

```text
A = illumination-corrected grayscale
B = adaptive-binarized page
```

For binarization compare:

* Otsu;
* Sauvola/adaptive thresholding.

The book's uneven paper background makes adaptive thresholding worth testing, but it can also amplify bleed-through.

Therefore the decision should be empirical:

[
P^*=\arg\min_P CER(P)
]

on your **training-internal OCR development set**.

Do not decide based on visual appearance.

The image that "looks cleaner" to a human is not necessarily the one with lower OCR error.

---

# 8. Avoid aggressive morphological operations

This deserves special emphasis for Bangla.

Morphological opening can remove small vowel signs.

Aggressive erosion can break conjuncts.

Excessive closing can join neighboring characters.

Therefore I would restrict morphology to very small kernels and include a **no-morphology baseline**.

Your ablation should explicitly compare:

```text
normalization only
normalization + denoise
normalization + binarization
normalization + binarization + morphology
```

If morphology does not reduce CER, remove it.

---

# 9. Stage 5 — Layout analysis

The assignment explicitly requires layout detection rather than sending every full page blindly into recognition. 

For this book, detect at least:

| Region type            | OCR treatment                                 |
| ---------------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| body text              | OCR normally                                  |
| heading                | OCR separately                                |
| numbered/list item     | preserve list order                           |
| page number            | metadata, usually exclude from retrieval text |
| figure                 | do not treat drawing as prose                 |
| figure caption         | OCR and preserve                              |
| table/list             | preserve row/order structure                  |
| stamp                  | generally exclude from corpus text            |
| handwritten annotation | mark/ignore unless relevant                   |

The contents pages are an obvious example where reading order and alignment matter, while ordinary chapter pages are mostly single-column. 

### Practical approach

For this corpus I would **not start with an extremely complex document-layout model**.

Start with:

1. page-area detection;
2. text-box detection;
3. grouping boxes into lines/blocks;
4. heuristic heading/page-number/figure handling.

Then benchmark a learned layout system if needed.

Surya currently provides OCR plus layout analysis and reading order across 91 languages, including Bengali; its published internal Bengali score is 82.7%, although that score is **not CER and should not be compared directly with your OCR metrics**. ([GitHub][1])

It therefore makes a useful **benchmark/layout candidate**, although its current implementation is relatively large and GPL-licensed. ([GitHub][2])

---

# 10. Line-level extraction is important

If your chosen recognizer operates on lines or text regions, crop lines with **generous vertical padding**.

This matters especially for Bangla because vowel marks can extend above or below the main character body.

A detector that tightly crops around the character body can create OCR errors before recognition even begins.

Therefore:

```text
detected line
     ↓
expand bounding box slightly vertically/horizontally
     ↓
recognizer
```

The expansion amount should be tuned on internal train-development data rather than hard-coded from the test set.

---

# 11. OCR engines I would actually compare

I would run **three baseline families**, then fine-tune one.

| OCR                                             | Role                      | Assessment for KrishiPath          |
| ----------------------------------------------- | ------------------------- | ---------------------------------- |
| **Tesseract 5 `ben+eng` using `tessdata_best`** | classical baseline        | Mandatory comparison               |
| **EasyOCR `bn+en`**                             | neural baseline           | Strong practical comparison        |
| **Surya 2 `bn`/multilingual**                   | modern document benchmark | Strong accuracy/layout reference   |
| PaddleOCR PP-OCRv5                              | optional                  | Do not make default for Bangla     |
| vanilla TrOCR checkpoint                        | experimental fine-tuning  | Only with proper Bangla adaptation |

Tesseract's official `tessdata_best` repository includes `ben.traineddata`, and the official training resources contain Bengali character data. ([GitHub][3])

EasyOCR has supported Bengali for several years and its architecture allows Bengali together with English, which matches this corpus's mixed-script pages. ([GitHub][4])

Surya explicitly benchmarks Bengali and includes layout/reading-order functionality. ([GitHub][1])

I would **not choose PaddleOCR PP-OCRv5 as the out-of-box Bengali recognizer** right now: its current official 106-language list surprisingly does not list Bengali, even though nearby Indic languages such as Hindi, Nepali, Tamil and Telugu are supported. ([PaddlePaddle][5]) It remains trainable, but there is no reason to add that work unless it wins an experiment.

---

# 12. Fine-tuning strategy

The assignment expects a pretrained recognizer to be reproduced/fine-tuned rather than only referenced. 

I would therefore use the following strategy.

### Phase A — benchmark first

Run all baseline OCR systems **before fine-tuning**.

Measure:

[
CER=\frac{S+D+I}{N}
]

and

[
WER=\frac{S_w+D_w+I_w}{N_w}
]

where (S,D,I) are substitutions, deletions and insertions.

Also calculate the assignment's character-level OCR F1.

This gives you a defensible pretrained baseline.

### Phase B — build training OCR ground truth

The test `grading_kit/labels.jsonl` **must not be used**.

Create OCR supervision only from the 23 training chapters.

You do not need to transcribe all 264 pages. A better strategy is to sample training pages covering:

* clean pages;
* faded pages;
* severe bleed-through;
* mixed Bangla/English;
* scientific Latin terminology;
* headings/lists;
* figure captions.

Then convert those pages into text-region or line-level `(image, transcription)` pairs.

I would start modestly and construct a **learning curve** rather than declaring an arbitrary huge annotation target:

[
10 \rightarrow 20 \rightarrow 30 \rightarrow 40 \text{ labelled training pages}
]

and observe whether CER continues improving.

Reserve a small subset of **training chapters** as an internal OCR-development set.

That preserves your external validation chapters for RAG tuning.

---

# 13. Which model should you fine-tune?

My preferred order is:

### Practical option — Bengali-capable recognizer

Fine-tune a recognizer that already has Bengali character support, such as the EasyOCR recognition architecture or Tesseract's Bengali LSTM.

This minimizes the amount of labelled data needed.

### More ambitious option — transformer OCR

TrOCR is architecturally attractive because it uses a Transformer image encoder and Transformer text decoder and is designed for fine-tuning on labelled image/text pairs. ([arXiv][6])

But do **not** assume a standard English TrOCR checkpoint is already a Bengali OCR model.

For Bengali, you must ensure that:

* tokenizer/decoder vocabulary represents Bengali Unicode correctly;
* conjunct sequences are supported;
* Bengali training samples are sufficiently represented.

Therefore I would treat TrOCR as your **experimental trained model**, not your only baseline.

---

# 14. Training augmentation

Augmentation should imitate the actual scan, not generic computer-vision distortions.

Useful training-only augmentations include:

| Augmentation                  | Why                    |
| ----------------------------- | ---------------------- |
| slight rotation               | scanner skew           |
| mild blur                     | focus/scan degradation |
| contrast variation            | fading                 |
| brightness gradients          | gutter/illumination    |
| synthetic background noise    | paper texture          |
| mild bleed-through simulation | reverse-page text      |
| slight scale variation        | line-size variation    |

Avoid large rotations, heavy elastic distortion, extreme blur, or aggressive perspective transforms unless those conditions genuinely exist.

The goal is:

[
p_{\text{augmentation}}(x)
\approx
p_{\text{real degradation}}(x)
]

not "make training images as distorted as possible."

---

# 15. VAE/diffusion enhancement: my recommendation

## Do not use it globally

The project specification places generative enhancement in Stage 1, but the operative codebase guide treats VAE/diffusion repair as an optional/gated enhancement for degraded material rather than something every page must use.

Based on the visible pages, much of the book is **degraded but legible**, rather than severely destroyed. 

So I would use:

```text
classical preprocessing
        ↓
OCR confidence / quality gate
        ↓
good → normal OCR
bad  → optional enhancement → re-OCR
```

This also gives the later agent's `enhance_page()` tool a meaningful use.

### Why not enhance everything?

A generative model can alter:

* Bengali vowel marks;
* conjunct shapes;
* punctuation;
* digits;
* Latin species names.

A visually attractive reconstruction that modifies one character is **worse OCR input**.

So enhancement survives only if an ablation shows:

[
CER_{\text{enhanced}}
<
CER_{\text{classical}}
]

on genuinely degraded internal-dev pages **without worsening clean pages**.

---

# 16. Post-OCR processing

Maintain two representations:

```text
raw_ocr
normalized_ocr
```

Never overwrite the first.

### `raw_ocr`

Used for:

* OCR auditing;
* CER/WER evaluation;
* reproducing model output.

### `normalized_ocr`

Used for retrieval/indexing.

Perform only deterministic operations initially:

```text
Unicode NFC normalization
whitespace normalization
line-break repair
safe dehyphenation
running header/footer removal
page-number removal from body text
```

Do **not** blindly spell-correct scientific names.

A text such as:

```text
Phytophthora infestans
```

should not be "corrected" through a Bengali lexicon.

---

# 17. Script-aware postprocessing

Your book contains frequent Bangla–Latin transitions. 

Token-level script identification is therefore useful:

```text
বাংলা রোগের কারণ Phytophthora infestans ...
██████████████ ███████████████
BN             LATIN
```

Use different correction policies:

[
f(t)=
\begin{cases}
f_{\text{bn}}(t), & t\in\text{Bangla}\
f_{\text{latin}}(t), & t\in\text{Latin}\
t, & \text{otherwise}
\end{cases}
]

For Latin tokens, build a small domain lexicon from botanical names occurring in the **training corpus**.

For Bangla, use constrained normalization rather than free-form LLM rewriting.

---

# 18. Do not use an LLM to "clean" your test OCR

This is important experimentally.

A generative language model could turn:

```text
ফাইটোপথোরা ...
```

into a plausible but unsupported spelling.

That would mix **OCR recognition** with **language-model reconstruction**, making CER uninterpretable.

If you later test LLM-based OCR correction for RAG, create it as a **separate ablation**:

```text
raw OCR
deterministically normalized OCR
LLM-corrected OCR
```

and retain all three.

---

# 19. Ground-truth design

Your previous split remains appropriate:

```text
Train:
23 chapters, 264 pages

Validation:
1, 12, 17, 25, 32
57 pages

Test:
6, 8, 14, 23, 30
57 pages
```

For OCR:

```text
TRAIN CHAPTERS
├─ OCR training labels
└─ internal OCR-dev labels

TEST CHAPTERS
└─ stratified held-out OCR subset
```

For RAG:

```text
VALIDATION CHAPTERS
└─ RAG / retrieval / agent tuning questions

TEST CHAPTERS
└─ final QA questions
```

The assignment explicitly defines `grading_kit/heldout_pages/` as pages never used for OCR training and `labels.jsonl` as their transcription oracle.  It also explicitly says only a few held-out sample pages need to be committed rather than the complete corpus. 

---

# 20. Held-out-page sampling

Do **not** simply take 15 random test pages.

Use stratification across:

[
\text{chapter}\times
\text{scan quality}\times
\text{layout}\times
\text{script mix}
]

For example, make sure the held-out set contains:

* pages from all five test chapters;
* normal prose;
* degraded prose;
* mixed Bangla/Latin;
* headings/lists;
* at least some captions or irregular layout.

Crucially:

> Select these pages **before examining which pages your final model fails on**.

Otherwise the evaluation sample becomes model-dependent.

---

# 21. OCR metrics I would report

Your main table should eventually look like:

| System                    | CER ↓ | WER ↓ | Char-F1 ↑ | Mixed-script CER ↓ |
| ------------------------- | ----: | ----: | --------: | -----------------: |
| Tesseract raw             |       |       |           |                    |
| Tesseract + preprocessing |       |       |           |                    |
| EasyOCR raw               |       |       |           |                    |
| Best neural baseline      |       |       |           |                    |
| Fine-tuned OCR            |       |       |           |                    |
| + optional enhancement    |       |       |           |                    |

Also break errors down into:

**Bangla base characters / vowel signs / conjuncts / punctuation / Bengali digits / Latin scientific terms / segmentation errors**.

That error analysis is much more useful academically than only reporting one average accuracy number.

The walkthrough explicitly expects OCR quality on a labelled sample and an honest worst-failure analysis. 

---

# 22. Ablations you should run

This is probably the strongest experiment set for A2:

| Experiment                | Question                              |
| ------------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| Raw image → OCR           | baseline                              |
| + crop/gutter removal     | does page cleanup matter?             |
| + illumination correction | does background normalization matter? |
| + denoise                 | does noise removal help?              |
| + adaptive threshold      | is binarization beneficial?           |
| + layout segmentation     | does region OCR beat full-page OCR?   |
| + OCR fine-tuning         | value of corpus adaptation            |
| + generative enhancement  | does VAE/diffusion actually help?     |

Do **not** assume each additional stage is beneficial.

A stage remains in production only if the measured result supports it.

---

# 23. Improvements beyond OCR

Once OCR is stable, there are several particularly useful improvements for this corpus.

First, preserve **page and chapter metadata** with every chunk, because crop-disease terminology repeats across multiple chapters. Second, use heading-aware or paragraph-aware chunking rather than cutting through disease descriptions. Third, retain both raw and normalized OCR so retrieval can benefit from normalization without compromising auditability. Fourth, consider hybrid retrieval later because dense retrieval tolerates some OCR noise while character/keyword retrieval can be valuable for exact disease names and Latin taxa. Finally, make `read_page` and `enhance_page` useful at query time: if retrieved text is low-confidence, the agent can reopen the source image and re-OCR it using the stronger path rather than trusting corrupted indexed text.

This fits the assignment's intended agent design, where retrieved pages can be read and optionally enhanced again rather than treating preprocessing as irrevocable. 

---

# 25. Recommended implementation order

If I were implementing this now, I would proceed in this exact order:

1. **Freeze chapter/split metadata.**
2. **Extract native page images and run EDA.**
3. **Create stratified OCR training GT + internal train-dev GT + frozen held-out test pages.**
4. **Benchmark Tesseract `ben+eng`, EasyOCR `bn+en`, and one modern document OCR.**
5. **Run preprocessing ablations against the internal OCR-dev set.**
6. **Implement layout/line extraction and re-evaluate.**
7. **Fine-tune the best practical Bengali-capable recognizer.**
8. **Perform script-aware deterministic normalization.**
9. **Test VAE/diffusion only on the degraded subgroup.**
10. **Freeze the OCR/preprocessing pipeline.**
11. **OCR all 403 source pages with the frozen pipeline.**
12. **Chunk → embed → index the entire volume.**
13. **Evaluate final OCR once on `grading_kit/heldout_pages/`.**
14. **Use validation-chapter QA to tune retrieval/agent settings.**
15. **Freeze the RAG system and evaluate final test questions.**

That sequence gives you a clean experimental story:

[
\boxed{
\text{raw baseline}
\rightarrow
\text{preprocessing}
\rightarrow
\text{layout}
\rightarrow
\text{fine-tuning}
\rightarrow
\text{optional enhancement}
}
]

rather than an opaque pipeline where several methods are introduced simultaneously.

One practical note: the `krishipath.pdf` attachment currently exposed here contains **150 PDF pages**, while your corpus provenance describes a 403-page source.  For the actual experiments and the assignment's ≥300-page requirement, make sure `get_data.sh` retrieves the complete version you used to derive the 403-page statistics. The assignment explicitly requires ≥300 pages and ≥60,000 usable words. 

**The most defensible production configuration for this corpus is therefore:**

[
\boxed{
\text{classical adaptive preprocessing}
\rightarrow
\text{layout/line segmentation}
\rightarrow
\text{fine-tuned Bangla-capable OCR}
\rightarrow
\text{script-aware deterministic normalization}
}
]

with **VAE/diffusion as a measured fallback/ablation, not the default preprocessing stage**.

### Preprocessing study

I made this considerably more extensive than the previous notebook. It evaluates both **stage-wise ablations** and complete candidate pipelines:

```text
raw_gray
stretch

crop_only
deskew_only
bg_only
crop_bg
deskew_bg
crop_deskew_bg

bg_clahe
bg_median
bg_bilateral

bg_otsu
bg_adaptive
bg_sauvola

bg_sauvola_open
bg_sauvola_close
```

This lets you answer both:

> “Which final preprocessing pipeline performs best?”

and the more scientifically useful:

> “Which individual operation actually contributed?”

For example, you can distinguish the effects of:

[
\text{raw}
\rightarrow
\text{crop}
\rightarrow
\text{deskew}
\rightarrow
\text{background normalization}
]

instead of comparing only a raw image with a complex final pipeline.

It also computes non-OCR **preservation diagnostics** such as connected-component fragmentation, foreground changes, edge-density changes, and sharpness. These do **not** claim OCR improvement; they are intended to catch preprocessing that visually cleans a page while damaging Bengali character structure.

### Proper OCR-driven preprocessing comparison

Once you prepare a small **training-only OCR-development transcription set**, the notebook can perform the stronger experiment:

[
\text{same labelled pages}
\times
\text{all preprocessing variants}
\times
\text{same OCR engine}
\rightarrow
CER/WER
]

There are implementations for:

* Unicode-safe evaluation normalization;
* CER;
* WER;
* Bengali/Latin script statistics;
* optional Tesseract `ben+eng`;
* optional EasyOCR `bn+en`.

The OCR probes are **disabled by default**, so the base EDA does not depend on OCR installations or model downloads.

There is also an optional second-stage parameter sweep for:

* CLAHE `clipLimit`;
* adaptive-threshold block size and (C);
* Sauvola window size and (k).

That sweep is also off by default because it should only be run after a preprocessing family has shown actual value.

### Held-out protection

I made the notebook deliberately difficult to misuse.

The final held-out OCR block defaults to:

```python
ENABLE_TEST_LABEL_EVAL = False
```

and the intended sequence is:

```text
TRAIN chapters
    ↓
EDA
    ↓
training OCR labels
    ↓
internal training-development labels
    ↓
preprocessing selection
    ↓
OCR selection/fine-tuning
    ↓
freeze OCR + preprocessing
    ↓
TEST held-out labels
    ↓
final CER/WER
```

The externally designated validation chapters therefore remain available later for **RAG/agent tuning**, rather than being consumed for OCR model selection.

```python
CHAPTER_PDF_RANGES = {
    # 1: (start_pdf_index, end_pdf_index),
}
```

These are **0-based PDF indices**, not printed page numbers. Once you verify those 33 ranges against the full source PDF, all split-aware analysis becomes active automatically.

This is preferable to trying to infer the mapping from Bengali printed page numbers and risking a silent split error.

It verifies 403 total images, 378 chapter pages, 264 train chapter pages, 57 validation pages, 57 test pages, and 25 auxiliary pages. If rerunning after an output already exists:

```bash
bash dump/split_krishipath_by_chapter.sh --force
```

`--force` deletes only `dump/krishipath_chapter_split/` before recreating it. Do not add `dump/` when committing.

[

The ranges remain **inclusive at both ends**, which matches how the EDA's chapter-range validation/assignment code was written.

With this mapping:

* PDF indices `0–12` are pre-chapter/auxiliary pages.
* Chapters occupy PDF indices `13–390`.
* That gives exactly **378 chapter pages**, consistent with your earlier count.
* If the full PDF has 403 pages, indices `391–402` are also auxiliary/post-chapter pages.

So this mapping is internally consistent with the **378 chapter + 25 non-chapter = 403 total pages** structure you established earlier.
ME: did the deskewing really work?
AI: No. **In the screenshot, the deskewing appears to have made the page more tilted rather than correcting it.**

I checked the dominant near-horizontal line orientations in the screenshot. Approximately:

| Variant       | Estimated text-line angle |
| ------------- | ------------------------: |
| `bg_only`     |                 **−3.0°** |
| `crop_bg`     |                 **−2.9°** |
| `deskew_only` |                 **−6.0°** |

These values are approximate because I am measuring the plotted screenshot rather than the original page image, but the difference is large enough to identify the problem: **the deskew operation is rotating in the wrong direction**.

## Why this happened

In the notebook I gave you, the relevant code is:

```python
angle, _ = estimate_skew(gray)

matrix = cv2.getRotationMatrix2D(
    center,
    -angle,
    1.0,
)
```

But `estimate_skew()` already returns the image-coordinate line orientation. For this implementation, negating it is apparently applying the correction in the **same visual direction** as the existing skew.

For example:

```text
Detected skew ≈ -3°
Current correction = -(-3°) = +3°
Result ≈ -6°
```

which is almost exactly what we see.

---

# Fix

Change:

```python
matrix = cv2.getRotationMatrix2D(center, -angle, 1.0)
```

to:

```python
matrix = cv2.getRotationMatrix2D(center, angle, 1.0)
```

So the corrected basic function is:

```python
def deskew_gray(
    gray: np.ndarray,
    max_abs_angle: float = 4.0
) -> tuple[np.ndarray, float]:

    angle, _ = estimate_skew(gray)

    # Avoid rotating already-straight pages
    if abs(angle) < 0.15 or abs(angle) > max_abs_angle:
        return gray.copy(), 0.0

    h, w = gray.shape
    center = (w / 2.0, h / 2.0)

    matrix = cv2.getRotationMatrix2D(
        center,
        angle,       # IMPORTANT: do not negate here
        1.0,
    )

    rotated = cv2.warpAffine(
        gray,
        matrix,
        (w, h),
        flags=cv2.INTER_CUBIC,
        borderMode=cv2.BORDER_REPLICATE,
    )

    return rotated, float(angle)
```

## But I would make it safer than that

Since this is an EDA/preprocessing pipeline, the function should **verify that the operation actually reduced skew** rather than blindly trusting the estimator.

Use:

```python
def deskew_gray(
    gray: np.ndarray,
    max_abs_angle: float = 4.0,
    min_angle: float = 0.15,
) -> tuple[np.ndarray, float]:

    before_angle, _ = estimate_skew(gray)

    # Too small to matter or suspiciously large
    if abs(before_angle) < min_angle:
        return gray.copy(), 0.0

    if abs(before_angle) > max_abs_angle:
        return gray.copy(), 0.0

    h, w = gray.shape
    center = (w / 2.0, h / 2.0)

    matrix = cv2.getRotationMatrix2D(
        center,
        before_angle,
        1.0,
    )

    candidate = cv2.warpAffine(
        gray,
        matrix,
        (w, h),
        flags=cv2.INTER_CUBIC,
        borderMode=cv2.BORDER_REPLICATE,
    )

    # Verify that correction actually helped
    after_angle, _ = estimate_skew(candidate)

    if abs(after_angle) < abs(before_angle):
        return candidate, float(before_angle)

    # Fail safely: don't make the image worse
    return gray.copy(), 0.0
```

This gives you an important invariant:

[
|\theta_{\text{after}}| < |\theta_{\text{before}}|
]

or the deskew operation is rejected.

---

# Add a proper deskew diagnostic

I strongly recommend adding this to the notebook because otherwise the gallery can look plausible without proving anything.

```python
def evaluate_deskew(page_indices):
    rows = []

    for page_index in page_indices:
        bgr = get_page_bgr(doc, page_index)
        gray = cv2.cvtColor(bgr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY)

        before_angle, before_iqr = estimate_skew(gray)

        corrected, applied_angle = deskew_gray(gray)

        after_angle, after_iqr = estimate_skew(corrected)

        rows.append({
            "page_index": page_index,
            "page_id": f"p{page_index + 1:04d}",
            "before_angle": before_angle,
            "applied_angle": applied_angle,
            "after_angle": after_angle,
            "abs_before": abs(before_angle),
            "abs_after": abs(after_angle),
            "improvement": abs(before_angle) - abs(after_angle),
            "successful": abs(after_angle) < abs(before_angle),
        })

    return pd.DataFrame(rows)
```

Then:

```python
deskew_eval_df = evaluate_deskew(
    range(doc.page_count)
)

display(
    deskew_eval_df.describe()
)

print(
    "Deskew success rate:",
    deskew_eval_df["successful"].mean()
)
```

And plot:

```python
plt.figure(figsize=(7, 7))

plt.scatter(
    deskew_eval_df["before_angle"],
    deskew_eval_df["after_angle"],
    alpha=0.6
)

lim = max(
    deskew_eval_df["before_angle"].abs().max(),
    deskew_eval_df["after_angle"].abs().max()
)

plt.plot(
    [-lim, lim],
    [-lim, lim],
    "--",
    label="No improvement"
)

plt.axhline(0)
plt.axvline(0)

plt.xlabel("Skew before correction (degrees)")
plt.ylabel("Skew after correction (degrees)")
plt.title("Deskew validation")
plt.legend()

plt.show()
```

For successful deskewing, points should generally move **toward the horizontal `y=0` line**.

---

## One more improvement

I would also slightly revise `estimate_skew()`.

At the moment it uses Hough lines over most of the page. For this book, that means it can accidentally detect:

* page borders,
* gutter edges,
* figure edges,
* underlines,
* horizontal rules,

rather than text baselines.

A better approach is:

```text
page
 ↓
remove outer ~8–10%
 ↓
background normalize
 ↓
threshold
 ↓
morphologically connect characters horizontally
 ↓
find text-line components
 ↓
estimate line orientations
 ↓
robust median angle
```

That will make the estimator more specifically about **text skew** rather than arbitrary geometric lines.

### For the page in your screenshot

It appears to start around:

[
\theta_{\text{before}}\approx -3^\circ
]

so a successful result should look roughly like:

[
-3^\circ
\rightarrow
0^\circ
]

whereas your current output is approximately:

[
-3^\circ
\rightarrow
-6^\circ
]

So **yes, there is a real bug in the current deskew implementation, and I would fix it before trusting any of the downstream preprocessing-comparison results involving `deskew_*` variants.**

### 2. Installation / environment cell

```python
# ============================================================
# Optional Surya OCR setup check
# ============================================================

import importlib.util
import shutil

def surya_status():
    installed = importlib.util.find_spec("surya") is not None

    return {
        "surya_installed": installed,
        "docker_available": shutil.which("docker") is not None,
        "llama_server_available": shutil.which("llama-server") is not None,
    }


surya_env = surya_status()
print(surya_env)

if not surya_env["surya_installed"]:
    print(
        "\nSurya is not installed.\n"
        "Install with:\n\n"
        "    pip install surya-ocr\n"
    )
```

If needed on Kaggle:

```python
# Run only if Surya is not already installed.
# !pip install -q surya-ocr
```

There is an important difference from EasyOCR/Tesseract: current **Surya 2 uses a VLM inference backend**. The official documentation says it expects `vllm` for NVIDIA GPU inference or `llama.cpp` for CPU/Apple Silicon, and `SuryaInferenceManager` manages that backend. ([GitHub][1])

So on Kaggle, installation of `surya-ocr` alone may not be sufficient depending on whether its required backend can run in that environment.

### 3. Surya helper cell

This integrates directly with your existing `get_page_bgr()` and `preprocess_variant()` functions.

```python
# ============================================================
# Surya OCR helper
# ============================================================

from PIL import Image
from bs4 import BeautifulSoup


def numpy_to_pil(image: np.ndarray) -> Image.Image:
    """
    Convert an OpenCV/grayscale preprocessing result into PIL RGB,
    which Surya expects.
    """
    if image.ndim == 2:
        return Image.fromarray(image).convert("RGB")

    # OpenCV BGR -> RGB
    rgb = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB)
    return Image.fromarray(rgb)


def html_to_plain_text(html: str) -> str:
    """
    Surya v2 OCR blocks return HTML.
    Convert it conservatively to plain text for inspection/CER/WER.
    """
    if not html:
        return ""

    soup = BeautifulSoup(html, "html.parser")

    # Preserve basic block separation.
    return soup.get_text(separator="\n", strip=True)


def surya_result_to_text(page_result) -> str:
    """
    Convert a Surya v2 PageOCRResult into reading-order plain text.
    """
    blocks = sorted(
        page_result.blocks,
        key=lambda block: getattr(block, "reading_order", 0),
    )

    texts = []

    for block in blocks:
        if getattr(block, "skipped", False):
            continue

        if getattr(block, "error", False):
            continue

        html = getattr(block, "html", "")
        text = html_to_plain_text(html)

        if text:
            texts.append(text)

    return "\n".join(texts)


def surya_result_to_dataframe(page_result) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """
    Convert Surya block output to a convenient dataframe for EDA.
    """
    rows = []

    for block in page_result.blocks:
        rows.append({
            "reading_order": getattr(block, "reading_order", None),
            "label": getattr(block, "label", None),
            "raw_label": getattr(block, "raw_label", None),
            "confidence": getattr(block, "confidence", None),
            "bbox": getattr(block, "bbox", None),
            "skipped": getattr(block, "skipped", False),
            "error": getattr(block, "error", False),
            "html": getattr(block, "html", ""),
            "text": html_to_plain_text(
                getattr(block, "html", "")
            ),
        })

    if not rows:
        return pd.DataFrame()

    return (
        pd.DataFrame(rows)
        .sort_values("reading_order")
        .reset_index(drop=True)
    )
```

Surya v2's current schema uses OCR `blocks` rather than the older `text_lines`; each block includes HTML, layout label, reading order, bounding box and confidence. ([GitHub][1])

## 4. Initialize Surya once

Do **not** initialize the model inside the page loop.

```python
# ============================================================
# Initialize Surya OCR
# ============================================================

surya_manager = None
surya_recognition_predictor = None
surya_layout_predictor = None

if ENABLE_SURYA_PROBE:

    from surya.inference import SuryaInferenceManager
    from surya.recognition import RecognitionPredictor

    print("Initializing Surya inference manager...")

    surya_manager = SuryaInferenceManager()

    print("Loading Surya recognition predictor...")

    surya_recognition_predictor = RecognitionPredictor(
        surya_manager
    )

    if SURYA_USE_LAYOUT:
        from surya.layout import LayoutPredictor

        print("Loading Surya layout predictor...")

        surya_layout_predictor = LayoutPredictor(
            surya_manager
        )

    print("Surya initialized successfully.")

else:
    print(
        "Surya probe disabled. "
        "Set ENABLE_SURYA_PROBE=True to run it."
    )
```

This follows the current official Python API. ([GitHub][1])

---

# 5. Surya OCR function

```python
# ============================================================
# Surya OCR wrapper
# ============================================================

def surya_ocr(image: np.ndarray) -> tuple[str, object]:
    """
    Run Surya v2 OCR.

    Parameters
    ----------
    image:
        Grayscale or BGR NumPy image.

    Returns
    -------
    text:
        Plain-text reconstruction of OCR blocks.

    result:
        Native Surya PageOCRResult for detailed inspection.
    """

    if surya_recognition_predictor is None:
        raise RuntimeError(
            "Surya has not been initialized. "
            "Set ENABLE_SURYA_PROBE=True and run the initialization cell."
        )

    pil_image = numpy_to_pil(image)

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Option A: full-page OCR
    # --------------------------------------------------------
    if not SURYA_USE_LAYOUT:

        predictions = surya_recognition_predictor(
            [pil_image]
        )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Option B: layout-guided OCR
    # --------------------------------------------------------
    else:

        if surya_layout_predictor is None:
            raise RuntimeError(
                "SURYA_USE_LAYOUT=True but LayoutPredictor "
                "was not initialized."
            )

        layouts = surya_layout_predictor(
            [pil_image]
        )

        predictions = surya_recognition_predictor(
            [pil_image],
            layouts,
        )

    page_result = predictions[0]

    text = surya_result_to_text(
        page_result
    )

    return text, page_result
```

Surya officially supports both of these modes: full-page OCR by calling `RecognitionPredictor([image])`, and block OCR by first running `LayoutPredictor` and passing those layout results to the recognizer. ([GitHub][1])

For your initial OCR baseline, I would use:

```python
SURYA_USE_LAYOUT = False
```

Then later test:

```python
SURYA_USE_LAYOUT = True
```

as a **layout ablation**.

# 6. Probe sample — equivalent to your EasyOCR/Tesseract cell

```python
# ============================================================
# Surya OCR qualitative probe
# ============================================================

if ENABLE_SURYA_PROBE:

    # Prefer TRAIN pages for development experiments.
    if split_df is not None:

        probe_pool = split_df.loc[
            split_df["split"] == "train",
            "page_index"
        ].tolist()

    else:

        probe_pool = list(range(doc.page_count))

        print(
            "WARNING: chapter ranges are not configured.\n"
            "Surya results are descriptive only and should NOT "
            "be used for preprocessing/model selection."
        )

    rng = np.random.default_rng(SEED)

    probe_indices = sorted(
        rng.choice(
            probe_pool,
            size=min(
                SURYA_PROBE_SAMPLE_N,
                len(probe_pool)
            ),
            replace=False,
        ).tolist()
    )

    for page_index in probe_indices:

        print("=" * 100)
        print(
            f"PDF page {page_index + 1} | "
            f"page_id=p{page_index + 1:04d}"
        )

        # --------------------------------------------
        # Original source
        # --------------------------------------------
        bgr = get_page_bgr(
            doc,
            page_index
        )

        # Use the same preprocessing candidate used
        # in the other OCR probes for fair comparison.
        processed = preprocess_variant(
            bgr,
            "crop_deskew_bg"
        )

        # --------------------------------------------
        # OCR
        # --------------------------------------------
        text, result = surya_ocr(
            processed
        )

        print("\nSURYA OCR OUTPUT\n")
        print(text[:3000])

        # --------------------------------------------
        # Block-level inspection
        # --------------------------------------------
        block_df = surya_result_to_dataframe(
            result
        )

        print("\nBLOCK SUMMARY\n")

        if len(block_df):

            display(
                block_df[
                    [
                        "reading_order",
                        "label",
                        "confidence",
                        "skipped",
                        "error",
                        "text",
                    ]
                ].head(30)
            )

            if block_df["confidence"].notna().any():

                print(
                    "\nMean block confidence:",
                    block_df["confidence"].mean()
                )

                print(
                    "Median block confidence:",
                    block_df["confidence"].median()
                )

        else:

            print("No OCR blocks returned.")

else:

    print(
        "Surya probe disabled. "
        "Set ENABLE_SURYA_PROBE=True to run it."
    )
```

# 7. I would also add this visual block inspection

Surya gives you bounding boxes, which is useful because for this book you want to distinguish **recognition failure** from **layout failure**.

```python
# ============================================================
# Visualize Surya OCR blocks
# ============================================================

def visualize_surya_blocks(
    image: np.ndarray,
    page_result,
    min_confidence: float = 0.0,
):
    """
    Draw Surya block bounding boxes and confidence values.
    """

    if image.ndim == 2:
        vis = cv2.cvtColor(
            image,
            cv2.COLOR_GRAY2RGB
        )
    else:
        vis = cv2.cvtColor(
            image,
            cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB
        )

    fig, ax = plt.subplots(
        figsize=(10, 14)
    )

    ax.imshow(vis)

    for block in page_result.blocks:

        if getattr(block, "skipped", False):
            continue

        confidence = getattr(
            block,
            "confidence",
            None
        )

        if (
            confidence is not None
            and confidence < min_confidence
        ):
            continue

        bbox = getattr(
            block,
            "bbox",
            None
        )

        if bbox is None:
            continue

        x1, y1, x2, y2 = bbox

        rect = plt.Rectangle(
            (x1, y1),
            x2 - x1,
            y2 - y1,
            fill=False,
            linewidth=1,
        )

        ax.add_patch(rect)

        label = getattr(
            block,
            "label",
            "?"
        )

        conf_text = (
            f"{confidence:.2f}"
            if confidence is not None
            else "NA"
        )

        ax.text(
            x1,
            max(0, y1 - 3),
            f"{label} | {conf_text}",
            fontsize=7,
            backgroundcolor="white",
        )

    ax.axis("off")
    ax.set_title(
        "Surya OCR blocks"
    )

    plt.tight_layout()
    plt.show()
```

Then after any probe:

```python
visualize_surya_blocks(
    processed,
    result
)
```

This is especially valuable for *শস্যের রোগ* because you need to see whether Surya separately identifies headings, prose, figures/captions, etc., rather than evaluating only the final text string.

# 8. Add Surya to your preprocessing comparison

Once you have `TRAIN_DEV_LABELS_PATH`, you can use exactly the same CER/WER evaluation framework as Tesseract.

```python
# ============================================================
# Surya preprocessing comparison using TRAIN-DEV GT
# ============================================================

surya_variant_results_df = pd.DataFrame()

if (
    len(train_dev_labels_df)
    and ENABLE_SURYA_PROBE
):

    label_map = dict(
        zip(
            train_dev_labels_df["page_id"],
            train_dev_labels_df["text"],
        )
    )

    rows = []

    for page_id, gold in label_map.items():

        page_index = int(
            metrics_df.loc[
                metrics_df["page_id"] == page_id,
                "page_index"
            ].iloc[0]
        )

        bgr = get_page_bgr(
            doc,
            page_index
        )

        for variant in PREPROCESS_VARIANTS:

            processed = preprocess_variant(
                bgr,
                variant
            )

            pred, result = surya_ocr(
                processed
            )

            block_df = surya_result_to_dataframe(
                result
            )

            mean_confidence = (
                float(
                    block_df["confidence"]
                    .dropna()
                    .mean()
                )
                if (
                    len(block_df)
                    and block_df["confidence"].notna().any()
                )
                else np.nan
            )

            counts = script_counts(
                gold
            )

            rows.append({
                "page_id": page_id,
                "page_index": page_index,
                "ocr_engine": "surya",
                "variant": variant,

                "cer": cer(
                    pred,
                    gold
                ),

                "wer": wer(
                    pred,
                    gold
                ),

                "mean_confidence": mean_confidence,

                **counts,
            })

    surya_variant_results_df = pd.DataFrame(
        rows
    )

    surya_variant_results_df.to_csv(
        OUTPUT_DIR
        / "surya_preprocessing_train_dev_results.csv",
        index=False,
    )

    summary = (
        surya_variant_results_df
        .groupby("variant")
        .agg(
            pages=("page_id", "nunique"),
            median_cer=("cer", "median"),
            mean_cer=("cer", "mean"),
            median_wer=("wer", "median"),
            mean_wer=("wer", "mean"),
            mean_confidence=("mean_confidence", "mean"),
        )
        .sort_values(
            ["median_cer", "median_wer"]
        )
    )

    display(summary)

else:

    print(
        "Skipped.\n"
        "Requires:\n"
        "  1. TRAIN_DEV_LABELS_PATH\n"
        "  2. ENABLE_SURYA_PROBE=True"
    )
```

## One methodological caution

For your initial comparison, keep all three engines on the **same source representation**:

```text
Tesseract  ─┐
EasyOCR    ─┼─ same page + same preprocessing
Surya      ─┘
```

Then compare:

[
CER,\quad WER,\quad \text{runtime/page}
]

Do **not** initially give Surya its own special preprocessing while Tesseract/EasyOCR receive something else. Otherwise you are comparing pipelines rather than OCR engines.

After choosing a strong OCR family, separately optimize its preprocessing.

Also note that current Surya 2 is **not quite as lightweight to probe as EasyOCR**: it now uses an inference-manager/backend architecture and its OCR returns layout-aware HTML blocks. ([GitHub][1]) For Kaggle in particular, I would first verify that Surya's required inference backend works in the runtime before making it a mandatory EDA dependency.

ME: as we try out different packages over the timeline of the project are the uvlock and pyproject.toml files supposed to change?
AI: Yes—but only when you permanently adopt a dependency.

- If you merely experiment locally/Kaggle with a package, do not change either file yet.
- If your submitted code or committed notebook requires that package, update `pyproject.toml` and regenerate `uv.lock`; commit both together.
- Never manually edit `uv.lock`.

Use:

```bash
uv add <package>
```

For development-only tools:

```bash
uv add --dev <package>
```

This updates both files consistently. CI runs:

```bash
uv sync --frozen
```

so a changed `pyproject.toml` with an outdated `uv.lock` will fail.

For your project, avoid changing existing core package versions casually. Add only packages you actually need for the final reproducible pipeline. A Kaggle-only experiment can remain uncommitted or be documented as optional, but if `notebooks/eda.ipynb` depends on a new package, add it properly so teammates and graders can reproduce it.
ME: Is it possible to run surya ocr 2 on kaggle with required vllm setup?
AI: **Not reliably through Surya OCR 2’s normal Kaggle setup.**

Surya 2’s OCR/layout/table features require a running inference backend. For NVIDIA GPUs, its official setup expects **vLLM plus Docker and the NVIDIA Container Toolkit**; its automatic manager spawns that backend for you. [Surya README](https://github.com/datalab-to/surya/blob/master/README.md)

A normal Kaggle notebook gives you an NVIDIA GPU, but not the privileged Docker/NVIDIA-container environment Surya’s auto-spawn workflow expects. So this is likely to fail:

```python
SuryaInferenceManager()
```

because it attempts the backend lifecycle Surya documents around Docker.

There is a possible advanced workaround:

1. Run a compatible vLLM OpenAI-compatible server yourself.
2. Point Surya to it:

```bash
export SURYA_INFERENCE_BACKEND=vllm
export SURYA_INFERENCE_URL=http://localhost:8000/v1
```

Surya officially supports attaching to a pre-running server. [Surya inference-backend documentation](https://github.com/datalab-to/surya/blob/master/README.md)

However, manually installing and serving vLLM on Kaggle can be fragile due to Kaggle’s preinstalled CUDA/PyTorch/Python versions. It also depends on the allocated GPU:

- **T4 or newer:** potentially compatible; vLLM requires NVIDIA compute capability 7.5+.  
- **P100:** not compatible with current vLLM GPU requirements, because its compute capability is below 7.5. [vLLM GPU requirements](https://docs.vllm.ai/en/stable/getting_started/installation/gpu/)

AI: PaddleOCR is much easier than Surya 2 to run on Kaggle: it uses PaddlePaddle/CUDA directly, not a separate vLLM server or Docker backend. Your dual T4 setup is more than adequate for a normal PaddleOCR inference trial.

Current official PaddleOCR language tables do **not** list Bengali/Bangla (`bn`) in the supported PP-OCRv3 or PP-OCRv5 recognition languages. The project also has a recent request for Bengali support, which indicates it is not an established official option. [PaddleOCR supported-language table](https://github.com/PaddlePaddle/PaddleOCR/blob/main/docs/version3.x/pipeline_usage/PP-StructureV3.en.md), [Bengali support discussion](https://github.com/PaddlePaddle/PaddleOCR/discussions/17751)

So this is not safe:

```python
PaddleOCR(lang="bn")
```

It may raise an unsupported-language error, choose an unsuitable model, or perform poorly on Bangla conjuncts and matras.

A sensible role for PaddleOCR would be:

- **layout/structure experiment:** possibly useful for detecting text regions, tables, and reading order;
- **not your primary Bangla recognizer** unless you locate and validate a maintained Bengali-specific Paddle checkpoint;
- **not worth fine-tuning from scratch** for A2—the labelled data requirement would be far beyond your small held-out transcription set.

Recommended OCR comparison order:

1. **Tesseract with Bengali language data** — lightweight baseline.
2. **EasyOCR with `['bn']`** — easy Kaggle candidate for Bangla.
3. **Surya 2** — high-potential experimental candidate, but vLLM setup is risky.
4. A credible Hugging Face multilingual/Bangla OCR checkpoint — if you find one and can reproduce it.

Use PaddleOCR only as a quick baseline/layout experiment, record the result honestly, and choose the actual OCR model based on CER/WER against your validation transcriptions.

It is JSON-valid and its Python cells compile. It starts with three pages, saves independent `layout.jsonl` and `ocr_full_page.jsonl` audit outputs, times both stages, visualizes boxes, and reports layout coverage versus full-page OCR line counts.

## What the reference notebook does

- Installs the **CPU** PaddlePaddle wheel, so its reported device is `cpu`; it does not use Kaggle’s T4 GPUs.
- Loads `PP-DocLayout-S`, a semantic layout detector—not the OCR pipeline.
- Finds five arbitrary images under all of `/kaggle/input`, then selects `image_files[4]`.
- Runs layout prediction on `page-0232.jpg`.
- Its result contains two `text` regions:

  - `[136, 86, 771, 243]`, score `0.745`
  - `[144, 1029, 769, 1094]`, score `0.587`

Those are large semantic regions, not per-line OCR boxes. The overlay and `save_to_img()` cells only visualize/save that layout output.

Crucially, the notebook never creates `PaddleOCR(...)` or calls an OCR recognizer. So it detects layout only; it produces no transcription.

The final `DocumentLayoutDetector` wrapper adds little value and has a fragile non-dictionary branch: it would `extend()` a string character by character if `.json` were returned as a string.

## Will text outside layout boxes disappear from OCR?

Only if you explicitly crop to the layout boxes and OCR only those crops.

The reference notebook does not run OCR, so no text is being omitted yet. In the new notebook, OCR always receives the original page image. PaddleOCR therefore runs its own text detection/recognition across the whole page, independently of `PP-DocLayout-S`; missed layout regions remain eligible for OCR.

This distinction is important because the sample’s two layout boxes visibly cover only limited parts of the page. Treat those boxes as structure/audit evidence, not as the complete OCR input. PaddleOCR’s layout module identifies semantic regions and returns labels, confidence, and `[xmin, ymin, xmax, ymax]` coordinates. [Official layout-detection docs](https://paddlepaddle.github.io/PaddleOCR/main/en/version3.x/module_usage/layout_detection.html)

## Important Bangla limitation

Conventional PaddleOCR 3.2 does not list Bengali/Bangla as a supported recognition language. The notebook makes that explicit and uses `lang="en"` only as a diagnostic baseline—useful for layout and Latin terms, but not a credible final Bangla OCR system. The official supported-language list does not include `bn`. [PaddleOCR OCR pipeline documentation](https://paddlepaddle.github.io/PaddleOCR/main/en/version3.x/pipeline_usage/OCR.html)

Do not use its raw output to populate human ground-truth labels.

## Expected Kaggle runtime

After the initial package/model downloads:

| Setup | Estimated warm runtime/page |
|---|---:|
| T4 GPU, layout + conventional PaddleOCR | ~2–6 seconds |
| CPU, like the reference notebook | ~20–60 seconds |
| First run | add roughly 3–10 minutes for installation/model downloads |

For 57 held-out pages on GPU, expect roughly **2–6 minutes of inference** after setup; for all 403 pages, roughly **15–40 minutes**. Actual timing is written to `summary.csv` by the notebook, which is more trustworthy than estimates for your scans.
