2. considering the fact that we will not be keeping the figures, is it necessary for the OCR or layout detector results to include something like \<figure> in place to indicate the presence of a figure or is that unnecessary considering it adds little to no value?
AI: For **your specific RAG pipeline**, I would separate the **OCR representation used internally** from the **clean text used for embedding/retrieval**.

## 1. Plain text vs Markdown for OCR output

Use **structured Markdown as the persisted OCR output**, but do **not** embed the raw Markdown blindly.

The assignment explicitly separates layout detection, OCR, chunking, embedding, and indexing, so preserving some document structure between OCR and chunking is useful.  The codebase also treats layout regions such as text, heading, table, and figure separately before OCR/chunking. 

For your Bengali crop-disease book, headings and numbered subsections are semantically important. Pages such as the chapter-opening and symptom-classification pages clearly contain hierarchical headings, numbered sections, and lists, not just undifferentiated prose. 

So I recommend something like:

```md
# দ্বিতীয় অধ্যায়
## উদ্ভিদরোগ, রোগের লক্ষণ ও সাক্ষ্য-প্রমাণ

### ২.১ রোগের লক্ষণ
আমরা বিভিন্ন লক্ষণ ...

### ২.২ রোগের সাক্ষ্য-প্রমাণ
উদ্ভিদের রোগের লক্ষণ ছাড়াও ...
```

rather than:

```text
দ্বিতীয় অধ্যায় উদ্ভিদরোগ রোগের লক্ষণ ও সাক্ষ্য-প্রমাণ
২.১ রোগের লক্ষণ আমরা বিভিন্ন লক্ষণ ...
```

Markdown gives you useful structure for later **heading-aware or paragraph-aware chunking**, while remaining simple text.

However, your embedding input should normally be a cleaned textual representation such as:

```text
দ্বিতীয় অধ্যায় > উদ্ভিদরোগ, রোগের লক্ষণ ও সাক্ষ্য-প্রমাণ > রোগের লক্ষণ

আমরা বিভিন্ন লক্ষণ ...
```

In other words:

**page image → layout → OCR → structured Markdown/JSON → chunk extraction → clean text passed to embedder**

I would not treat `.md` itself as the formal data contract. Ideally each OCR region/chunk has metadata such as:

```json
{
  "page_id": 20,
  "region_type": "paragraph",
  "heading_path": [
    "দ্বিতীয় অধ্যায়",
    "উদ্ভিদরোগ, রোগের লক্ষণ ও সাক্ষ্য-প্রমাণ",
    "২.১ রোগের লক্ষণ"
  ],
  "text": "..."
}
```

and Markdown is the **human-readable persisted/export representation**.

So between your two choices:

**Markdown > plain `.txt` for storage**, provided the Markdown only captures meaningful textual structure.

---

## 2. Should you insert `<figure>` when figures are discarded?

For **your project, generally no**.

The specification says layout analysis detects regions including **text blocks, headings, figures, tables, columns, marginalia**, but it does not require every detected region to enter the retrieval text.  The codebase similarly says `layout.py` detects text/table/figure/heading regions; OCR then converts relevant regions into text chunks. 

If you have already decided that:

* figures will not be retained,
* figures will not be interpreted,
* figure captions are not useful or not present,
* answers will not depend on visual disease examples,

then inserting:

```text
<figure>
```

into the searchable corpus adds almost **zero semantic information**.

Worse, if many pages contain figures, you may create repetitive artificial tokens:

```text
...
<figure>
...
<figure>
...
```

These can slightly contaminate chunk text without improving retrieval.

### Better policy

Keep the fact that a figure existed as **metadata**, not retrieval text:

```json
{
  "page_id": 87,
  "has_figure": true,
  "figure_count": 1
}
```

while the text sent to the chunker remains:

```text
ধানের বাদামী দাগ রোগের লক্ষণ ...
```

rather than:

```text
ধানের বাদামী দাগ রোগের লক্ষণ ...

<figure>

রোগ নিয়ন্ত্রণের জন্য ...
```

That preserves provenance without polluting the text.

There is one important exception: **figure captions**.

If a page contains:

```text
চিত্র ১৬.২: ধানের বাদামী দাগ রোগের লক্ষণ
```

keep the caption if OCR can reliably extract it, because it contains meaningful searchable information even if the actual image is discarded.

A sensible rule is therefore:

> **Discard the figure pixels and `<figure>` placeholder, but preserve meaningful captions and retain figure presence/type in metadata.**

---

## Recommended representation for KrishiPath

For your corpus I would use this:

```text
Page image
   ↓
Layout detector
   ├── heading
   ├── paragraph
   ├── list
   ├── table
   └── figure
          ↓
       ignored for OCR text
       but recorded in metadata
   ↓
OCR
   ↓
Structured page representation
   ↓
Markdown export + JSON metadata
   ↓
normalization
   ↓
semantic/paragraph chunking
   ↓
embedding
```

For example:

```md
---
page_id: 20
source_page: 7
has_figure: false
---

# দ্বিতীয় অধ্যায়
## উদ্ভিদরোগ, রোগের লক্ষণ ও সাক্ষ্য-প্রমাণ

### ২.১ রোগের লক্ষণ

আমরা শস্যের নানাবিধ রোগ দেখতে পাই...
```

The YAML header is optional; the actual pipeline metadata should preferably remain in your `Page`/`Region`/`Chunk` objects rather than being parsed back from Markdown.

### Final recommendation

| Decision                     | Recommendation                                                            |
| ---------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| OCR output stored on disk    | **Structured Markdown**                                                   |
| Machine-level representation | **JSON/dataclass objects + metadata**                                     |
| Embedding input              | **Clean text, not raw Markdown syntax**                                   |
| Headings                     | **Keep**                                                                  |
| Numbered lists               | **Keep**                                                                  |
| Paragraph boundaries         | **Keep**                                                                  |
| Page numbers                 | Metadata, normally not embedded                                           |
| Figure image                 | **Discard**                                                               |
| `<figure>` token             | **Do not include**                                                        |
| Figure presence              | Metadata only                                                             |
| Figure caption               | **Keep if semantically meaningful**                                       |
| Tables                       | Do **not** treat like figures; preserve their textual content if relevant |

This also aligns well with the assignment's intended flow of **layout → OCR → chunk → embed → index**, rather than flattening the page immediately after OCR. 
ME: how much time would surya ocr 2 take when run locally on a 5060Ti through wsl a& docker per page and for the entire 403 page book. My teammate used it locally it took 3-5 minutes per page, is this reasonable or is there any room for optimization
AI: Yes. **3–5 minutes per page on an RTX 5060 Ti is much slower than I would expect for Surya OCR 2**, assuming the GPU is actually being used by vLLM and you are running full-page OCR efficiently. There is substantial room for optimization.

For your 403-page corpus, 3–5 min/page means roughly **20.2–33.6 hours**, with ~26.9 hours at 4 min/page. That runtime strongly suggests either a configuration problem or an inefficient inference path rather than a fundamental limitation of the 5060 Ti.

## What Surya 2 itself tells us

The current Surya 2 release is a **650M-parameter VLM** that handles OCR, layout, and table recognition. On NVIDIA GPUs, the intended backend is **vLLM through Docker/NVIDIA Container Toolkit**. The official benchmark reports **5.35 pages/s on an RTX 5090**, using 96-DPI pages, about 2,410 generated tokens/page, and concurrency 128. ([GitHub][1])

That does **not** mean a 5060 Ti will achieve anything close to 5.35 pages/s. The 5090 has 3,352 AI TOPS, 21,760 CUDA cores, 1,792 GB/s bandwidth, and 32 GB VRAM, whereas the 5060 Ti has 759 AI TOPS, 4,608 CUDA cores, 448 GB/s bandwidth, and either 8 or 16 GB VRAM. ([NVIDIA][2])

A naive hardware-ratio scaling would put a 5060 Ti at roughly 20–25% of the 5090's theoretical capability. But that is **not a reliable OCR benchmark**, because vLLM batching, KV-cache capacity, output length, image resolution, and concurrency matter enormously.

Still, it establishes something important:

> **3–5 minutes/page is not a normal target for a correctly accelerated 650M Surya 2 workload on a 5060 Ti.**

---

# What I would expect

There is no official Surya 2 benchmark for the 5060 Ti, so the following is an **engineering estimate**, not a published result.

### RTX 5060 Ti 16 GB

| Configuration                     |          Plausible effective rate |     403 pages |
| --------------------------------- | --------------------------------: | ------------: |
| Poorly optimized but GPU working  |                      30–60 s/page |    ~3.4–6.7 h |
| Reasonable sequential setup       |                     ~15–45 s/page |    ~1.7–5.0 h |
| Batched/continuous vLLM inference | ~5–15 s/page effective throughput |   ~34–101 min |
| Very well optimized at low DPI    |                potentially faster | possibly <1 h |

For the **8 GB 5060 Ti**, I would expect lower concurrency and therefore somewhat worse book-level throughput, but still not normally 3–5 min/page.

For your actual Bengali book, I would initially target approximately:

> **10–30 seconds effective time/page**, or roughly **1.1–3.4 hours for 403 pages**, before attempting more aggressive optimization.

Getting the whole book down toward **30–90 minutes** may be possible with effective batching and lower-resolution inputs, but I would not make that a requirement until you benchmark OCR quality.

Your uploaded book contains fairly dense Bengali prose, mixed Bengali/Latin scientific terminology, aged paper/background variation, and some faint show-through; pages such as the main text pages visible around pp. 14–35 are substantially denser than many clean modern benchmark pages.  So I would **not automatically force 96 DPI everywhere just for speed**.

---

# Why your teammate may be getting 3–5 minutes/page

There are several likely causes. I would investigate them in approximately this order.

## 1. They may be accidentally using **block-mode OCR**

This is probably the first thing I would check.

Surya 2 supports two approaches:

```python
predictions = recognition_predictor([image])
```

This is **full-page OCR**:

> one VLM request per page.

But this:

```python
layouts = layout_predictor([image])
predictions = recognition_predictor([image], layouts)
```

automatically switches recognition into **block mode**, where individual detected blocks are OCR'd separately. The Surya documentation explicitly distinguishes these paths. ([GitHub][3])

For a dense page like this book, suppose layout finds 15–30 blocks.

Instead of:

```text
1 page
 └─ 1 VLM generation
```

you can effectively end up doing:

```text
1 page
 ├─ layout VLM generation
 ├─ OCR block 1
 ├─ OCR block 2
 ├─ OCR block 3
 ...
 └─ OCR block N
```

That can transform tens of seconds into minutes.

### For your corpus

I would benchmark:

```text
A. Full-page Surya OCR
B. Layout + per-block Surya OCR
```

on the same 10 pages.

I strongly suspect **A will be dramatically faster**.

And importantly, Surya's full-page OCR result already returns `blocks` containing:

* block label,
* reading order,
* HTML,
* bbox/polygon,
* confidence.

So you may not need to pay for per-block recognition merely to obtain structured text. ([GitHub][3])

---

# 2. Verify that it really is Surya **2**

Run:

```bash
pip show surya-ocr
```

Surya 2 was introduced with **v0.20.0**. The project was substantially redesigned from the v1 architecture. ([GitHub][4])

If they are actually running something such as:

```text
0.17.x
```

then you are not benchmarking the current Surya 2 pipeline.

---

# 3. Verify that vLLM is actually using the 5060 Ti

During OCR, run:

```bash
watch -n 1 nvidia-smi
```

You want to see something broadly like:

```text
GPU-Util:   high
Memory:     several GB allocated
Process:    vLLM / python / container
```

If you see:

```text
GPU-Util: 0–5%
VRAM:     ~0 GB
```

while a page takes four minutes, that is the first problem to solve.

Surya 2's intended NVIDIA path is the vLLM backend using Docker and NVIDIA Container Toolkit. WSL2 officially supports CUDA workloads and GPU-accelerated Docker workloads, so **WSL + Docker itself should not explain a 10×–50× slowdown** when properly configured. ([Microsoft Learn][5])

Explicitly set:

```bash
export SURYA_INFERENCE_BACKEND=vllm
```

rather than relying on automatic backend detection while debugging.

---

# 4. They may be reloading the model for every page

This would be a major mistake.

Surya notes that the default CLI lifecycle starts the inference server and shuts it down afterward. The model-load/startup cost can therefore be repeatedly incurred if you invoke the command separately for every page. ([GitHub][1])

Bad:

```bash
for image in *.png; do
    surya_ocr "$image"
done
```

Potentially:

```text
load model
OCR page 1
shutdown

load model
OCR page 2
shutdown

load model
OCR page 3
shutdown
...
```

Much better:

```bash
surya_ocr /path/to/all/pages --keep_server
```

or process the complete list of images from one Python process using **one** `SuryaInferenceManager`.

For example conceptually:

```python
manager = SuryaInferenceManager()
predictor = RecognitionPredictor(manager)

predictions = predictor(images)
```

Do **not** recreate `manager` and `predictor` inside the page loop.

---

# 5. Increase concurrency

Surya's current default is:

```text
SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL=8
```

and its documentation specifically recommends increasing client concurrency and/or vLLM's:

```text
--max-num-seqs
--max-num-batched-tokens
```

to keep more pages in flight. ([GitHub][3])

For a **5060 Ti 16 GB**, I would benchmark:

```bash
SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL=2
SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL=4
SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL=8
SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL=12
```

until either:

* throughput stops improving,
* VRAM becomes problematic,
* or OOM occurs.

Do not optimize on **single-page latency** for the 403-page job. Optimize:

[
\text{Throughput} =
\frac{N_{\text{pages}}}
{t_{\text{total}}}
]

If 8 pages all finish in 80 seconds, your effective cost is:

[
80/8 = 10 \text{ s/page}
]

even if an individual page waited substantially longer.

---

# 6. DPI may be unnecessarily high

This is another major one.

The Surya developers explicitly say that DPI significantly affects throughput and recommend testing a reduction from:

```text
192 DPI
```

to:

```text
96 DPI
```

for higher throughput. They also recommend reducing overly large images to no more than roughly **2048 px width**. ([GitHub][3])

For your particular Bengali scans, I would test:

```text
96 DPI
128 DPI
144 DPI
192 DPI
```

on perhaps **10 representative pages**:

* clean text,
* dense text,
* faded text,
* mixed Bengali/English,
* page with figure/table,
* page with bleed-through.

Then measure OCR quality.

This is particularly important for your assignment: A2 explicitly expects **measured OCR quality against labelled pages**, rather than simply claiming that an OCR configuration looks good.  The codebase also places OCR evaluation against `grading_kit/labels.jsonl` in the required evaluation pipeline. 

You might discover something like:

| DPI |  CER |  Throughput |
| --: | ---: | ----------: |
|  96 | 7.8% | 0.30 page/s |
| 128 | 5.1% | 0.22 page/s |
| 144 | 4.9% | 0.18 page/s |
| 192 | 4.7% | 0.10 page/s |

Those numbers are illustrative, but if your actual result resembled this, **128/144 DPI would be a far more defensible engineering choice than 192 DPI**.

---

# 7. Do not run unnecessary Surya stages

For your RAG corpus, you said previously that figures themselves do not need to be preserved.

You therefore probably do **not** need:

```text
OCR
+ separate text detection
+ separate layout
+ table recognition
+ OCR again
```

for every page.

Surya 2's main VLM itself can produce structured full-page OCR including block labels and HTML. ([GitHub][1])

For your A2 architecture, you still need an explicit **layout stage**, because your assignment specifies:

```text
ingest → preprocess → layout → OCR → chunk → embed → index
```

and the Codebase Guide provides separate `vision/layout.py` and `vision/ocr.py` homes.  

But that doesn't necessarily mean:

> layout must force OCR into expensive per-block VLM inference.

You can keep:

```text
layout result
     ↓
metadata / regions / reading order

full-page OCR
     ↓
text
```

as separate logical pipeline stages without making every detected region an independent generative request.

---

# 8. Keep the files inside the WSL filesystem

For a 650M generative OCR model, filesystem I/O is unlikely to explain minutes/page, but I would still avoid processing hundreds of images directly from:

```text
/mnt/c/Users/...
```

if possible.

Put the working dataset somewhere like:

```text
~/krishipath/data/raw/
```

inside WSL and mount that into Docker.

That removes Windows↔WSL filesystem traversal from the page-loading path. It won't turn 5 minutes into 10 seconds by itself, but it removes unnecessary overhead.

---

# A diagnostic benchmark I recommend

Before processing all 403 pages, take **20 representative pages**.

Run four experiments:

| Experiment | OCR mode           | Resolution | Parallelism |
| ---------- | ------------------ | ---------: | ----------: |
| A          | full page          |    192 DPI |           1 |
| B          | full page          |    144 DPI |           4 |
| C          | full page          |     96 DPI |           8 |
| D          | layout → block OCR |    144 DPI |           4 |

For each record:

```text
total wall-clock time
pages/sec
mean seconds/page
peak VRAM
average GPU utilization
CER/WER or character-F1
```

This will immediately tell you where the problem is.

---

## The most important test

Have your teammate run one OCR batch while another terminal shows:

```bash
nvidia-smi
```

Then determine:

```text
Surya version:
5060 Ti VRAM: 8 GB or 16 GB
GPU utilization:
GPU VRAM usage:
SURYA_INFERENCE_BACKEND:
SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL:
input resolution/DPI:
full-page or block OCR:
number of pages sent per predictor call:
```

If you send me those **eight values**, I can tell you fairly precisely why they are seeing 3–5 minutes/page.

### My expectation for your project

I would **not accept 3–5 min/page as the final Surya 2 runtime** without investigating it. The most likely culprits are, in order:

1. **per-block OCR rather than full-page OCR,**
2. **restarting/loading the server/model for each page,**
3. **GPU/vLLM not actually being used correctly,**
4. **no batching/concurrency,**
5. **unnecessarily high DPI/resolution.**

Given the 5060 Ti and this book, getting from **~27 hours down to a few hours should be very realistic**, and getting toward **roughly 0.5–1.5 hours for the complete corpus may be feasible** with a 16 GB card and well-tuned continuous batching—provided your held-out Bengali OCR accuracy does not materially deteriorate. The latter is an optimization target, not a promised benchmark. ([GitHub][3])

If this ingestion is only performed once for building the knowledge base, I would prioritize **OCR quality first and throughput second**: choose the fastest configuration whose held-out CER/F1 is statistically indistinguishable, or acceptably close, to your high-resolution configuration. That gives you a defensible A2 design decision rather than simply chasing maximum pages/s.

At first, review the plan and let me know about what I should expect.
AI: ## Assessment of the plan

Using a **Qwen-VL model as the layout-analysis stage is a reasonable approach for your KrishiPath corpus**, especially because you want more than conventional text detection. Your target output is essentially:

[
\text{page image}
\rightarrow
{(\text{bbox},\text{semantic type},\text{reading order})_i}
]

rather than merely:

[
\text{page image}\rightarrow\text{text pixels}
]

That aligns well with the assignment. The specification explicitly requires Stage 2 to identify regions such as **text blocks, headings, figures, tables, columns, and marginalia**, and the starter code specifically expects `vision/layout.py → detect()` to return regions such as text/table/figure/heading.  

For your particular book, I would consider Qwen-VL a **strong experimental candidate**, but I would not treat its generated boxes as unquestionable ground truth.

---

# 1. What Qwen-VL can realistically give you

Modern Qwen VL models explicitly support visual grounding. Qwen2.5-VL can output bounding boxes/points and structured JSON, while Qwen3-VL improves its spatial grounding capabilities further. ([Qwen][1])

So something like this is realistic:

```json
{
  "page": "page_0042.png",
  "regions": [
    {
      "id": 0,
      "label": "header",
      "bbox": [92, 31, 465, 74]
    },
    {
      "id": 1,
      "label": "heading",
      "bbox": [138, 119, 420, 166]
    },
    {
      "id": 2,
      "label": "text",
      "bbox": [71, 190, 493, 823]
    },
    {
      "id": 3,
      "label": "figure",
      "bbox": [105, 845, 450, 1120]
    }
  ]
}
```

You could additionally request:

```json
{
  "reading_order": [0, 1, 2, 3]
}
```

or put an `order` field into every region.

That would be substantially more useful downstream than merely asking:

> "Find all text."

---

# 2. Your corpus is actually fairly favorable for this approach

Looking at *KrishiPath / শস্যের রোগ*, much of the corpus has a relatively regular structure:

* predominantly **single-column Bengali prose**;
* centered chapter headings;
* section/subsection headings;
* numbered lists;
* page headers and page numbers;
* Bengali text mixed with Latin scientific terminology;
* occasional larger non-text content/illustrations;
* front matter that differs substantially from the body;
* some bleed-through/background degradation.

For example, page 14 has a centered chapter title followed by one main text region, whereas pages 21–24 contain section headings, prose, and numbered symptom lists. The table-of-contents pages have a distinctly different layout from normal body pages. 

That is good news.

You do **not** have an extremely difficult newspaper-style 3–4-column document corpus where reading-order reconstruction is the central problem.

So I would expect most pages to fall into a few recurring templates:

```text
BODY PAGE
 ┌───────────────────────────┐
 │ page/header               │
 │                           │
 │ section heading           │
 │                           │
 │ main text                 │
 │ main text                 │
 │ main text                 │
 │                           │
 │ page number               │
 └───────────────────────────┘
```

with some:

```text
FIGURE PAGE
 ┌───────────────────────────┐
 │ heading                   │
 │ text                      │
 │                           │
 │        FIGURE             │
 │                           │
 │ figure caption            │
 │ text                      │
 └───────────────────────────┘
```

That makes your layout detector's job considerably easier.

---

# 3. Semantic tags are worth keeping

Earlier, for the final **OCR text passed into RAG**, I would not insist on retaining `<figure>` placeholders everywhere.

But that is different from the **layout stage**.

At layout detection time, I strongly recommend retaining semantic labels.

Use approximately:

```text
text
heading
figure
table
caption
list
header
footer
page_number
```

You could make the first version even simpler:

```text
text
heading
figure
table
other
```

The reason is architectural rather than cosmetic.

The assignment explicitly describes layout analysis as:

> detecting text blocks, headings, figures, tables, columns, etc.

and places it before OCR. 

Therefore the layout output should preserve that information even if later stages decide that figures should not enter the RAG index.

For example:

```text
Layout detector
       │
       ├── text ──────────────► OCR
       │
       ├── heading ───────────► OCR
       │
       ├── caption ───────────► OCR
       │
       ├── table ─────────────► table OCR / OCR
       │
       └── figure ────────────► SKIP
```

This is preferable to throwing the semantic information away immediately.

---

# 4. Where Qwen is especially attractive

A normal layout detector typically performs something like:

[
I \rightarrow {\text{bbox}_i,\text{class}_i,\text{confidence}_i}
]

Qwen instead has semantic reasoning available.

That potentially lets it distinguish:

```text
"this dark rectangular region is an illustration"
```

from:

```text
"this rectangular region contains Bengali text"
```

using broader page context.

Qwen2.5-VL was explicitly developed with document parsing and layout-position understanding, in addition to OCR and grounding. ([Hugging Face][2])

Qwen3-VL further improves spatial grounding and has an official 2-D grounding cookbook demonstrating object localization through bounding boxes. ([GitHub][3])

For **zero-shot layout detection**, this is therefore quite appealing.

---

# 5. But there is an important limitation

Do not confuse:

> **Qwen can output bounding boxes**

with:

> **Qwen is a deterministic object detector.**

These are not equivalent.

Qwen is fundamentally generating tokens describing coordinates.

So you may encounter:

### Missed regions

It might detect:

```text
heading
text block
figure
```

but miss a small caption.

### Merged regions

Two adjacent paragraphs may become one box.

This may actually be fine for your OCR pipeline.

### Over-segmentation

One text block may become:

```text
text block 1
text block 2
text block 3
```

when you wanted one region.

### Semantic inconsistency

The same thing could become:

```text
"title"
```

on one page and:

```text
"heading"
```

on another.

You therefore need a **closed class vocabulary in the prompt**.

### Coordinate problems

There have also been practical reports of coordinate scaling/resizing inconsistencies with Qwen2.5-VL grounding when the input image is internally resized. ([GitHub][4])

So coordinate normalization must be tested carefully rather than assumed.

---

# 6. This is the output format I would use

Do **not** ask Qwen to produce arbitrary prose.

Force something like:

```json
{
  "image_width": 550,
  "image_height": 1024,
  "regions": [
    {
      "id": 0,
      "type": "heading",
      "bbox": [141, 183, 411, 241],
      "reading_order": 0
    },
    {
      "id": 1,
      "type": "text",
      "bbox": [76, 282, 486, 905],
      "reading_order": 1
    }
  ]
}
```

Use:

[
[x_{\min},y_{\min},x_{\max},y_{\max}]
]

and enforce either:

### Option A — absolute pixel coordinates

```text
[76, 282, 486, 905]
```

or:

### Option B — normalized coordinates

[
x' = \frac{x}{W},\qquad y'=\frac{y}{H}
]

such as:

```text
[0.138, 0.275, 0.884, 0.884]
```

I actually prefer **normalized coordinates in your stored contract**, because page images may later be resized.

For example:

```json
"bbox_norm": [0.1382, 0.2754, 0.8836, 0.8838]
```

You can reconstruct pixels when necessary.

---

# 7. Don't ask Qwen to transcribe the text in this stage

This is an important design decision.

I would use Qwen only for:

```text
PAGE IMAGE
    │
    ▼
Qwen-VL
    │
    ├── bounding boxes
    ├── semantic classes
    └── reading order
```

and then pass the crops to your chosen OCR engine:

```text
bbox crop
   ↓
Surya OCR / OCR model
   ↓
Bengali transcription
```

rather than asking Qwen:

```text
detect + classify + OCR everything
```

in one generation.

Why?

Because your assignment explicitly separates:

> layout → OCR → chunking

and `vision/layout.py` and `vision/ocr.py` are distinct components.  

More importantly, separating them gives you independently measurable components:

[
\text{Layout quality}
]

versus

[
\text{OCR CER/WER}
]

instead of one opaque VLM output.

That will make your A2 analysis much stronger.

---

# 8. Qwen3-VL vs Qwen2.5-VL

Since you are implementing this **now**, I would test Qwen3-VL before defaulting to Qwen2.5-VL.

Qwen3-VL currently has dense 2B, 4B, 8B and 32B variants, among others, and the family specifically emphasizes improved spatial perception and grounding. ([arXiv][5])

For Kaggle, I would start with:

> **Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct**

and then test:

> **Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct**

if the available GPU memory permits it.

The 8B model is likely the more interesting quality point, but **you probably do not need a 30B+ model merely to locate text/figure/table regions in this book**.

Your pages are visually much simpler than arbitrary photographs or complex scientific PDFs.

The experiment should determine whether:

[
4B \approx 8B
]

for your specific layout classes.

If so, use 4B.

---

# 9. I would not immediately fine-tune it

Start zero-shot.

Your first experiment should be roughly:

[
20\text{–}30\text{ representative pages}
]

covering:

```text
5 normal prose
5 numbered/list-heavy
3 chapter opening pages
3 contents/front matter
3 figure-heavy
3 degraded/problematic pages
3 unusual pages
```

Manually annotate bounding boxes for those.

Then evaluate.

You may discover that zero-shot Qwen already performs well enough that fine-tuning adds little value.

That is preferable because the assignment wants you to **justify the model choice**, not necessarily train every model. The walkthrough specifically frames A2 as comparing alternatives, choosing one, reproducing a pretrained method, and reporting real performance. 

---

# 10. Measure layout quality independently

Don't judge it by looking at screenshots only.

For each ground-truth box (B_g) and predicted box (B_p), calculate:

[
IoU =
\frac{|B_p\cap B_g|}
{|B_p\cup B_g|}
]

For example:

[
IoU \geq 0.5
]

could count as a match.

Then report:

[
Precision =
\frac{TP}{TP+FP}
]

[
Recall =
\frac{TP}{TP+FN}
]

and perhaps:

[
F_1 =
\frac{2PR}{P+R}
]

for each class.

Example:

| Class   | Precision | Recall |   F1 |
| ------- | --------: | -----: | ---: |
| Text    |      0.96 |   0.98 | 0.97 |
| Heading |      0.91 |   0.88 | 0.89 |
| Figure  |      0.94 |   0.90 | 0.92 |
| Table   |      0.83 |   0.71 | 0.77 |

Those numbers are just an illustration—not expected results.

This gives you a defensible answer to:

> "Does our layout detector actually work?"

---

# 11. One issue I expect with your book: region granularity

This may actually become your biggest design question.

Consider a page like page 20:

```text
Chapter heading
       ↓
introductory paragraphs
       ↓
2.1 section heading
       ↓
several paragraphs
```

You could ask Qwen for:

### Line-level boxes

```text
hundreds of boxes/page
```

Bad choice for your pipeline.

### Paragraph-level boxes

```text
5–10 boxes/page
```

Potentially useful.

### Block-level boxes

```text
heading
body text
section heading
body text
```

Probably best.

For RAG/OCR, **block-level detection** is what I recommend.

Do not ask the VLM to box every line or every sentence.

Your OCR system can recover lines internally.

---

# 12. Figures should still be detected even if you discard them

Suppose the page is:

```text
┌───────────────────────┐
│ text                  │
│ text                  │
│                       │
│     plant photo       │
│                       │
│ caption               │
│ text                  │
└───────────────────────┘
```

If the layout detector knows:

```json
{"type":"figure"}
```

you can exclude that region before OCR.

That may actually **improve OCR**, because the recognizer no longer tries to interpret textures inside photographs as text.

So semantic figure detection has value even though:

[
\text{figure content} \not\rightarrow \text{RAG corpus}
]

---

# 13. Proposed architecture

For your current pipeline, I would structure it as:

```text
                    ORIGINAL PAGE
                          │
                          ▼
                Classical preprocessing
             deskew / crop / mild denoise
                          │
                          ▼
                    Qwen3-VL
                 layout grounding
                          │
             ┌────────────┼────────────┐
             │            │            │
           text        heading       figure
             │            │            │
             └─────┬──────┘            │
                   │                   SKIP
                   ▼
               crop region
                   │
                   ▼
               Surya OCR
                   │
                   ▼
             Unicode cleanup
                   │
                   ▼
           reading-order merge
                   │
                   ▼
               Markdown
                   │
                   ▼
                Chunker
                   │
                   ▼
                RAG KB
```

This fits the prescribed A2 sequence:

> ingest → enhance/preprocess → **layout → OCR → chunk → embed → index**. 

---

# 14. What I expect experimentally

For **your specific corpus**, I would expect the following qualitative behavior:

| Task                                           | Expected difficulty                          |
| ---------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| Detect main text block                         | **Easy**                                     |
| Detect chapter heading                         | **Easy**                                     |
| Separate header/footer                         | **Easy–moderate**                            |
| Detect figures                                 | **Easy–moderate**                            |
| Detect captions                                | **Moderate**                                 |
| Distinguish heading vs ordinary bold text      | **Moderate**                                 |
| Detect lists as separate semantic regions      | **Moderate**                                 |
| Reading order                                  | **Easy on most pages**                       |
| Table detection                                | **Depends on how many actual tables appear** |
| Pixel-perfect boxes                            | **Not guaranteed**                           |
| Stable class naming without schema enforcement | **Poor**                                     |
| Block-level regions with constrained JSON      | **Promising**                                |

The key point is that **you do not require pixel-perfect segmentation**.

Your real objective is:

[
\boxed{
\text{Does the detected crop contain all the text needed by OCR without mixing unrelated regions?}
}
]

That is much easier than conventional fine-grained document segmentation.

---

# 15. The one thing I would change in your current plan

Don't phrase the objective as:

> "Use Qwen-VL as my layout detector."

Phrase it experimentally as:

> **Evaluate Qwen3-VL as a zero-shot semantic layout detector and compare it against a conventional document-layout baseline.**

That gives you an actual model-selection experiment.

For example:

```text
Baseline:
Doc-layout detector
      vs.
Qwen3-VL-4B
      vs.
Qwen3-VL-8B
```

Evaluate approximately 20–30 annotated pages.

Then select the model based on:

[
\text{layout F1}
+
\text{OCR downstream quality}
+
\text{runtime}
+
\text{VRAM}
]

That is academically much stronger than choosing Qwen beforehand.

---

## Recommended initial configuration

I would therefore start with:

```text
Model:
    Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct

Inference:
    local Kaggle GPU
    bf16/fp16 where supported

Input:
    one page per request

Classes:
    heading
    text
    table
    figure
    caption
    header
    footer
    page_number
    other

Granularity:
    block-level

Output:
    strict JSON

BBox:
    normalized [x1, y1, x2, y2]

Additional output:
    reading_order

OCR text:
    DO NOT request here

Fine-tuning:
    none initially

Pilot:
    20–30 manually annotated representative pages

Evaluation:
    IoU@0.5
    precision / recall / F1 by class
    downstream OCR CER/WER
    inference time/page
```

### Bottom line

**The approach is technically sound and particularly well suited to your corpus, but Qwen should be treated as a semantic grounding model rather than a classical deterministic detector.** Qwen's documented grounding support makes the idea viable, and Qwen3-VL is worth testing before Qwen2.5-VL because of its stronger spatial capabilities. ([GitHub][3])

The most important design choices are **block-level boxes, a fixed semantic vocabulary, strict JSON, coordinate normalization, independent OCR, and a small manually annotated layout test set**. With those in place, this becomes a credible A2 layout-analysis experiment rather than simply another VLM call. 

The next useful step would be to design the **exact JSON contract + Qwen prompt + Kaggle inference/evaluation pipeline**, including visualization of predicted boxes over the page and IoU-based scoring.

[1]: https://qwenlm.github.io/blog/qwen2.5-vl/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Qwen2.5 VL! Qwen2.5 VL! Qwen2.5 VL!"
[2]: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Qwen/Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct"
[3]: https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen3-VL/blob/main/cookbooks/2d_grounding.ipynb?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Qwen3-VL/cookbooks/2d_grounding.ipynb at main"
[4]: https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen3-VL/issues/1030?utm_source=chatgpt.com "OCR Cookbook BBox Issues With Api/Local qwen2.5-vl- ..."
[5]: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21631?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Qwen3-VL Technical Report"
ME: what about docling or the lyout analysis of surya ocr itself?
AI: Yes. For **your specific goal—semantic page layout with bounding boxes—Surya’s own layout analysis and Docling are both more natural first-line candidates than Qwen-VL**.

The distinction is important: Qwen-VL is a general-purpose VLM that *can be prompted* to perform grounding, whereas Surya and Docling have dedicated document-layout machinery.

## Short recommendation

For your KrishiPath pipeline, I would test them in this order:

| Candidate                | Role                                                   | My expectation for your case                                    |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Surya 2 layout**       | Dedicated OCR/layout model                             | **Best first choice**                                           |
| **Docling layout model** | Dedicated document-layout detector + structured parser | **Strong baseline / comparator**                                |
| **Qwen3-VL**             | Generative semantic grounding                          | Useful experiment, but probably unnecessary as primary detector |

The assignment explicitly expects a standalone layout stage producing text/table/figure/heading regions before OCR, so either Surya or Docling fits the intended architecture directly.  

---

# 1. Surya is much more relevant than it may initially seem

The current **Surya 2** is not merely an OCR recognizer. Its official project describes a single **650M-parameter VLM** handling:

* OCR
* layout analysis
* reading order
* table recognition

with text detection and OCR-error detection handled separately. ([GitHub][1])

The current documentation explicitly states that layout analysis includes semantic elements such as tables, images, headers, etc., together with reading order. ([GitHub][2])

So Surya can essentially give you:

[
I
\rightarrow
{B_i,; C_i,; O_i}
]

where

* (B_i) = bounding box,
* (C_i) = semantic class,
* (O_i) = reading order.

That is almost exactly what you need.

---

# 2. Surya potentially lets you reuse the same model stack

This is a major advantage.

Your current conceptual pipeline was:

```text
Page
 ↓
Qwen layout
 ↓
crop regions
 ↓
Surya OCR
 ↓
text
```

You could instead have:

```text
Page
 ↓
Surya
 ├── layout
 ├── reading order
 └── OCR
 ↓
structured page
```

Or, preferably for your assignment:

```text
Page
 ↓
Surya layout
 ↓
regions
 ↓
Surya OCR
 ↓
text
```

You still keep the stages logically separate even though they use related model infrastructure.

That is cleaner operationally.

---

# 3. It also addresses the speed issue we discussed earlier

You mentioned that your teammate was seeing roughly **3–5 minutes per page** with Surya locally.

That is nowhere near what the current Surya 2 design is intended to achieve under an optimized GPU inference setup.

The official repository reports a headline OCR throughput of around **5 pages/s on an RTX 5090**, though that benchmark should not be interpreted as what you will necessarily see on Kaggle or when running the full layout + OCR pipeline. ([GitHub][2])

More importantly, Surya 2's current architecture is a single ~650M model rather than a huge general VLM. ([GitHub][3])

Therefore, comparing it to something like Qwen3-VL-8B:

[
650M \ll 8B
]

You should expect a substantial computational advantage.

---

# 4. Why I would currently favor Surya for KrishiPath

Your pages are structurally fairly conventional.

Most of the examples in the book are:

```text
page number/header

       centered chapter heading

paragraph
paragraph
paragraph

subheading

paragraph
paragraph
```

with occasional figures, lists, contents pages, etc. 

You do not need Qwen's high-level visual reasoning just to identify those regions.

Surya's layout model is specifically built for document elements, making it a better inductive fit.

Conceptually:

### Qwen

[
\text{general multimodal reasoning}
\rightarrow
\text{prompted layout grounding}
]

### Surya

[
\text{document understanding}
\rightarrow
\text{layout structure}
]

For this problem, the second is preferable unless experiments prove otherwise.

---

# 5. What about Docling?

Docling is also a strong option, but it occupies a somewhat different level of abstraction.

Docling is fundamentally a **document conversion framework** rather than just one detector. It converts inputs including PDF and page images into its structured `DoclingDocument` representation. ([GitHub][4])

Its structured output can preserve things such as:

* layout geometry,
* text blocks,
* headings,
* tables,
* figures,
* reading structure,
* document hierarchy.

This makes it extremely convenient for RAG.

---

# 6. Docling's layout detector is a real detector, not an LLM prompt

Historically, Docling used dedicated layout models trained around **DocLayNet**, together with TableFormer for table structure. ([arXiv][5])

More recent Docling work has introduced improved object-detection-based layout models built using architectures including:

* RT-DETR,
* RT-DETRv2,
* D-FINE.

Their 2025 technical report describes training on approximately **150,000 documents**. The strongest reported model, `heron-101`, reached around **78% mAP** on their evaluation while reporting 28 ms/image on an A100. ([arXiv][6])

Again, your Kaggle runtime will not equal that A100 benchmark, but architecturally it matters:

Docling does:

[
\text{pixels}
\rightarrow
\text{object detector}
\rightarrow
\text{layout objects}
]

rather than asking an autoregressive VLM to emit coordinates.

That generally gives you more stable geometry.

---

# 7. Qwen vs Docling vs Surya

This is the comparison I would actually use for your project.

| Property                     | Surya 2                   | Docling                           | Qwen3-VL                                  |
| ---------------------------- | ------------------------- | --------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
| Dedicated document layout    | **Yes**                   | **Yes**                           | No                                        |
| Semantic boxes               | **Yes**                   | **Yes**                           | Yes                                       |
| Reading order                | **Yes**                   | **Yes**                           | Can infer                                 |
| OCR built in                 | **Yes**                   | OCR pipeline available            | Yes, generative                           |
| Bengali OCR                  | **Strong reason to test** | Depends on configured OCR backend | Good VLM capability but not dedicated OCR |
| Tables                       | **Yes**                   | **Very strong tooling**           | Semantic understanding                    |
| Figures                      | Yes                       | Yes                               | Yes                                       |
| Deterministic output         | Better                    | **Better**                        | Worse                                     |
| Strict region geometry       | Good                      | **Likely strongest**              | Potentially variable                      |
| Runtime/model size           | **Excellent**             | Excellent/good                    | Heavier                                   |
| RAG-ready document structure | Good                      | **Excellent**                     | Requires custom processing                |
| Easy custom semantics        | Moderate                  | Moderate                          | **Excellent**                             |
| Hallucination risk           | Low                       | **Low**                           | Highest                                   |
| Kaggle suitability           | **Very good**             | Very good                         | Good                                      |
| One unified OCR/layout stack | **Yes**                   | Pipeline-based                    | Yes                                       |
| Best role in your experiment | **Primary candidate**     | Baseline/comparator               | Experimental candidate                    |

---

# 8. There is one reason Docling may be overkill

You are **not trying to convert arbitrary enterprise PDFs into perfect Markdown**.

You have a controlled corpus of scanned Bengali books.

Your assignment also wants explicit stages:

[
\text{layout}
\rightarrow
\text{OCR}
\rightarrow
\text{chunk}
]

where `vision/layout.py` and `vision/ocr.py` are separate modules. 

Docling can perform much more of the complete transformation for you:

```text
PDF
 ↓
Docling
 ↓
structured document
 ↓
Markdown / JSON / etc.
```

That is convenient in production, but academically it can obscure which component is responsible for what.

You do **not** want your A2 explanation to essentially become:

> "We passed the book to Docling."

You need to demonstrate and evaluate your layout and OCR decisions independently.

---

# 9. But the Docling layout model alone is attractive

You do not necessarily need to adopt the whole Docling pipeline.

You could conceptually use:

```text
Page
 ↓
Docling layout detector
 ↓
regions
 ↓
Surya OCR
```

This is actually a very strong experiment.

It isolates:

### Layout

Docling

### Recognition

Surya

Then you can measure:

[
F1_{layout}
]

and

[
CER_{OCR}
]

independently.

---

# 10. Surya gives an even cleaner experimental setup

Because Surya itself supports layout analysis, you can run:

### Pipeline A

```text
Surya Layout
    ↓
Surya OCR
```

### Pipeline B

```text
Docling Layout
    ↓
Surya OCR
```

### Pipeline C

```text
Qwen3-VL Layout
    ↓
Surya OCR
```

Now OCR is held constant.

That means any difference in final OCR quality is largely attributable to layout.

This is a much better experimental design.

Formally, you are controlling the recognition model:

[
OCR = \text{Surya}
]

while varying:

[
L \in
{
L_{\text{Surya}},
L_{\text{Docling}},
L_{\text{Qwen}}
}
]

Then evaluate:

[
CER(OCR(L(I)))
]

as well as direct layout metrics.

That's a proper ablation/comparison.

---

# 11. I'd actually reduce it to two models initially

You don't need to benchmark everything from day one.

First:

[
\boxed{\text{Surya Layout vs Docling Layout}}
]

Run them on about 20 representative pages.

Why these two?

Because they are both specialized detectors.

If one clearly works:

```text
Surya    F1 ~ high
Docling  F1 ~ high
```

and both handle all your page types, there is little reason to incur the complexity of Qwen.

Only add Qwen if you observe something like:

```text
Figure vs text confusion
caption confusion
unusual botanical illustrations
complex semantic regions
```

that specialized detectors mishandle.

---

# 12. For your actual corpus, my prediction

This is an **expectation**, not a measured result.

For simple body pages such as those visible throughout KrishiPath:

### Text region

I would expect:

[
Surya \approx Docling \approx Qwen
]

### Headings

Likely:

[
Docling \approx Surya \ge Qwen
]

because dedicated layout datasets explicitly encode document classes.

### Bounding-box precision

Likely:

[
Docling \gtrsim Surya > Qwen
]

### Bengali-specific OCR downstream compatibility

Likely:

[
Surya\ layout + Surya\ OCR
]

will be operationally simplest.

### Weird semantic interpretation

Potentially:

[
Qwen > Surya \approx Docling
]

but your corpus does not appear to require much of that.

---

# 13. Another major advantage: reading order

For your RAG pipeline, reading order may actually matter more than exact box precision.

Suppose:

```text
Heading

paragraph A

paragraph B

Figure

caption

paragraph C
```

If the boxes are geometrically correct but assembled as:

```text
paragraph B
caption
heading
paragraph A
paragraph C
```

your Markdown and chunking become poor.

Surya explicitly provides **reading-order detection**. ([GitHub][1])

Docling also builds structured document representations intended to preserve layout and document structure. ([GitHub][4])

This makes both significantly more convenient than implementing reading order yourself from Qwen coordinates.

---

# 14. Be careful about Surya version numbers

This point matters given your teammate's earlier runtime experience.

**Surya 2 was released as a major redesign with breaking API/output changes.** The official release notes describe it as a ground-up rework and explicitly warn about schema/API changes relative to v1. ([GitHub][3])

Therefore make sure your teammate was actually using:

> **Surya 2 / current Surya**

rather than following an older Surya tutorial or API.

That could explain some of the strange performance you're seeing.

---

# 15. What I would choose now

My current architecture recommendation would change from the previous message.

I would now put:

[
\boxed{\textbf{Surya 2 Layout}}
]

as your **primary implementation candidate**.

Use:

```text
Scanned page
      │
      ▼
mild preprocessing
      │
      ▼
Surya Layout
      │
      ├── text
      ├── title / heading
      ├── figure
      ├── table
      ├── caption
      ├── header/footer
      └── reading order
      │
      ▼
filter wanted regions
      │
      ▼
Surya OCR
      │
      ▼
Unicode/text normalization
      │
      ▼
structure-aware Markdown
      │
      ▼
chunking
```

Then test **Docling Layout** as your main alternative.

Qwen becomes the third candidate, not the default.

---

# 16. The experiment I recommend for A2

Take **20–30 pages** covering your corpus variation.

Manually annotate:

```json
{
  "regions": [
    {
      "type": "heading",
      "bbox": [...]
    },
    {
      "type": "text",
      "bbox": [...]
    }
  ]
}
```

Then run:

```text
                 ┌── Surya Layout ───┐
page ────────────┼── Docling Layout ─┼──► evaluation
                 └── Qwen (optional) ┘
```

Evaluate:

### Detector metrics

[
IoU@0.5
]

[
Precision,\ Recall,\ F1
]

### Structural metrics

* reading-order accuracy,
* missed text regions,
* false text regions,
* heading detection.

### Downstream metric

Run **the same Surya OCR model** on each detector's regions:

[
CER_{Surya-layout}
]

versus

[
CER_{Docling-layout}
]

This last metric is particularly useful because your ultimate objective is not object detection for its own sake.

It is:

[
\boxed{\text{good layout} \Rightarrow \text{better document text for RAG}}
]

---

## My current ranking for KrishiPath

**1. Surya 2 layout + Surya OCR**
Most sensible starting point. Small model, multilingual, layout + reading order + OCR, and minimal integration complexity. ([GitHub][2])

**2. Docling layout + Surya OCR**
Probably the strongest dedicated-layout comparison. Docling's latest layout stack is backed by dedicated object-detection architectures and extensive document-layout training. ([arXiv][6])

**3. Qwen3-VL layout + Surya OCR**
Useful only if you find that semantic understanding handles unusual regions better than dedicated detectors.

For this corpus, **I would not start by putting an 8B Qwen model in front of a 650M Surya OCR system when Surya itself already provides the layout capability you need**. First find out whether Surya's built-in layout model already solves the problem. If it does, the simplest architecture is also likely the best one.

I can next examine the **actual output schema and Python APIs of current Surya 2 and Docling**, and design a Kaggle benchmark that runs both on the same pages and draws their boxes side-by-side.

[1]: https://github.com/datalab-to/surya?utm_source=chatgpt.com "datalab-to/surya: OCR, layout analysis, reading order, table ..."

ME: my teammate is already trying out surya layout, give me kaggle ready scripts to try out docling and qwen 3 vl. I want to test out both. The expected output is the bounding box alongside semantic tags/classes.
AI: Yes. Since your teammate is already covering **Surya layout**, I would make your comparison deliberately clean:

* **Docling**: dedicated document-layout pipeline, deterministic structured elements.
* **Qwen3-VL**: zero-shot VLM grounding with a constrained semantic vocabulary.
* Both should write the **same normalized JSONL schema**, so you can compare them directly.

Docling supports image inputs and represents document elements with semantic labels plus provenance containing page number and bounding-box geometry. ([Docling Project][1]) Qwen3-VL officially supports spatial grounding, and the 4B model has a current Transformers interface suitable for Kaggle. ([GitHub][2])

I recommend starting with **Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct**, not 8B. If 4B is clearly inadequate, change one model-name constant and rerun with 8B.

## Common expected output

Both scripts below produce records conceptually like:

```json
{
  "image": "page_014.png",
  "width": 550,
  "height": 1024,
  "regions": [
    {
      "id": 0,
      "label": "heading",
      "bbox": [142, 184, 411, 241],
      "bbox_norm": [0.2582, 0.1797, 0.7473, 0.2354],
      "reading_order": 0
    },
    {
      "id": 1,
      "label": "text",
      "bbox": [74, 279, 487, 904],
      "bbox_norm": [0.1345, 0.2725, 0.8855, 0.8828],
      "reading_order": 1
    }
  ]
}
```

Use this as the contract between your experiment and `vision/layout.py`.

# A. Docling layout benchmark

Docling is a full document-conversion framework, so this script extracts only the information you care about:

[
\text{semantic label} + \text{bbox} + \text{reading order}
]

Docling's `ProvenanceItem` stores `page_no` and a bounding box with `l,t,r,b`, and coordinates can have either top-left or bottom-left origin. ([Docling Project][3]) The conversion below explicitly converts everything to conventional image coordinates:

[
(x_1,y_1,x_2,y_2)
]

with origin at the **top-left**.

### Cell 1 — install

```python
!pip install -q -U docling pillow tqdm matplotlib pandas
```

Restart the Kaggle kernel after installation if Kaggle reports package-version conflicts.

### Cell 3 — imports and helper functions

```python
import json
import traceback
from pathlib import Path

from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
from tqdm.auto import tqdm

from docling.document_converter import DocumentConverter
```

```python
# ============================================================
# LABEL NORMALIZATION
# ============================================================

def normalize_docling_label(label):
    """
    Map Docling's relatively rich semantic classes into the
    common vocabulary used for our layout comparison.
    """

    raw = str(label).lower()

    # Some enum string representations look like:
    # DocItemLabel.TEXT
    if "." in raw:
        raw = raw.split(".")[-1]

    mapping = {
        # body text
        "text": "text",
        "paragraph": "text",

        # headings / titles
        "title": "heading",
        "section_header": "heading",
        "heading": "heading",

        # list structures
        "list_item": "list",
        "list": "list",

        # tables
        "table": "table",

        # figures / pictures
        "picture": "figure",
        "figure": "figure",

        # captions
        "caption": "caption",

        # page furniture
        "page_header": "header",
        "header": "header",

        "page_footer": "footer",
        "footer": "footer",

        # equations
        "formula": "formula",
        "equation": "formula",

        # code
        "code": "code",

        # other
        "footnote": "footnote",
    }

    return mapping.get(raw, raw)
```

### Cell 4 — bounding-box conversion

This is worth doing carefully because Docling supports both coordinate origins. Its API explicitly represents `TOPLEFT` and `BOTTOMLEFT`. ([Docling Project][3])

```python
def docling_bbox_to_pixels(bbox, image_width, image_height):
    """
    Convert a Docling BoundingBox to:

        [x1, y1, x2, y2]

    using TOP-LEFT image coordinates.

    Parameters
    ----------
    bbox:
        Docling BoundingBox.
    image_width:
        Original image width in pixels.
    image_height:
        Original image height in pixels.
    """

    x1 = float(bbox.l)
    x2 = float(bbox.r)

    origin = str(bbox.coord_origin).upper()

    if "BOTTOMLEFT" in origin:
        # Docling PDF-style geometry:
        # bottom and top measured upward from bottom edge.
        y1 = image_height - float(bbox.t)
        y2 = image_height - float(bbox.b)

    else:
        # conventional top-left image geometry
        y1 = float(bbox.t)
        y2 = float(bbox.b)

    # Enforce ordering.
    x1, x2 = sorted((x1, x2))
    y1, y2 = sorted((y1, y2))

    # Clip to image
    x1 = max(0.0, min(x1, image_width))
    x2 = max(0.0, min(x2, image_width))
    y1 = max(0.0, min(y1, image_height))
    y2 = max(0.0, min(y2, image_height))

    return [x1, y1, x2, y2]
```

```python
def normalize_bbox(bbox, width, height):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = bbox

    return [
        x1 / width,
        y1 / height,
        x2 / width,
        y2 / height,
    ]
```

---

# Important Docling issue: coordinate scale

For image input, provenance geometry should correspond to the source page, but I recommend checking actual values against image dimensions rather than blindly assuming this.

The following helper detects obviously differently scaled coordinates:

```python
def maybe_rescale_bbox(bbox, page_width, page_height, image_width, image_height):
    """
    If Docling page geometry differs from actual image geometry,
    scale the bbox into the original image coordinate system.
    """

    if not page_width or not page_height:
        return bbox

    sx = image_width / float(page_width)
    sy = image_height / float(page_height)

    x1, y1, x2, y2 = bbox

    return [
        x1 * sx,
        y1 * sy,
        x2 * sx,
        y2 * sy,
    ]
```

Docling itself uses bbox scaling when transferring coordinates between document page sizes and rendered page images, so keeping this explicit is the safer approach. ([GitHub][4])

---

# Cell 5 — Docling extraction

```python
converter = DocumentConverter()
```

```python
def process_docling_image(image_path):
    """
    Run Docling on one image and return semantic layout regions.
    """

    image_path = Path(image_path)

    with Image.open(image_path) as im:
        image_width, image_height = im.size

    result = converter.convert(str(image_path))

    doc = result.document

    # Image documents should normally have one page.
    # Determine Docling's internal page dimensions when available.
    page_sizes = {}

    try:
        for page_no, page in doc.pages.items():
            page_sizes[int(page_no)] = (
                float(page.size.width),
                float(page.size.height)
            )
    except Exception:
        pass

    regions = []

    # iterate_items() follows document structure / reading order.
    for item, level in doc.iterate_items():

        provenance = getattr(item, "prov", None)

        if not provenance:
            continue

        label_raw = getattr(item, "label", "other")
        label = normalize_docling_label(label_raw)

        for prov in provenance:

            bbox_obj = getattr(prov, "bbox", None)

            if bbox_obj is None:
                continue

            page_no = int(getattr(prov, "page_no", 1))

            bbox = docling_bbox_to_pixels(
                bbox_obj,
                image_width=image_width,
                image_height=image_height,
            )

            # If Docling uses a page geometry different from image pixels,
            # rescale.
            if page_no in page_sizes:
                page_w, page_h = page_sizes[page_no]

                # Only rescale if dimensions are meaningfully different.
                if (
                    abs(page_w - image_width) > 2
                    or abs(page_h - image_height) > 2
                ):
                    bbox = maybe_rescale_bbox(
                        bbox,
                        page_width=page_w,
                        page_height=page_h,
                        image_width=image_width,
                        image_height=image_height,
                    )

            # Skip invalid / zero-area boxes
            x1, y1, x2, y2 = bbox

            if x2 <= x1 or y2 <= y1:
                continue

            region = {
                "id": len(regions),
                "label": label,
                "label_raw": str(label_raw),
                "bbox": [round(v, 2) for v in bbox],
                "bbox_norm": [
                    round(v, 6)
                    for v in normalize_bbox(
                        bbox,
                        image_width,
                        image_height
                    )
                ],
                "reading_order": len(regions),
                "page_no": page_no,
            }

            regions.append(region)

    return {
        "image": image_path.name,
        "width": image_width,
        "height": image_height,
        "model": "docling",
        "regions": regions,
    }
```

Docling's structured representation stores components and their geometry, and its traversal corresponds to document ordering, making this considerably more deterministic than asking a VLM to invent reading-order numbers. ([Docling Project][3])

---

# Cell 6 — visualization

```python
def visualize_layout(image_path, prediction, output_path):
    image = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
    draw = ImageDraw.Draw(image)

    for region in prediction["regions"]:
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = region["bbox"]

        draw.rectangle(
            [x1, y1, x2, y2],
            outline="red",
            width=3,
        )

        text = f'{region["reading_order"]}: {region["label"]}'

        draw.text(
            (x1 + 3, max(0, y1 - 15)),
            text,
            fill="red",
        )

    image.save(output_path)
```

For experimentation, colors are irrelevant; if you want later, you can assign one color per semantic class.

print("Saved:", JSONL_PATH)

# B. Qwen3-VL layout detector

For Qwen, I recommend **4B-Instruct first**:

```text
Qwen/Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct
```

Its current official model card supports the Transformers Qwen3-VL class and recommends recent Transformers builds. ([Hugging Face][5])

Qwen3-VL also has an official 2-D grounding cookbook. ([GitHub][2])

## Critical coordinate convention

Qwen3-VL grounding uses a **0–1000 normalized coordinate space** in its grounding setup rather than necessarily returning original-image pixels. This behavior has also been discussed in the project's grounding issues. ([GitHub][6])

Therefore we will explicitly tell the model:

```text
All coordinates MUST be integers between 0 and 1000.
```

and convert:

[
x_{pixel}=x_{qwen}\frac{W}{1000}
]

[
y_{pixel}=y_{qwen}\frac{H}{1000}
]

This avoids confusion caused by model-side image resizing.

---

# Cell 4 — fixed layout vocabulary and prompt

This part is important.

Do **not** let Qwen invent arbitrary class names.

```python
LAYOUT_CLASSES = [
    "text",
    "heading",
    "list",
    "table",
    "figure",
    "caption",
    "header",
    "footer",
    "page_number",
    "formula",
    "other",
]
```

```python
LAYOUT_PROMPT = """
Analyze this scanned document page as a document-layout detector.

Detect every meaningful BLOCK-LEVEL layout region.

Allowed semantic labels ONLY:
- text
- heading
- list
- table
- figure
- caption
- header
- footer
- page_number
- formula
- other

Important rules:

1. Detect BLOCKS, not individual words and not individual text lines.
2. A normal paragraph or group of adjacent paragraphs may be one text region.
3. Headings and chapter/section titles must be separate from body text.
4. Figures, photographs, diagrams, and illustrations must be labeled "figure".
5. Do not transcribe the document.
6. Do not describe the page.
7. Return all coordinates in a normalized 0-1000 coordinate system.
8. Bounding-box format is:
   [x1, y1, x2, y2]
   where:
   - x1 = left
   - y1 = top
   - x2 = right
   - y2 = bottom
9. Follow natural reading order from beginning to end.
10. Output ONLY a JSON array. No markdown fences and no explanation.

Exact output schema:

[
  {
    "label": "heading",
    "bbox_2d": [x1, y1, x2, y2],
    "reading_order": 0
  },
  {
    "label": "text",
    "bbox_2d": [x1, y1, x2, y2],
    "reading_order": 1
  }
]
"""
```

For Bengali pages, there is deliberately **no instruction to read or translate Bengali**. We are testing layout perception, not transcription.

---

# Cell 5 — inference function

```python
import json
import re
from PIL import Image
```

````python
def parse_json_response(text):
    """
    Parse Qwen output robustly.

    The prompt asks for plain JSON, but generative models occasionally
    wrap it in ```json ... ```.
    """

    text = text.strip()

    # Remove optional markdown fence
    text = re.sub(
        r"^```(?:json)?\s*",
        "",
        text,
        flags=re.IGNORECASE
    )

    text = re.sub(
        r"\s*```$",
        "",
        text
    )

    # First try direct decoding
    try:
        data = json.loads(text)
        return data

    except json.JSONDecodeError:
        pass

    # Recover first apparent JSON array
    start = text.find("[")
    end = text.rfind("]")

    if start >= 0 and end > start:
        candidate = text[start:end + 1]
        return json.loads(candidate)

    raise ValueError(
        f"Could not parse JSON from model response:\n{text[:1000]}"
    )
````

```python
def qwen_bbox_to_pixels(box, width, height):
    """
    Convert Qwen's 0-1000 coordinate space to original-image pixels.
    """

    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(float, box)

    # Clip normalized coordinates
    x1 = max(0, min(1000, x1))
    x2 = max(0, min(1000, x2))
    y1 = max(0, min(1000, y1))
    y2 = max(0, min(1000, y2))

    # Ensure ordering
    x1, x2 = sorted((x1, x2))
    y1, y2 = sorted((y1, y2))

    return [
        x1 * width / 1000.0,
        y1 * height / 1000.0,
        x2 * width / 1000.0,
        y2 * height / 1000.0,
    ]
```

```python
def bbox_pixel_to_norm(box, width, height):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = box

    return [
        x1 / width,
        y1 / height,
        x2 / width,
        y2 / height,
    ]
```

Now the actual inference:

```python
def process_qwen_image(image_path, max_new_tokens=1400):

    image_path = Path(image_path)

    image = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")

    width, height = image.size

    messages = [
        {
            "role": "user",
            "content": [
                {
                    "type": "image",
                    "image": image,
                },
                {
                    "type": "text",
                    "text": LAYOUT_PROMPT,
                },
            ],
        }
    ]

    inputs = processor.apply_chat_template(
        messages,
        add_generation_prompt=True,
        tokenize=True,
        return_dict=True,
        return_tensors="pt",
    )

    # Put tensors on model device.
    inputs = inputs.to(model.device)

    with torch.inference_mode():

        generated_ids = model.generate(
            **inputs,
            max_new_tokens=max_new_tokens,

            # Deterministic decoding is important for a detector experiment.
            do_sample=False,
        )

    # Remove input tokens from output
    trimmed_ids = [
        output_ids[len(input_ids):]
        for input_ids, output_ids
        in zip(inputs.input_ids, generated_ids)
    ]

    raw_output = processor.batch_decode(
        trimmed_ids,
        skip_special_tokens=True,
        clean_up_tokenization_spaces=False,
    )[0]

    raw_regions = parse_json_response(raw_output)

    clean_regions = []

    for i, item in enumerate(raw_regions):

        label = str(
            item.get("label", "other")
        ).strip().lower()

        if label not in LAYOUT_CLASSES:
            label = "other"

        bbox_qwen = item.get("bbox_2d")

        if (
            not isinstance(bbox_qwen, list)
            or len(bbox_qwen) != 4
        ):
            continue

        try:
            bbox_px = qwen_bbox_to_pixels(
                bbox_qwen,
                width,
                height,
            )
        except Exception:
            continue

        x1, y1, x2, y2 = bbox_px

        if x2 <= x1 or y2 <= y1:
            continue

        reading_order = item.get(
            "reading_order",
            i
        )

        clean_regions.append({
            "id": len(clean_regions),
            "label": label,

            # Keep original Qwen 0-1000 bbox too.
            "bbox_qwen_1000": [
                round(float(v), 2)
                for v in bbox_qwen
            ],

            "bbox": [
                round(v, 2)
                for v in bbox_px
            ],

            "bbox_norm": [
                round(v, 6)
                for v in bbox_pixel_to_norm(
                    bbox_px,
                    width,
                    height
                )
            ],

            "reading_order": int(reading_order),
        })

    # Sort by declared reading order.
    clean_regions.sort(
        key=lambda x: x["reading_order"]
    )

    return {
        "image": image_path.name,
        "width": width,
        "height": height,
        "model": MODEL_NAME,
        "regions": clean_regions,
        "raw_model_output": raw_output,
    }
```

I intentionally retain:

```json
"raw_model_output"
```

for Qwen.

You should keep this during the experiment because generative failures are themselves useful evidence:

* malformed JSON,
* invented labels,
* missing regions,
* duplicate boxes,
* strange reading order.

Later you can remove it from your production format.

---

# D. Put both outputs into exactly the same schema

Your final comparison output should discard model-specific details and become:

```json
{
  "image": "page_0014.png",
  "width": 550,
  "height": 1024,
  "model": "docling",
  "regions": [
    {
      "id": 0,
      "label": "heading",
      "bbox": [135.1, 180.4, 415.0, 245.8],
      "bbox_norm": [
        0.2456,
        0.1762,
        0.7545,
        0.2400
      ],
      "reading_order": 0
    }
  ]
}
```

versus:

```json
{
  "image": "page_0014.png",
  "width": 550,
  "height": 1024,
  "model": "Qwen/Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct",
  "regions": [...]
}
```

That gives you:

[
\text{same images}
+
\text{same labels}
+
\text{same bbox convention}
]

which is what you need for a defensible comparison.

---

## E. One modification I strongly recommend for KrishiPath

For the **first experiment**, I would reduce the semantic vocabulary further.

Use:

```python
LAYOUT_CLASSES = [
    "text",
    "heading",
    "list",
    "table",
    "figure",
    "caption",
    "header",
    "footer",
    "other",
]
```

Drop `formula` and `page_number` initially unless you actually care about them.

Why? You are primarily trying to answer:

[
\boxed{
\text{Can the model correctly partition content that should and should not go to OCR?}
}
]

For your book, the important distinction is approximately:

```text
heading
text
list
caption
        ─────► OCR

figure
        ─────► skip

table
        ─────► special handling / OCR
```

The book pages we've already examined are predominantly single-column Bengali text with headings and numbered material, rather than highly heterogeneous scientific-document layouts. 

---

# F. What pages to test

Don't choose 30 random pages.

Use a stratified pilot of roughly **25 pages**:

| Page type                            | Approx. pages |
| ------------------------------------ | ------------: |
| Ordinary body prose                  |             6 |
| Chapter opening / prominent headings |             4 |
| Numbered/list-heavy                  |             4 |
| Figure-containing                    |             4 |
| Contents/front matter                |             3 |
| Degraded/bleed-through               |             2 |
| Unusual/edge cases                   |             2 |

Then run the **same 25 images** through:

```text
Surya
Docling
Qwen3-VL-4B
```

Do not change preprocessing between models initially.

---

# G. What to record besides boxes

For every run, record:

```json
{
  "runtime_seconds": 1.87,
  "num_regions": 6,
  "parse_success": true
}
```

Qwen especially should have:

```json
{
  "json_parse_success": true
}
```

because malformed structured output is a legitimate drawback of using a generative model as a detector.

You can add runtime trivially:

```python
import time

start = time.perf_counter()

result = process_qwen_image(image_path)

result["runtime_seconds"] = round(
    time.perf_counter() - start,
    4
)
```

Use the same thing for Docling.

---

# H. What I expect this experiment to reveal

The comparison is more interesting than simply asking which draws prettier boxes.

You are essentially comparing two paradigms:

[
\textbf{Docling: }
I
\xrightarrow{\text{dedicated detector}}
(B,C)
]

against

[
\textbf{Qwen3-VL: }
I
\xrightarrow{\text{VLM}}
\text{tokens describing }(B,C)
]

Docling's current stack is explicitly designed for layout analysis, and modern Docling layout models use dedicated detector architectures trained over large heterogeneous document collections. ([arXiv][7]) Docling's underlying document representation directly retains semantic labels and bounding-box provenance. ([Docling Project][3])

Qwen3-VL, conversely, explicitly provides 2-D grounding capability, but it remains autoregressive structured generation rather than a conventional detector. ([GitHub][2])

For your project, I would therefore evaluate four things:

[
\boxed{
\text{layout accuracy}
}
]

[
\boxed{
\text{semantic-class accuracy}
}
]

[
\boxed{
\text{runtime/page}
}
]

[
\boxed{
\text{downstream OCR quality}
}
]

The last one matters most. A box does not need to match a human annotation perfectly if it produces a clean, complete crop that Surya OCR transcribes correctly.

Your course specification explicitly separates **layout analysis** from **OCR**, and `vision/layout.py` is expected to detect semantic regions before `vision/ocr.py` performs recognition.  

So once all three outputs are available, the strongest experiment will be:

[
\text{Surya-layout}\rightarrow\text{same OCR}
]

[
\text{Docling-layout}\rightarrow\text{same OCR}
]

[
\text{Qwen-layout}\rightarrow\text{same OCR}
]

with OCR held constant. That will tell you whether the extra complexity of Qwen actually buys anything.

[1]: https://docling-project.github.io/docling/reference/document_converter/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Document converter - Docling"
[2]: https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen3-VL/blob/main/cookbooks/2d_grounding.ipynb?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Qwen3-VL/cookbooks/2d_grounding.ipynb at main"
[3]: https://docling-project.github.io/docling/reference/docling_document/?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Docling Document"
[4]: https://github.com/docling-project/docling/blob/main/docs/examples/post_process_ocr_with_vlm.py?utm_source=chatgpt.com "docling/docs/examples/post_process_ocr_with_vlm.py at ..."
[5]: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Qwen/Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct"
[6]: https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen3-VL/issues/1623?utm_source=chatgpt.com "qwen3-vl系列的模型对grouding的坐标进行了1000倍的缩放"
[7]: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11720?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Advanced Layout Analysis Models for Docling"

walk me through this import error relating to pillow
AI: This error is almost certainly a **broken/mixed Pillow installation inside the Kaggle kernel**, not a problem with your import statement.

Your line is valid:

```python
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
```

The failure occurs **inside Pillow itself**:

```text
PIL/ImageText.py
    from ._typing import _Ink

ImportError:
cannot import name '_Ink' from 'PIL._typing'
```

In a consistent Pillow installation, `ImageText.py` and `_typing.py` come from the **same Pillow release**, and the current Pillow source does define `_Ink` in `PIL._typing`. ([Pillow (PIL Fork)][1])

## What likely happened

Your earlier Kaggle install command included something like:

```bash
pip install -q -U docling pillow tqdm matplotlib pandas
```

Kaggle already ships Pillow. `pip` then upgraded/replaced parts of that installed package **while the Python kernel was still running**.

You can end up with something approximately like:

```text
before pip install
Pillow version A already imported / cached
        ↓
pip installs Pillow version B
        ↓
some files on disk = B
some Python modules already loaded = A
        ↓
ImageText.py expects _Ink
but loaded/installed _typing.py does not match
        ↓
ImportError
```

Pillow's own code checks for this general class of mismatch between its Python package and compiled/runtime components. ([Pillow (PIL Fork)][2])

So **don't modify the Docling code yet**. Fix Pillow first.

---

despite the rest of the installation expecting it.
