# Why `pip install -U pillow` can cause this on Kaggle

Notebook environments have an important distinction:

```text
Python process memory
          ≠
packages currently on disk
```

Suppose Kaggle starts with:

```text
Pillow 11.x
```

and some dependency imports:

```python
PIL._typing
```

Then you run:

```bash
pip install -U Pillow
```

and get Pillow 12.3.

Your disk now contains:

```text
Pillow 12.3
```

but the running Python interpreter can still have pieces of:

```text
Pillow 11.x
```

loaded.

Then a later import loads:

```text
Pillow-12.3/ImageText.py
```

while referring to an already-loaded:

```text
Pillow-11.x/PIL/_typing.py
```

and you get exactly this kind of internal missing-symbol error.

---

## Why `Image` may work while `ImageDraw` fails

Your traceback gives another useful clue.

The chain is:

```text
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
                 │
                 └─ ImageDraw.py
                       │
                       └─ imports ImageText
                               │
                               └─ imports _Ink
```

So:

```python
from PIL import Image
```

might succeed.

But:

```python
from PIL import ImageDraw
```

loads additional Pillow modules, eventually reaching:

```python
PIL.ImageText
```

which expects:

```python
PIL._typing._Ink
```

That's where the inconsistent installation becomes visible.

You can demonstrate it with:

```python
from PIL import Image
print("Image OK")
```

then:

```python
from PIL import ImageDraw
print("ImageDraw OK")
```

The second one is likely the failing import.

---

# I would slightly change the Kaggle installation sequence

Instead of:

```bash
!pip install -q -U docling pillow tqdm matplotlib pandas
```

I recommend **not explicitly upgrading Pillow unless Docling requires it**:

```bash
!pip install -q -U docling tqdm matplotlib pandas
```

Then restart the session and inspect:

```python
import PIL
print(PIL.__version__)
```

`pip` will resolve an appropriate Pillow dependency if Docling requires one.

Explicitly asking:

```bash
-U pillow
```

adds another moving package to the environment unnecessarily.

and:

`pip check` is particularly useful because Docling may have pulled dependencies that produced a version conflict.

You want something like:

```text

```

# One more thing: don't install `PIL`

There are two names that often cause confusion:

```text
PIL       ← import namespace
Pillow    ← package you install
```

Correct:

```bash
pip install Pillow
```

and then:

```python
from PIL import Image
```

Do **not** run:

```bash
pip install PIL
```

Pillow is the actively maintained fork/package. PyPI currently lists Pillow 12.3.0 as the stable release. ([PyPI][3])

ME: okay, then what about paddleOCR?
AI: PaddleOCR is much easier than Surya 2 to run on Kaggle: it uses PaddlePaddle/CUDA directly, not a separate vLLM server or Docker backend. Your dual T4 setup is more than adequate for a normal PaddleOCR inference trial.

But for **your Bangla corpus**, I would not choose it as the primary OCR model.

Current official PaddleOCR language tables do **not** list Bengali/Bangla (`bn`) in the supported PP-OCRv3 or PP-OCRv5 recognition languages. The project also has a recent request for Bengali support, which indicates it is not an established official option. [PaddleOCR supported-language table](https://github.com/PaddlePaddle/PaddleOCR/blob/main/docs/version3.x/pipeline_usage/PP-StructureV3.en.md), [Bengali support discussion](https://github.com/PaddlePaddle/PaddleOCR/discussions/17751)

So this is not safe:

```python
PaddleOCR(lang="bn")
```

It may raise an unsupported-language error, choose an unsuitable model, or perform poorly on Bangla conjuncts and matras.

A sensible role for PaddleOCR would be:

- **layout/structure experiment:** possibly useful for detecting text regions, tables, and reading order;
- **not your primary Bangla recognizer** unless you locate and validate a maintained Bengali-specific Paddle checkpoint;
- **not worth fine-tuning from scratch** for A2—the labelled data requirement would be far beyond your small held-out transcription set.

Recommended OCR comparison order:

1. **Tesseract with Bengali language data** — lightweight baseline.
2. **EasyOCR with `['bn']`** — easy Kaggle candidate for Bangla.
3. **Surya 2** — high-potential experimental candidate, but vLLM setup is risky.
4. A credible Hugging Face multilingual/Bangla OCR checkpoint — if you find one and can reproduce it.

Use PaddleOCR only as a quick baseline/layout experiment, record the result honestly, and choose the actual OCR model based on CER/WER against your validation transcriptions.
ME: I have already tried out EasyOCR and tesseract for Bengali, my teammate successfully used surya ocr locally using his 4060Ti, how much time would it roughly take per page on his hardware for surya ocr?
AI: Assuming the successful local setup is already warm and stable:

- **RTX 4060 Ti 16 GB:** budget roughly **20–60 seconds per dense page** for Surya 2 full-page OCR.
- **RTX 4060 Ti 8 GB:** expect **slower/more fragile runs**—often **45–120 seconds per page**, lower concurrency, or possible VRAM errors on long/complex pages.

For all 403 pages, that is roughly:

```text
20 s/page  → ~2.25 hours
60 s/page  → ~6.7 hours
120 s/page → ~13.4 hours
```

The first run is separate: downloading/loading models and starting the backend may take several minutes.

This is only a planning estimate. Surya’s published benchmark is on a much faster 32 GB RTX 5090, where it reports high throughput only under very high concurrency; request latency is still around 19–43 seconds depending on percentile. You should not linearly scale that result to a 4060 Ti. [Surya performance benchmark](https://github.com/datalab-to/surya/blob/master/README.md)

Best approach: benchmark 20 representative pages—normal text, faded pages, dense pages, tables/headings—after the model is warm:

```text
average seconds/page = elapsed processing time ÷ 20
estimated total = average seconds/page × 403
```

Use a lower image resolution if needed; Surya recommends avoiding extremely high-resolution inputs and suggests a maximum width around 2048 px for efficiency. [Surya troubleshooting guidance](https://github.com/datalab-to/surya/blob/master/README.md)

AI: I reviewed the reference notebook and created a Kaggle-ready companion: [paddle_layout_ocr_kaggle.ipynb](C:/Users/HP/Desktop/4-1/CSE-429-Deep-Learning/doc-agent-template/doc-agent-starter/dump/paddle_layout_ocr_kaggle.ipynb).

It is JSON-valid and its Python cells compile. It starts with three pages, saves independent `layout.jsonl` and `ocr_full_page.jsonl` audit outputs, times both stages, visualizes boxes, and reports layout coverage versus full-page OCR line counts.

## What the reference notebook does

- Installs the **CPU** PaddlePaddle wheel, so its reported device is `cpu`; it does not use Kaggle’s T4 GPUs.
- Loads `PP-DocLayout-S`, a semantic layout detector—not the OCR pipeline.
- Finds five arbitrary images under all of `/kaggle/input`, then selects `image_files[4]`.
- Runs layout prediction on `page-0232.jpg`.
- Its result contains two `text` regions:

  - `[136, 86, 771, 243]`, score `0.745`
  - `[144, 1029, 769, 1094]`, score `0.587`

Those are large semantic regions, not per-line OCR boxes. The overlay and `save_to_img()` cells only visualize/save that layout output.

Crucially, the notebook never creates `PaddleOCR(...)` or calls an OCR recognizer. So it detects layout only; it produces no transcription.

The final `DocumentLayoutDetector` wrapper adds little value and has a fragile non-dictionary branch: it would `extend()` a string character by character if `.json` were returned as a string.

## Will text outside layout boxes disappear from OCR?

Only if you explicitly crop to the layout boxes and OCR only those crops.

The reference notebook does not run OCR, so no text is being omitted yet. In the new notebook, OCR always receives the original page image. PaddleOCR therefore runs its own text detection/recognition across the whole page, independently of `PP-DocLayout-S`; missed layout regions remain eligible for OCR.

This distinction is important because the sample’s two layout boxes visibly cover only limited parts of the page. Treat those boxes as structure/audit evidence, not as the complete OCR input. PaddleOCR’s layout module identifies semantic regions and returns labels, confidence, and `[xmin, ymin, xmax, ymax]` coordinates. [Official layout-detection docs](https://paddlepaddle.github.io/PaddleOCR/main/en/version3.x/module_usage/layout_detection.html)

## Important Bangla limitation

Conventional PaddleOCR 3.2 does not list Bengali/Bangla as a supported recognition language. The notebook makes that explicit and uses `lang="en"` only as a diagnostic baseline—useful for layout and Latin terms, but not a credible final Bangla OCR system. The official supported-language list does not include `bn`. [PaddleOCR OCR pipeline documentation](https://paddlepaddle.github.io/PaddleOCR/main/en/version3.x/pipeline_usage/OCR.html)

Do not use its raw output to populate human ground-truth labels.

## Expected Kaggle runtime

After the initial package/model downloads:

| Setup | Estimated warm runtime/page |
|---|---:|
| T4 GPU, layout + conventional PaddleOCR | ~2–6 seconds |
| CPU, like the reference notebook | ~20–60 seconds |
| First run | add roughly 3–10 minutes for installation/model downloads |

For 57 held-out pages on GPU, expect roughly **2–6 minutes of inference** after setup; for all 403 pages, roughly **15–40 minutes**. Actual timing is written to `summary.csv` by the notebook, which is more trustworthy than estimates for your scans.

AI: Start with a conservative, reversible preprocessing stage: **auto-orient → deskew → detect/crop page boundary → mild background normalization → save a derived copy**. Do not start with aggressive binarization or sharpening; both can erase Bengali vowel marks and fine printed strokes.

`src/doc_agent/ingest/preprocess.py` is currently only a stub, so this is the correct next implementation target.

## 1. Add explicit preprocessing settings

In [config.yaml](C:\Users\HP\Desktop\4-1\CSE-429-Deep-Learning\doc-agent-template\doc-agent-starter\configs\config.yaml), add a `data` section and this `preprocess` section:

```yaml
data:
  raw_dir: data/raw/krishipath

preprocess:
  enabled: true
  output_dir: data/interim/preprocessed

  deskew: true
  max_skew_degrees: 3.0

  crop_page: true
  border_margin_px: 12
  min_page_area_ratio: 0.55

  denoise: true
  normalize_background: true

  # Keep off initially; tune only after visual/OCR comparison.
  binarize: false
  binarize_method: adaptive
```

Rationale:

- `output_dir`: raw scans remain untouched; preprocessing writes a reproducible derived corpus.
- `max_skew_degrees: 3.0`: correct slight scanner rotation but avoids rotating genuinely tilted/irregular pages too aggressively.
- `crop_page`: solve neighbouring-page text and blurred borders through page-boundary detection, not a hard-coded crop that may cut Bengali text.
- `border_margin_px`: removes remaining dark/blurred boundary after the detected page crop.
- `binarize: false`: yellow paper alone is not enough reason to turn scans black-and-white. Preserve grayscale/RGB until you compare OCR quality.

## 2. Add the needed image dependencies

The project currently does not declare image-processing libraries. Add these to `pyproject.toml`:

```toml
"Pillow>=10.2,<12.0",
"numpy>=1.26,<3.0",
"opencv-python-headless>=4.9,<5.0",
```

Then update the lockfile:

```powershell
uv lock
uv sync --extra dev
```

Use `opencv-python-headless`, not `opencv-python`: the pipeline has no GUI requirement, and the headless package is more suitable for CI/Kaggle.

## 3. Define what `run()` must preserve

The `Page` contract is fixed:

```python
class Page(BaseModel):
    id: str
    image_path: str
    doc_id: str
```

Your preprocessing function must:

1. Read `page.image_path`.
2. Create a processed image under `data/interim/preprocessed/`.
3. Return a new `Page` with the same `id` and `doc_id`.
4. Replace only `image_path` with the processed file path.

That lets layout and OCR transparently work on cleaned images while all citations still refer to the original stable page IDs.

## 4. Implement one page-processing helper first

Inside [preprocess.py](C:\Users\HP\Desktop\4-1\CSE-429-Deep-Learning\doc-agent-template\doc-agent-starter\src\doc_agent\ingest\preprocess.py), first create a private helper such as:

```python
def _preprocess_image(image: np.ndarray, settings: dict) -> np.ndarray:
    ...
```

Its ordered operations should be:

```text
load image
  → apply EXIF orientation
  → grayscale working copy
  → estimate/correct skew
  → detect page contour and crop it
  → remove a small residual border
  → mild denoise
  → background normalization
  → optional binarization
  → write output
```

This order matters. If you denoise or threshold before finding the page edge, the page boundary becomes less reliable.

## 5. Deskew safely

Use the text-containing pixels to estimate a rotation angle, then rotate only when it is reasonable.

Suggested approach:

1. Convert to grayscale.
2. Use Otsu thresholding to obtain dark ink.
3. Find the minimum-area rectangle around foreground pixels.
4. Convert its reported angle into a normal `[-45°, 45°]` rotation.
5. If `abs(angle) <= max_skew_degrees`, rotate by `-angle`.
6. Otherwise leave the page unchanged and record the angle in logs/manifest.

Important details:

- Use white border fill during rotation, otherwise black triangles can confuse OCR.
- Do not deskew cropped text regions individually; deskew the full page once.
- Store the estimated angle for auditability.

## 6. Crop the neighbouring page and blurred edges

Avoid fixed percentages such as “remove 5% from each side.” Pages may have narrow margins, and fixed crops can cut headings or Bengali characters.

Instead:

1. Convert image to grayscale.
2. Blur slightly, then threshold to separate paper from scanner/background.
3. Find external contours.
4. Select the largest contour whose area is at least `min_page_area_ratio` of the image.
5. Crop to that contour’s bounding rectangle.
6. Trim the additional `border_margin_px` from all four sides, while ensuring the result remains valid.

Fallback safely: if no plausible page contour is found, retain the whole deskewed image rather than failing or applying a guessed crop.

This directly addresses both issues:

- Text from a facing/adjacent page is outside the detected physical-page contour.
- Blurred, dark, or uneven scanner edges are removed before OCR.

For your book, inspect pages near the binding especially. If the gutter shadow remains inside the page boundary, crop only a narrow inner strip after testing; never remove it globally without checking pages from both left and right sides.

## 7. Normalize yellow paper carefully

For the first baseline, use mild illumination normalization rather than binarization:

```text
grayscale
  → estimate background with a large Gaussian blur
  → divide the grayscale image by that background estimate
  → rescale to 0–255
```

This makes yellow/brown paper more uniform while retaining light Bengali marks. Save the final result as RGB if Surya expects normal colour images:

```python
processed_rgb = cv2.cvtColor(processed_gray, cv2.COLOR_GRAY2RGB)
```

A light median blur or `fastNlMeansDenoising` can reduce speckles, but use mild parameters. Strong denoising often removes:

- Bengali diacritics,
- narrow punctuation,
- small printed conjuncts,
- faint glossary entries.

## 8. Treat binarization as an experiment, not the default

Later, test these variants on 8–12 representative pages:

| Variant | Use |
|---|---|
| Original cropped/deskewed scan | Control baseline |
| Crop + deskew only | Likely safest candidate |
| Crop + deskew + background normalization | Best candidate for yellow pages |
| Adaptive binarization | Experimental; may help contrast but risk character loss |

Choose the version with the best held-out OCR error rate and visual readability, not the whitest-looking image. Your held-out labels must only be used for evaluation—not to tune preprocessing on the test pages. Use validation chapters for choosing settings.

## 9. Preserve audit evidence

For each generated image, write a record to:

```text
data/interim/preprocessed/preprocess_manifest.jsonl
```

Each line should contain at least:

```json
{
  "page_id": "page-0001",
  "source_path": "data/raw/krishipath/page-0001.jpg",
  "output_path": "data/interim/preprocessed/page-0001.png",
  "deskew_angle_degrees": -0.8,
  "page_crop_bbox": [24, 18, 1462, 2114],
  "binarized": false
}
```

This is valuable for your **auditable** NFR: you can trace exactly which raw image generated each OCR input and what transformations were applied.

ME: let's work on validate.py, walk me step by step through the implementation
AI: Implement `validate.py` as a **fail-fast integrity check for loaded `Page` objects**. It should validate what the fixed signature can actually know, and not pretend it can validate OCR word counts or split leakage.

The fixed function is:

```python
def validate(pages: list[Page]) -> None:
```

It receives only `Page` objects—no configuration, transcripts, or split labels.

## Step 1: Define what this function can validate

It should enforce:

- at least 300 loaded pages;
- every item is a `Page`;
- non-empty, unique page IDs;
- non-empty document IDs;
- every `image_path` exists and is a real file;
- supported image extension;
- no duplicate source image path.

For your chapter-only corpus, successful validation should report **378 pages**.

Do not enforce exactly 378 inside this generic function. The loader has an override to intentionally include all 403 pages, and the assignment minimum is 300.

## Step 2: Recognize the two limits of this contract

Do not implement these inside `validate(pages)`:

- **Word-count validation:** pages are images; text does not exist until OCR. The 116,539-word corpus claim belongs in provenance/EDA and later OCR evaluation.
- **Train/validation/test leakage checks:** `Page` has no `split` or `chapter` field. Also, the KB deliberately contains pages from all chapters; held-out pages are reserved against OCR tuning, not excluded from the final knowledge base.

You can document these limitations in the function docstring.

## Step 3: Use `Path` and explicit error messages

At the top of [validate.py](C:\Users\HP\Desktop\4-1\CSE-429-Deep-Learning\doc-agent-template\doc-agent-starter\src\doc_agent\data\validate.py), import:

```python
from pathlib import Path

from ..contracts import Page
```

Avoid the wildcard contract import while editing this file.

Then, inside `validate()`, begin with:

```python
minimum_pages = 300

if len(pages) < minimum_pages:
    raise ValueError(
        f"Corpus has {len(pages)} pages; at least {minimum_pages} are required."
    )
```

## Step 4: Validate each page and track duplicates

Create two sets:

```python
seen_ids: set[str] = set()
seen_paths: set[Path] = set()
```

Then loop with page position for useful errors:

```python
for position, page in enumerate(pages, start=1):
```

Check type first:

```python
if not isinstance(page, Page):
    raise TypeError(
        f"Page {position} must be a Page instance, got {type(page).__name__}."
    )
```

Check identity fields:

```python
if not page.id.strip():
    raise ValueError(f"Page {position} has an empty page ID.")

if page.id in seen_ids:
    raise ValueError(f"Duplicate page ID: {page.id!r}.")
seen_ids.add(page.id)

if not page.doc_id.strip():
    raise ValueError(f"Page {page.id!r} has an empty document ID.")
```

Check the image path:

```python
image_path = Path(page.image_path)

if not image_path.is_file():
    raise FileNotFoundError(
        f"Page {page.id!r} points to a missing image: {image_path}"
    )

if image_path.suffix.lower() not in {
    ".bmp", ".jpeg", ".jpg", ".png", ".tif", ".tiff", ".webp"
}:
    raise ValueError(
        f"Page {page.id!r} has unsupported image type: {image_path.suffix!r}."
    )
```

Then detect duplicate files through different paths:

```python
resolved_path = image_path.resolve()

if resolved_path in seen_paths:
    raise ValueError(
        f"Duplicate image path in corpus: {resolved_path}"
    )
seen_paths.add(resolved_path)
```

## Step 5: Make the final function silent on success

`validate()` should return `None` if valid. Do not print results; callers can log page counts separately.

Its final structure should be conceptually:

```python
def validate(pages: list[Page]) -> None:
    """Validate loaded image-page integrity before preprocessing.

    This function validates page-level properties only. OCR word-count and
    train/validation/test leakage checks require information not present in
    the fixed Page contract.
    """
    # minimum corpus size
    # initialize duplicate trackers
    # validate every Page
    # return None implicitly
```

## Step 6: Test it before wiring it into the pipeline

In `tests/test_data.py`, add small temporary-image tests for:

1. valid 300-page corpus passes;
2. 299-page corpus raises `ValueError`;
3. duplicate page ID raises;
4. missing image raises `FileNotFoundError`;
5. duplicate image path raises.

Use tiny generated PNG files in pytest’s `tmp_path`; never depend on your 378-image real corpus in unit tests.

## Step 7: Do not wire it into `pipeline.py` yet

The pipeline order is explicitly fixed. First implement and test `validate()` in isolation. After it passes, we can decide the least invasive way to invoke it without violating the fixed pipeline rules.

The next implementation action is therefore: replace the `NotImplementedError` in `validate.py` with the checks above, then write its five unit tests.

ME: can I expect similar ocr performance from easyOCR as I am getting from surya?
AI: Probably not. For your dense Bangla textbook scans, I would expect EasyOCR to be a weaker baseline than Surya overall.

EasyOCR does support Bengali plus English (`['bn', 'en']`), so it can read the script and mixed Latin terms. But its Bengali recognizer is an older generation-1 model, and it does not provide Surya’s document-specific layout classes and reading-order model. [EasyOCR Bengali model configuration](https://github.com/JaidedAI/EasyOCR/blob/master/easyocr/config.py)

Surya is designed around document OCR: layout detection, reading order, full-page/block OCR, tables, and multi-column documents are integrated. That matters directly for your glossary pages, chapter lists, and two-/three-column Bangla–English layouts. [Surya’s document OCR and layout capabilities](https://github.com/datalab-to/surya)

Use EasyOCR only as:

- a CPU-friendlier fallback/baseline;
- a comparison in your A2 notebook;
- a possible emergency autograder-safe path.

Do not assume its character error rate or page reconstruction will match Surya. Measure both on the same **validation** pages, then keep the test labels untouched for final reporting.
ME: can we use easyOCR here and should we consider yolo for the layout? Also can both of these run on the cpu?
AI: Yes, you can use **EasyOCR + a document-trained YOLO layout model** as a CPU-capable fallback pipeline.

- EasyOCR supports Bengali and English together, and its official CPU mode is `easyocr.Reader(["bn", "en"], gpu=False)`. [EasyOCR CPU mode](https://github.com/JaidedAI/EasyOCR/blob/master/README.md)
- A document-layout YOLO model such as **DocLayout-YOLO** can detect document regions on CPU; Ultralytics also explicitly supports `device="cpu"` inference. [DocLayout-YOLO model usage](https://github.com/opendatalab/DocLayout-YOLO), [Ultralytics device configuration](https://docs.ultralytics.com/usage/cfg)

But I would treat it as a **fallback/baseline**, not assume it will beat or equal Surya.

| Option | Bangla recognition | Layout / reading order | CPU suitability |
|---|---|---|---|
| Surya | Likely better for your scans | Integrated document layout/order | Technically possible, operationally difficult |
| EasyOCR alone | Usable but weaker | No document semantic layout | Yes |
| DocLayout-YOLO + EasyOCR | Usable but likely below Surya | Good region classes; you must implement reading order | Yes |

Important details:

- Do not use a generic COCO-trained YOLO model. It detects objects like people/cars, not `text`, `title`, `list`, `table`, and `figure`. Use a **document-layout-trained** checkpoint such as DocLayout-YOLO.
- YOLO only gives regions/classes. You must crop applicable text regions, run EasyOCR per crop, then implement deterministic reading order.
- On your three-column glossary pages, reading order remains the difficult part. A layout detector can help segment columns, but it does not automatically guarantee correct Bangla–English ordering.
- Both will be slow on CPU for 378 pages, especially EasyOCR. They are appropriate for a smoke test or autograder fallback, not necessarily your production full-corpus OCR run.
- Neither becomes autograder-safe merely by supporting CPU: the package and pretrained weights must be installed/cached, which is often unavailable in a network-restricted grader.

My recommendation: use Surya for the quality-focused A2 corpus build on GPU, and implement **DocLayout-YOLO + EasyOCR CPU mode** only if you need a genuine no-GPU fallback. Keep imports lazy and test the pipeline with mocks so CI never downloads models.

ME: compare this to [page-0036.png](dump/yolo-layout/page-0036.png) , why does the preprocessed version appear worse, it is more skewed adn the crop is also bad, also the edges are still blurred
AI: You are right to question it. The attached result looks worse than [page-0036.png](C:\Users\HP\Desktop\4-1\CSE-429-Deep-Learning\doc-agent-template\doc-agent-starter\dump\yolo-layout\page-0036.png), especially around the left gutter.

First, verify that Kaggle actually ran the updated helper with `--preprocess`. The attached page still has its original yellow paper tone, whereas the current `normalize_background` step should produce a much whiter image. In `summary.json`, confirm this is not `null`:

```json
"preprocessing": { ... }
```

and inspect:

```text
/kaggle/working/<output-dir>/preprocessed_pages/page-0036.png
```

Kaggle input datasets are immutable snapshots, so you must upload/create a new dataset version after changing the script locally.

If preprocessing did run, these are the actual weaknesses in the current approach:

- **Skew:** `_estimate_skew_degrees()` uses `minAreaRect` on all dark pixels. On a dense, unevenly distributed text page, that detects the shape/distribution of the text block—not necessarily the text baseline angle. It can therefore introduce a slight wrong rotation. The raw page is already close to straight, so automatic deskew is more harmful than helpful here.
- **Bad crop:** `_detect_page_crop()` detects the largest bright-paper region. The target page and the sliver of the adjacent page are connected by similar paper/background, so it considers them one object. The later 12 px trim is far too small to remove the gutter.
- **Blurred edges:** the pipeline does not deblur or sharpen; it only denoises. `fastNlMeansDenoising` can slightly soften already weak edge strokes. The blurred left gutter remains because it was not cropped out.

For this corpus, I would change strategy before relying on it:

1. Disable automatic deskew unless a stronger line-based method detects a meaningful, high-confidence rotation.
2. Replace automatic physical-page cropping with a corpus-specific gutter crop—detect the dark book seam and remove everything left of it, or use a validated fixed left margin.
3. Keep background normalization, but make denoising optional/off during comparison.
4. Do not try to “sharpen” the blurred neighboring page; crop or mask it out instead.

The clear older `yolo-layout` output likely came from stronger whitening/cropping than this conservative repository preprocessor. The next useful test is raw vs. preprocess with **deskew off**, **denoise off**, and a **larger left-gutter crop** on the same 10 pages.

AI: Updated [compare_easyocr_doclayout_yolo_kaggle.py](C:\Users\HP\Desktop\4-1\CSE-429-Deep-Learning\doc-agent-template\doc-agent-starter\dump\compare_easyocr_doclayout_yolo_kaggle.py).

With `--preprocess`, it now performs only:

```text
EXIF correction → fixed 80 px left-gutter trim → high-confidence small-angle deskew
```

The deskew:

- detects long near-horizontal text lines with Hough lines;
- rotates only if at least six lines agree;
- ignores estimates below `0.15°`;
- never accepts an estimate beyond `2°`.

So it avoids the old whole-page-box skew estimator that could rotate pages incorrectly. No auto-crop, denoising, whitening, or binarization remains.

Use:

```bash
--preprocess
```

To test without rotation:

```bash
--preprocess --no-deskew
```

The 80 px trim removes the neighboring-page gutter on the 825 px-wide scans while preserving more margin than the prior 90 px trim. All checks pass.
ME: do the vision files now use both the gpu nd cpu as a fallback support?
AI: Yes. Both vision files support device fallback.

- [layout.py](C:\Users\HP\Desktop\4-1\CSE-429-Deep-Learning\doc-agent-template\doc-agent-starter\src\doc_agent\vision\layout.py)
  - `device: auto` selects `cuda:0` when `torch.cuda.is_available()` is true.
  - Otherwise it uses CPU.
  - If configuration explicitly requests CUDA but none is available, it logs a warning and uses CPU.

- [ocr.py](C:\Users\HP\Desktop\4-1\CSE-429-Deep-Learning\doc-agent-template\doc-agent-starter\src\doc_agent\vision\ocr.py)
  - `device: auto` enables EasyOCR GPU mode only when CUDA is available.
  - Otherwise it initializes EasyOCR with `gpu=False`.
  - An unavailable explicit CUDA request also falls back to CPU.

Your [config.yaml](C:\Users\HP\Desktop\4-1\CSE-429-Deep-Learning\doc-agent-template\doc-agent-starter\configs\config.yaml) sets both to `auto`.

One distinction: this is availability fallback at startup. If CUDA is available but later fails during inference due to an out-of-memory or driver error, the code currently reports that error; it does not automatically retry that individual page on CPU.
